I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) The Ambassador's and the Counselor's
conversations with Japanese officials.
On February 6 Mr. Teresaki, Director of the American
Bureau of the Foreign Office, called upon the Ambassador
at the behest of the Foreign Minister, who desired that
Mr. Terasaki inform Mr. Grew of his observations during
his recent visit to China. Mr. Terasaki stated that he
had returned convinced of the desire of the Japanese mili-
tary and other authorities in China to settle the various
cases affecting Anerican rights and interests which had
been the cause of complaint by the United States Government.
He ascribed the present difficulties in this respect di-
rectly to Japanese military recuirement due to the exist-
once of hostilities in China. When Mr. Terasaki had fin-
ished his talk, the Ambassador pointed out to him that
despite these assurances as to the intentions of the Japa-
nese authorities such occurrences as bombings of American
missions were still continuing in China and from the con-
crete evidence which camc to the Embassy ther could not
be the slightest doubt that many of the attacks on the
missions were intentional. Mr. Grow noted that these fla-
grant attacks rendured it impossible for American authori-
ties to believe that the Japanuse authoritics in China were
endeavoring to avoid new incid nts.
In reply to Mr. Terasaki's explanition of the curroncy
quostion in China, the Ambassador obscrved thnt the matter
was only one phase of a patent intention to drive American
business nd other interests completcly out of China nd

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
that other means, such as monopolies, traffic rostrictions,
to name only a few, introduced by thc Japanese had alroady
wrecked American business interests built up in China through
generations. Mr. Grew s id that it was all very well to
ascribe thesc various mcasures to the existence of hostili-
ties, but the concrete ovidence available left little coubt
of the intention of th Japancse authorities to establish
themselvos permanently in Chinc and to eliminate or to
divert into Japanose hands the interests of other countries.*
By a letter dated February 7 the Anbassador transmitted
to the Foreign Minister summaries of cditorinl comment in
the American press, up to Fubruary 1, concerning Japanesc
mediation in the dispute between Fronch Indochinn and Thei-
land. In his letter Mr. Grew informed Mr. Matsuoka that
the cditorial comment, whether well founded or not, was in
no way "inspired" nd that it might bo taken s accurately
reflocting the trend of public opinion in tho Unitud St tes.
He stated that it was the carnest hope of the American
Government that the adverse opinions expressed in thes
editorials would prove not to have boen well founded in
fact and that this would be revealed by the terms of any
settlement in the controversy bctween Thailand and Indochina
th : might result from Japan's medition.**
During the course of his conversation with the Foreign
Minister on Fobruary 26, the Ambassador delivored to the Min-
ister excerpts of further cditorial comment in the American
press on Far Eastern affnirs, Admiral Nomura's mission to
Washington, and Amorican-Japencse rulations. The Minister

The Ambarsedo. as ir past months, lost no opportunity
during February to bin c tho attention of and emphasize
to Japanese in otfci  civilian walrs of life the
serious implications of the prosent course of Japanese
foreign policy, as revealed by official statenents of the
Government and by the utterances of important personages
in the country.
Following his return from leave of absence in the United
States, the Counselor of the Embassy on February 14 faid a
courtesy call on the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs. Mr.
Ohashi asked Mr. Dooman for an account of the trends of re-
cent American opinion, and Mr. Dooman then presented a pic-
ture of American views in which he emphasized the following
points. He stated that the American people, huving in view
the ultimate safety of the United States, were determined to
provide Great Britain with all necessary equipment, materials,
and supplies for that country to withstand the expected Ger-
man onslaucht, and that although the overwhelming majority
of the American people abhorred the thou ht or war, even the
possibility of involvement in the European war would not
deter them from implenenting their deternination to help
England. Mr. Dooman then mentioned that the Anerican Gov-
ernment and people, in line with the policy which has been
faithfully pursued during the recent years of disturbances
in the Far East, were consciously avoidin any initiative
which might lead to war with Japan. It vould, however, be
* Embassy's telegram No. 311, February 26, 8 p.m.

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
a mistake to assume that the United States, so long as as-
sistance to England in her war with Germany and Italy re-
mained the dominant American objective, would remain indif-
ferent to any threat, actual or potential, by Japan, or any
other power, to the lines of communication within the British
Empire which would imperil the continuance of thc flow of
war materials and foodstuffs to England from the British
dominions and colonies and thereby England's very existence.
He said that it must be obvious that American aid to England
would be of little avail if essential commodities from the
British Empire were withheld and that, therefore, the sua-
cess of the policy of American help to England must be
predicated upon this consideration. Following a long dis-
course by Mr. Ohashi regarding Japan's grievances against
the United States and Britain for thcir attitude respecting
Japan's problems with the continent of Asia and the Sino-
Japaneso conflict, nd a reaffirmation of Japan's intention
to honor its commitments under the tripartite alliance, Mr.
Dooman stated that despite the intentions of tho United
States not to seek a war with Japan it would be extravagant
to believe that so long as Japan remained a prtner of Ger-
many and Italy and so long is Japn was unable to solve her
troubles with China on a mutually satisfactory and equitable
basis, stabilization of rolations batween tho United States
and Japan could be hoped for. He observed in conclusion
that the world is now facing a crisis of the first magnitude
and that any effort on the part of Japan substantially to
alter the status quo in Southeastern Asia might well lead
to the most serious consequences.*
Embassy's telegram No. 230, February 14, 8 p.m.

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
On February 26 the Ambassador informed the Minister
for Foreign Affairs that he wished him to know that every-
thing Mr. Dooman had said to Mr. Ohashi had the Ambassador's
entire concurrence and approval.*
In the course of the Ambassador's conversation with the
Foreign Minister on February 26 Mr. Matsuoka spoke of recent
British activities in the Pacific area and he accused the
British Government of taking measures in the Far East which
were a direct incitement to Japan and which rendered very
difficult an improvement in the situation. Mr. Grew ob-
served to Mr. Matsuoka that it seemed extraordinary that the
Japanese should interpret and characterize obviously defen-
sive measures as measures of offense. The Ambassador re-
ferred in this connection to what he had said at the America-
Japan Society luncheon on December 19, 1940, regarding "facts
and actions" and he stated that certainly the fects and ac-
tions relating to Japan's southward advance were concrete
causes for serious anxiety not only on the part of Great
Britain but of the United States. He recounted the facts
that Japan had occupied in succession Waichow, H inan, the
Spratly Islands, and other aroas; that the Japanese military
were pouring troops into Indochina nd, according to reports,
had occupied the airport at Saigon, and that they were under-
taking navel activities in the waters off Indochina and
Thailand. He said that these steps taken in conjunction with
public utterances of many Japanese statesmen, generals and
admirals conc crning Japanese intentions towards southeastern
Asia had created a situation which could hardly be regarded
with equanimity either by the United States or by Grcat
Britain since it threatencd not only those countries' in-
terosts but their possessions.**

(b) Representations made to the Foreign
Office during the month under review.
On February 26 the Ambassador brought to the attention
of the Minister for Foreign Affairs the question of the
opening of American official mail by Japanese authorities,
and he handed to Mr. Matsuoka in evidence thereof an envelope
addressed by the Department of State to the American Consul
at Kobe which had clearly been opened and resealed before
delivery.*
On February 6 representations were made by the Embassy
to the Foreign Office with regard to the endangering of the
personnel of the American Consulate at Kunming, Yunnan Prov-
ince, China, by a Japanese aerial attack on that city on
January 30.**
The Embassy made representations to the Foreign Office
during February regarding the bombing of property of the
Standard-Vacuum Oil Company at Sukiapa, Chungking,*** and
property of the Lutheran Brethren Mission at Tangho.****
During the month of February the Embassy made repre-
sentations in regard to the following cases of Japanese in-
terference with American rights and interests in China and
Indochina:
Detention by Japanese naval authorities in the
Pearl River area of Mr. Merrill S. Ady.*****
Further regard to Japanese interference with the
movement of American-owned merchandise in French Indo-
china.******

(e) Japanese attitude during the month regard-
ing Japan's relations with the United States.
In contrast to the attitude of the Japanese press dis-
played during January, which was marked by bombastic declar-
tions and demand on the Governient for aggressive action,
particularly in relaticns to Japan's southward advance, the
Lone of the press in February was decidedly reserved and mild.
Emphasis durin the month under review was placed on an
attempt to defend the policy of southward oxpansion as non-
agressive and primarily economic in character. As has been
roted, the press, with the exceptien of occusional outbursts
narticularly in the KokUMIN, stressed the point that Japan
did not want war with the United States and that war could
nd shoulu be avoidcd. Coiment on the deiiberations in the
imerican Congress regarding the Lease-Lend Bill vos ulso
noticeably cubducd, and von contained the infurence that
the bill to aid the dufense of democracies would not neces-
arily risult in the embroilmont of the United States and
ian.
The onus for the so-called Far Eastcrn crisis was
r leed prinarily on Great Britain. The impression reccived

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.98)
-13-
from the tone of the press durine February was that the Japa-
nese Government had become perturbed by the reaction abroud to
Japan's rccont moves in connection with the southward advance.
Although there was no cvidence to support the viow that Japa-
nese policies were necessarily to be altered or modified as
a result of a stiffonins attitude on the part of the countries
affected by Japan's southvard advince, it was possible to de-
tect a note of hesitancy and caution.*

(a) Groat Britain.
1. "The Matsuoku Mcdiation Statement".
On February 18 the spokesman of the Cabinet Infornation
Bureau, Mr. Ko Ishii, issucd : statement denouncing the at-
titude of undue concern felt in ccrtain countries rugarding
the alleged tension in the Far East as being without grounds,
reiterating the penceful purpose of the Triportite Alliance,
decrying the prolongation of war, and concluding with the
statement that "Japan is fully prepared to act s mediator or
to take whatevur action is c lcul :ted to recover norral con-
ditions not only in Enst Asic but nywh e in the world".
The spokesman, in ruply to inquirius by corrospondents,
stated that this offer of Jap n's cood offices pplied to
war anywhere. He lso said that any further initiative which
Japan might take toward mediation depended on Japan's being
approached and on whether the Japanese Government considered
the situation favorable for modiation.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Diet.
With but only minor flurries over such matters as the
revision of the National Goneral Mobilization Law und the
establishment of tho Imp rial Rulo Assistance Associntion,
the proceedings of the Dict during February wore notable
for the calmness of the deliberations, the support given to
the government measures, nd the expeditious enactment of
government bills. By the ond or the month all of the
eighty-seven logislative bills submitted by the Government
had boen approved nd the mn in work of the session compluted.
It was undcrstood that tho Diet would go into recess on the
first of March.
Three subjocts to which the Diet gave its groatest at-
tention wero the Imperial Rule Assistance Association, a
rovision of th Natioral General Mobilization Law, and the
National Security Defense Bill.
Continuing the controversy
which had appeared last fall and during the early winter
throughout the country regarding the nature and functions
94
0
of tho Impcrial Rule Assistance Association, both houses
discussed the matter.
The debates finally narrowed down to
two main issues: the pclitical nature of the Association
and the bearing of the Association upon the prerogatives of
the Diet. VWith respect to the first point, the cuesticn
was argued whether the Association should possess a strong
political character or whether its functions should merely
be restricted to social, moral, and spiritual matters. In
reply to interpellations both the Prime Minister and the
Home lHinister stated that the Associat ion, while maintaining

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.98)
-2F-
a definite political character, was not a political associa-
tion but rather a "publ ic" organization and an "auxiliary
organization " which would cooperate with the Government but
would not engage in policy-making.
The second poir , which
in effect sprang from the first, regarded the question of
whether the Association was to usurp the functions and powers
of the Diet. Despite the dininution of the influence of the
Diet since the beginning of the China Incident, that body
showed itself as being roluctant to relinquish its powers to
such a vaguely conceived organ as the Association. In this
connection the Government explained that the difference be-
tween the Diet's "assistance to the Throne" and that of the
Association was that the Diet was an organ of gov rnment
established by the Japanese Constitution whose actions have
legal effect, whereas the Association aimed only to extend
cooperation and assistance to the several organs of the
Government.
It was emphasized that there was no thought
of establishing within Japan's national structure a forn of
dictatorship such as exists in some foreign countries.
The Diet cventually passed an eight million yen appro-
priation for the maintenance of the Association. Despite
this legislative sanction of
the Association, its prestige
had clearly been reduced in the country but it is still too
early to foresee what its role is to be in Japan. Views
have been put forward that it may merely be another form of
stimulating national enthusiasm for the Covernment's policies
to tide over the present difficulties of the country. On the
other hend it has been thought that the Association might
result in a more or less permanent and useful liaison organ

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.96)
between the Government and the pcople. In any ovent it soems
highly doubtful thas tie Asscsiatior will bccome the ruslous
for a totalitariar orranization of Japan.*
Inasmuch as dospatches aro to be subaitted leter uy the
Embassy concernir ti r vision of the Nat: onal Cen ral
Mobilizat ion Law  (. tional Defonse Sccurity Bill,
comment on those o inport nt matters is not ipiuded here.
Among other important lcgislative acts enacted by the
Diet were: the Building and Plunning Association Law, to
cstablish a juriuical corporation capitalized at Yen 100,000,000,
to plan and buila housus for the working clcssos: the Manual
Laborors Employment-Book Lav, preseribing employment cards
for a large category of laborers and technicians as a meas-
ure to assist in the control and rogulation of cmployment;
the Silk Industry Control Bill, to reculate production and
domostic consumption of riv silk; the revision of the For-
eign Exchange Control Lav, to control the assets of Japanese
nationals held abroad, to cquire and dispose of propurty
held by foreignurs or foroign corporations in Japan, to regu-
late methods or torns for cettloment between Japan nd for-
cign traders, na to tightun rustrictions  gainst foruign
exchange evasion; the rovision of the Provision.i Export-
Import Adjustment Law, whereby Japan's entire forcimn trado

Industry Law; the Shipping Protoction Law, to ruduce war-
tine shippin ricks and to arrange for th protection of

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The budgots passed by the Diot for the fiscal year
1941-42 amounted to the sum of Yen 12,875,000,000. This
total was made up of three accounts: the General Account,
amounting to Yen 6,863,000,000; the Temporary Var Expenditure
Account, anounting to Yen 4,880,000,000; and a Supplementary
Account, amounting to Yen 1, 132,000,000. The total budget
represented an increase of Yen 1,241,000,000 over that of
C
last year. To meet these expenditures, national bonds in
7
the amount of Yen 7,555,000,000 are to be issued during the
fiscal year. This bond issue represents an increase of
Yen 550,000,000 as compared with the bond issue authorized
1.58
for the preceding year. The Diet also approved a third
supplementary budget of Yen 1,000,000,000 for the current
fiscal year to cover expenditures for the months of January,
February, and March.
The Finance Minister stated before the Diet on February 5
that the Government would not resort to conpulsory bond sales
to the public.

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.97)
1.
RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES
(a) Treatment of foreigners ir Japan.
As Doan of the Diplomatic Corps, the Ambasnador called
on March 12 on the Minister for Foreign Affairs, an hour
or two before Mr. Matsuoka's departure for Mosccw, Berlin
and Rome, and strongly supported representations ma e by
the French Ambassador concerning a recent incident in
which the French Commercial Attaché, Count de Tascher,
was attacked by taxi drivers in Kobe reguitiny in serious
injuries to him. The Ambassador stressed. as the worst
aspect of the case, the negligent and indifferent attitude
of the local police officials toward Count de Tascher sub-
sequent to the assault. He presented to the llinister a
letter setting forth the details of the Jase and the
general considerations involved. It was pein? cut in
this letter that other recent cases of bodily attacks or
of insulting belavior on the part or Japanese nationals
against members of the Diplomatic Corps had occurreu and
it was noted that such incidents were likely to continue
and to increase as long as the Japanese press continued
to publish material anti-forein in tone and as lon; as
anti-foreign posters were permitted by the police author-
ities to appear, producirg a cenerally xenophobe attitude
on the part of Japanese public and of certain elements
of Japancse officialdom towards all foreigners regardless
of their nationality.
The Ambnssador appca led to the
Minister, not only to take effective steps to put an end to
theanti-foreign pruss and poster campaign, but also to
cause such instructions tc be issued to the police through-

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
out Japan as vould in the future insure moro considerate
and humanitarian treatment of foreignors. Mr. Matsuoka
assured Mr. Grew, in reply to these representations, that
he had already madc an effort to stop the anti-roreign
campaign and that he would urgo the Homo
Minister to take
further steps for the protcction of forcigners in which he
acknowledged that Japan's honor was involved.
On March l4th the Foreign Minister, in a statement
made at Osaka, deprecated the apparent inclination on the
part of some Japanese "to believe that it is a manifesta-
tion of patriotism to be anti-foreign and to assume a rude
attitude towards foreigners." He seid that Japan's diplo-
macy was being seriously harmed in this way and that such
acts were injurious to the honor of the Yamato race. He
called for a positive end to be put to this attitude to-
ward foreigners.
As indicative of the fact that these renarks of Mr.
Matsuoka were a direct result of the Ambassador's representa-
tions, the Embassy was infomed by the Foreign Office that
they had been published in the press by instruction of the
Foreign Minister.
On March 25 and on March 23 the Ambassador again had
occasion in two personal letters to the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs to take up the question of anti-foreign
agitation in Japan. Mr. Grew brought to the attention of
Mr. Ohashi the anpearance in Osaka of a considerable number
of posters attacking thc United States, Creat Britain and
China under the ception "Strike
the Enemies of the Imperial
Race" and he bespoke, on behalf of the Diplomatic Corps
and other foreigners in Japan, the good offices of tho

Vice Minister in having the porters removed.*
(b) Fur Soal Ccnvention.
It will be recull edt*that in a note dated catouer
23, 1940, the Japarose Government ave notice of its
abrogation of the Fur Soal Convention of 19ll. On March
18 the Embassy transmitted to the Foreign Office a formal
note of the American Government presenting its views on
the notice of the Japanese Government and offering for
consideration the proposals that (1) the take of fur seal
on Pribiloff Islands be increased, beginning with the
sealing season of 194l, in the amount of approximately
30,000 a year above the take for the year cf 1940 of
65,263; (2) that a thorough survey of the fur seal situa-
tion be made which estimatedly would govern at least
the sealing of 1941-1942 and that upon the conclusion
of this investigation there be considerad by the con-

(e) General relations and press comments.
All the Japanese vernacular papers gave proninence
to enactment of the Lease-Lend Bill and discussed its
significance. The general conclusion drawn in the edi-
torial comments was to the effect that the bill had brought
the United States near to actual participation in the
European War. The danger was seen in the possibility of
the convoy by the American fleet of ships to Britain with
ret-liatory measures being taken by Germany which might
in turn result in the outbreak of hostilities. The passage
of the bill itself was ascribed as evidencing a loss of
political influencc by isolationist groups in the United
States and as indicating united Anerican opinion to carry
out the progran of giving all aid possible not only to
Great Britain but to China. It was stated, however, that
the United States fcced tremendous difficulties in orga-
nizing war production to provide war materials to Britain,
Greece and China and particularly with rospect to China
doubt was oxpressed that nuch of these supplius would be
forth-coming for the present at lsast. The KOKUMIN,

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
1. Military Oporations.*
Military operations during the month under review in-
cluded a surprise landing by Japanese troops on the southern
coast of Kwangtung, two major clearing operations, and the
usual small-scale moppin& up campaigns in occupied territory
and along fringes of Japanese salients in the Central and
North-west area of China. In the first part of the month
Japanese forces, estimated at about two to three divisions,
made landings at Sunwui, Kuanghaichai, Yeungkong, Tinpak,
Shuitungshih, Leichou, and Pakhoishih, along the southern
coast of Kwangtung. These were followed later in the month
by further landings along the Kwangtung coast from the
vicinity of Chaoyanε to Bias Bay. On March 24 another
landing was made on the peninsula to the west of Swabue
and that city itself was also occupied.
Most of the
forces were withdrawn before the end of the month from
the southern coast of Kvangtung.
It appeared that these
operations were primariiy for tho purpose of raiding
various supply points and se zin or des roving military

(c) French Indochina - Thailand.
1. Japanese mediation in the French Indo-
China - Thailand dispute.
On February 24, it vill be recel led, the Japanese
Government presented what was announced as a 'final" plan
for the settlement by Japanese mediation of the border
dispute between French Indochina and Thailand. It will
92
be further recalled that by the end of February Thailand
had reportedly agreed to accept the Japanese plan but that
the French had declined to do so. On March 2 a "second"
plan embodying certain unimportant changes was put forward
by the Japanese Foreign Minister and it was learned that the
French also found this unacceptable. Further inconclusive
consultations were conducted during the next few days.
The situation at this period of the conference began to
appear critical. In the Japanese press a thrcatening tone
towards Indochina was adopted. Editorials were published

which made menacing comients io the effect that if French

the obstinate French attitude was in large measure the
result of British and American intrigues designed to thwart
the pacific aims of Japan. On March 5 the Japanese again
put forward the original Japanese plan with some minor
concessions to the French views in a form that was des-
cribed as a peremptory ultimatum. At the same time the
Japanese pressed for a prompt conclusion of the conference.
On March 1l the formal announcement was made that
the negotiations had been brought to a successful con-
clusion and that protocols were signed that day arranging
a "permanent settlement" of the Indochina - Thailand
dispute based on the Japanese mediation plan. By the

of Laos. It was provided that the total areas ceded would
be demilitarized, that citizens of Indochina would enjoy

guarantee the execution of the agreement. Three commissions

(a) Venezuela.
An announcement was made on March 11 by the Cabinet
Information Board that an understanding had been reached
with Venezuela whereby trade relations between the two
countries would be continued for a year on the basis of
an understanding concluded July 1940. By that understand-
ing Japan had undertaken to purchase from Venezuela as
large quantities of goods as possible and Venezuela had
agreed to refrain from imposing unreasonable restrictions
on imports from Japan.**
(e) General International Relations.
1. Visit of Foreign Minister Yosuke
Matsuoka to Europe.
***
It will be recalled/that rumors were current during
the latter part of February to the effect that the Minister
for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Yosuke Matsuoka, would shortly
make a trip to Europe. On March 1l, the official announce-
ment was issued that Mr. Matsuoka would leave the next day
to visit Germany and Italy. The announcement was made
on the same date as that of the settlement of the Indochina -
Thailand dispute and was evidently purposely so timed for
psychological reasons. According to the statement is-
sued by the Foreign Office Mr. Matsuoka was making the

Before his departure Mr. Matsuoka made several statements

Way" and on the need of personal acquaintanceship and con-

that the country should not expect him to return with any
particular "present" and that his intended frank talks
with Chancellor Hitler, Foreign Minister Von Ribbentrop and
Premier Mussolini would do more than anything else to
strengthen the Tripartite Alliance.
The Foreign Minister left Tokyo on the evening of
March 12 to proceed via Manchuria and Siberia to Moscow
and thence to Berlin. Ho arrived at Moscow on March 23,
staying there until the next day when he left for Berlin,

remained until March 30. Then he continued his journey
to Rome. It was understood that he would return to Japan

2. Southward Advance.
Largely attendant upon the occasion of the announce-
ment of the settlement of the Indochina-Thailand dispute,
the Japnese press gave a great deal of attention to the
question of Japanese southward advance policy. As has
been mentioned in the foregoing paragraphs (Vide Relations
with French Indochina - Thailand) the settlement of the
dispute was hailed as constituting a step forward in
Japan's establishment of the so-called co-prosperity sphere
in Greater East Asia. It was urged by various quarters
that as a matter of imperative necessity "Japan's unal-
terably fixed policy" with respect to the sphero should
be further and immediately implemented. In the argumenta-
tion relative to this policy the thesis of Asiatic race-
consciousness was more pronouncedly than usual turned to
and emphasis was placed upon the creation of a pan-Asiatic

the legislative program submitted by the Government.*
(b) Cabinet.
With the beginning of the month under review criticisms
began to appear of the Konoye Cabinet, indicating a state
of some unrest regarding the present political situation
in the country. The criticisms were to the effect that
the Cabinet was not strong enough to cope with the current
internal and external problems faced by Japan and that
the Cabinet needed to be strengthened to the end that
a firmer and more positive national leadership be iven
to the country. Somo dissatisfaction was evidenced over
the apparent lethargy of the Prime Minister as woll as over
the muddled situation concerning the Imperial Rule As-
sistnce Association and the economic difficulties in the
country. By the end of the month it had become apparent

that a reorganization of the Cabinet was very likely in

(c) Imperial Rule Assistance Association.
In giving its assent for the appropriation of eight
million yen for the expenses of the Imperial Rule As-
sistance Association, the Diet received, it was understood,
the assurances from the Government that the reorganiza-
tion of the Association would be undertaken upon the close
of the Diet Session. Even before the Diet Session was
formally ended discussions were begun for this reorganiza-
tion and numerous conferences were held throughout the
month by representatives of the Diet, officials of the
Association and the Government ministers. The feelings
of the Diet members concerning the reorganization were
reported to include the views that the Association should
be formed into merely an auxiliary organ of the Government
and should not have a voice in legislation and administra-
tion inasmuch as such was not admitted under the Japanese
constitution. Greater representation by Diet members in
the organization was also desired. The Government on the
other hand was reported to have advanced a plan which in
effect would give the organization greater political
character and place it under more diruct control by the
Cabinet.
Accordin: to this plan the Vice Presidont of
the Association was to be a state minister and greater liason
between the Government and the organization wcs to be
provided by means of various commissions. While the dis-
cussions were being carricd on a statement was issued by
* Embassy's telegram No. 390, March 11, 8 p.m.

a spokesman of the Army stressing the Army's continued
support of the Association. In the statement mention
was made of obstruction to the new national structure move-
ment by various sources addressed as the "status quo
maintainance groups". Although clarification was not
offered as to whom this remark referred it was apparently
made to the end of manifesting the Army's displeasure with
elements in Japanese society, possibly principally roups
in the Diet, which entertained more liberal and less
authoritarian views. On March 7 it was announced that
Count Arima together with forty other officials of the
Association resigned their positions and that the Justice
Minister, Lieutenant General Yanagawa, was expected to
assume the vice presidency of the Association.*
As the
month closed it was expected that complete reorganiza-
tion of the Association would be nade with the indication
that such reorganization wculd conforn ainly to the
Government's proposed plan.

According to press reports the Finance Minister
declared in a statement made on April 12 that Japan's
export trade began to improve in the beginning of March.
He was quoted as statin that exports to third countries
outside of the Yen block showed little change during the
first three months of the year und that while exports to
Europe dropped, there were cains in exports to French
Indochina, Thailand and the Netherlands East Indies. Ex-
ports of cotton and silk, he stated, had increased during
the period compared with thc same months of the previous
year. No figures concerning Japan's foreign trade have
been released thus far this year and little information
otherwise has become available in the matter. It was
reported in the press that manufacturers of cotton yarn

bottoms. It appearud, however, that despite any shortage

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) General Rolations and Pross Comments.
On April ll the Prime Minister in a press inter-
view, the third which he had given since he assumed
office last July, made several comments regarding
American-Japanese relations. He gave as his opinion
that relations between the two countries were not
worsening. He stated that he presumed that so long
as Japan remained with the Axis and so long as the
Japanese continued the China Incident tho United
States would continue to bring economic prossuro to
bear but that he diā not think that the United States
would "oppresss Japan to the last". He said that it
was likely that Anglo-American aid-to-China measures
would be increased but that the effect was rather
doubtful. He stated that "on the busis of matter-of-
fact diplomacy there was not much difference between
what the United States had done with regard to Japan
since the conclusion of the Tripartite Alliance and
what tho United States did in the pre-alliance period.
In comments made by the Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs at the conference of the Prefectural Governors
held at Tokyo in mid-April, Mr. Ohashi is reported to
have stated that American aid to the Chinese National
Government could be expected to increase on the basis
of Mr. Lauchlin Currie's report to the President and
that moreover it would be difficult to foretell what
future developmonts might erise from tho continued
Goonomic pressuro on Japan by Great Britain and the

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
、心
United States.*
Little comnent appeared regerding the President's
acknowledgement at his press conforence on April 18
that dispositions hed been made to give China a share
of the Lease-Lend armanents end regarding the announce-
ment made on April 25 that paraliel agreements had
been concluded by the United States and British Govern-
ment with the Chinese National Government to make avail-
able to it, for currency stabilization purposes, the
sum of sixty million dcllars and ten million pounds,
respectively. In an editorial appeering in the ASAHI

The President's romarks in a press interview on
April 25 that American patrol vessols would operate as
far as the waters of tho Seven Seas and the speechos
of Secretaries Hull, Knox end Simpson during the latter
part of the month were given full publicity in all the
Japanese press. The general reaction was summarized
in the one comment that the United States was rushing
headlong down the road to war. It was contended that
the patrol systems instituted by the United States
represented merely a step towards outright convoy, if
they did not in effect constitute in themselves such
act ion. In tho oarly part of the month the Vico
Minister for Foroign Affairs was roportod to have made

of the press. On April 29 the Deputy Spokesman of the

to an inquiry whether an extension of the American

In an editorial appearing in the JAFAN TIMES AND
ADVERTISER on April 27, issue was taken with the
Secretary of State's speech on April 25. The editorial
held that it could be deduced from Mr. Hull's address
that the American Government intended to initiate a
vigorous policy involving some form of intervention
in the European War, possibly American entry theroin,
and that such action was to be considered as militating
against the early restoretion of peace throughout the
world. It stated that the way to increase the prospects
for peace was for other nations now cutside of the scope
of hostilities to remain alcof; that if other countries
join in the conflict peace must await a world-wide
decision. It offered the fact of Japan's entry into
the Tripartite Alliance as a caso in point in that such
action was a peaceful move as the Alliance was directed
at confining the war to those alroady in it. The
oditorial called upon the Presidont and his aides to
avoid further provocative acts and eddress thomselves
to schomes for the limitetion of war and the arresting
of all hostilities.
The news of the German successes in the Balkans
and of the reverses suffered by Britain and Allied
forces in Greece and predictions of the capture in
the near future of the Suez Canal and Gibraltar by the

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.99)

Axis and the cooperation of Turkey end Spain with the
Axis were strongly played up in the Japanese press. They
were, moreover, given a good bit of emphasis in connec-
tion with Anerican-Japanese relations. It was asserted
that a British victory could now be regarded as vir-
tually impossible and that this must effect American
foreign policy. The efficacy and value of continued
American assistance to Great Britain was directly chal-
lenged in cortain Japanese sections and there was
ventured the hope that the United Statos, recognizing
tho futility of its policy with rospect to Great Britain,
might allow the latter country to be defeated and pormit
a quick peace to bo reachcd on negotiated or Axis dic-
tated torms.*
These views were associated with the reports,
given prominent space in the Japanese press, of Mr.
Charles E. Lindbergh's recent speeches and the anti-war
mass meetings held in the United States. The hope was
expressed that such opinions being voiced by American
isolationists might represent a wavering of Amorican
public opinion in its support to Great Britain and an
indication of the possibility of the United States re-
examining its world policy.**
In its issue of April 29 the JAPAN TIMES AND AD-
VERTISER published a striking editorial in which under
the title of "Feace Terms" it set forth a suggested
basis for a negotiated peece in the European War and
the establishment of regional spheres of domination of

Embassy's despatch No. 5575, May 7, 1941.

the world by Germany, Japan, the British Empire, Soviet
Russia and the United States. A fairly detailed delimi-

power was given: with the United States bei"s assigned
the American continent; Japan the Eastern Pacific area;
and Germany Europe and most of Africa. The peace terms
were based on the supposition that the United States,
in whose hands rests the "momentous decision of war and
peace", would alter its policy of all-aid to Britain
to one of mediation. The main purpose of the editorial
was apparently to givo publicity to the view that the
United States' Govornment must bear responsibility for
the continuance of the war; and the peace terms, which
the editorial admitted would virtually constitute a
dictated peace by the Axis Powers, had a decided of-
ficially inspired cast.*
Towards the end of the month under review there
appeared in the Japanese press suggestions for a visit
of Foreign Minister Yosuke Matsuoka to the United States.
No official comment in the matter appeared but the idea
was accorded rather widospread suprort and omphasis.
Typical of the remarks mado in this connection were
those contained in a feature article written by a
commentator of the CHUGAI, urging Mr. Matsuoka to

policy, which he had announced on November 3, 1938, of
cooperution with the Chungking Government provided it
abandoned its present policy and revised its personnel
but that Japan should support the Nanking Regime under
Wang Ching-wei.**

(i) Russia.
(1) Russo-Japanese Neutrality Pact.
Following his visits to Berlin and Rome the Foreign
Minister arrived at Moscow on April 7 en route back to
* Embassy's telegram No. 619, April 30, 8 p.m.

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
-18
Japan. with the ennouncement that he would prolong his
stay in Moscow beyond the two or three days originelly
scheduled, the press in Japan predicted that Mr. Matsuoka's
interviews with the Soviet officials had had greater
results than had been anticipated and it was suggested

On the afternoon of April 13 a pact of neutrality
between Japan and the Soviet Union was signed at Moscow
between Mr. Matsuoka and the Japanese Ambassador, Lieu-
tenant General Tatekawa and Soviet Commissar for Foreign
Affairs, Mr. Molotov. The pact provided (1) "That the
two contracting parties agree to maintain peaceful and
friendly relations between themselves and to respect
mutually the territorial integrity and inviolability of
the other contractine party" and that (2) "in the event
that either of the contracting parties becomes an object
of military action on the part of one or more third
powers the other contracting party shall observe neutra-
lity throughout the entire period of such a conflict."
The pact was to come into force on the day of its ratifi-
cation and to remain valid for a period of five years
with the provision that it would be automatically extend-
ed for a further five years unless denounced a year prior
to its expiration.
Simultaneously with the signature of the pact a
joint declaration wes issued by the two Governments stating
that "Japan on its part agreed to respect the territo-
rial integrity and inviolability of the People's Republic
of Mongolia and the Soviet Union on its part to respect

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-19
the territorial integrity end inviolability of the Empire
of Manchoukuo".*
It will be noted that the pect is negative in
character. It is not an alliance and it dces nct provide
for concentration on problems of mutual interest to the
two parties and contains no commitment binding either
party to refrain from aiding directly or indirectly the
enemies of the other in the event of war but merely
engages them to "observe neutrality throughout the entire
period of such conflict." It is noted, however, that
the operation of the pact is not expressedly limited to
the eventuality of one party becoming subject of attack
and apparently, from the use of the plhrase "an object
of military action" in article 2, it provides for neu-
trality even in the event of one party's taking eggressive
action against a third state. With respect to Russia's
relations with China, there is no indication either
expressed or implied in the text of the treaty itself or
in the declaration made by the two Governments of any
Soviet agreement to alter its present policy vis-à-vis
the Chinese National Government. It was considered
possible that the reputed distinction made by the Soviet
Government between a neutrality and a non--aggression
pact related to the question of Soviet policy towards
China. Indeed, it appeared**that the question of Soviet
aid to China was excluded from Mr. Matsuoka's discussions
with the Soviet officials.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.

ment was commented upon in the press as being of con-
sierable significance. Mr. Ogura was expected to assumo
responsibility for the direction of economic affairs of
the country and the selection of a person from Japanese
commercial circles was believed to be aimed at affording
close cooperation between the Gov ernment end business.
At the same time it was understood that the policy which
he would institute would be one of adjusting the theore-
tical economic plans with business practices. It was
hoped that he might ameliorate bureaucratic control
which had mot with a good deal of opposition from business
leaders and that with the support from commercial and
financial circles he could bring about closer cooperation
between private business and industry end the Government.
It was stated that there wes a possibility of a trium-
virate of Prince Konoye, Baron Hiranuma and Mr. Ogura

voiced was that the reconstruction of the Cabinet had
resulted in its strengthening and that there was fulfilled
the need of a union between representatives of the
military, business and financial interests. It was
further considered that the Cabinet, which had become
a bit she. had been tiod over a possible crisis and

Throne but is considered now to be little more than an
association to carry out national spiritual mobiliza-
tion.*

Ministers of the Government upon domestic and foreign

that such funds were not surficient and that efforts
were being made further to increase them. In a press
interview given by the Governor of the Bank of Japan,
Mr. Toyo Yuki, ho was reported to have stated, inter
alia, that there need be no fear in Japan of uncontrol-
led inflation and that there was no reason
to hold that a state of inflation in the real sense had
already set in. He said that with the measures already
U
undertaken in Japan, despite the expenditures of the
Government which was provided in the huge budget of
12,200,000,000 yen the country should be able to over-
come any major inflationary difficulties. He further
stated that the low price policy was absolutely neces-
sary but with somo adjustments to secure increased
production of somo goods where it was found that the
production had been retarded by too low prices. He
also indicated in his remarks the possibility of further
more stringent measures being taken to increase savings
and control of funds.
It was announced during the month that the total
national debit at the end of March had reached the figure
of 29,847,000,000 yen and represented an increase of
6,962,000,000 yen from the year before.

VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.*
Prepared with the assistance of the office of the
Commercial Attaché.

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.98)
-30
Augmented Government control; the decision to revise
price control and fixing sstom and reeijust offivial
prices to combat the inflationary tendency: the in-
tensification of efforts to inoreass the supply of raw
materials, coal end power tc defense industries end
in turn reduce supplies to peace-time plants; greater
Government pressure to ste-up industrial production,
conserve materials and increase national savings in
order to absorb a heavier volume of national bonds; and
the continuance of the rise in commodity prices, featured
the trend of economic developments during April. Little
information was available concerning business conditions
COMM
and industrial production. The stock market sentiment
was dominated primarily by apprehension over the pos-
sibility of the United States entering the European War
and although prices firmed slightly on the reported suc-
cessful visit to Europe of the Foreign Minister and the
conclusion of the Jananese-Soviet Neutrality Pact the
1T7
improvement was short-lived in fece of the beliof that
Americen aid to Britain would soon assume a more active
form. Wholesale prices during April were up .4% in
comparison with those for the month of March and 2.6%
in comparison with the same period of the year before
and retail prices increased by .2% in comparison with
those for March and 2.2% in comparison with those for
the same month of the previous year. The stronger up-
ward tendency of commodity prices apparently caused
the Government to tighten the luxury ban by adding
several articles to the prohibited list, fixing new
maximum prices for other articles and issuing strict
warnings to dealers egainst breaking regulations. Follow-

ing the rationing of rice as of April 1 it was announced
that four moatless days would be enforced during each
month as a moans to furthor conserve food supplics.

The first of tho new Associations for the control
of key industries was set up on April 26 in the iron and
steel industries. The Association, known as the "Iron
and Steel Control Association", was charged with ef-
fecting the increase of output of iron and steel to
4
Jepan and Manchuria with the end in view of attaining
71
self-sufficiency in iron and steel in the so-called
"yen-bloc". It was also announced that the Army would
place the management of war-time factories under a
planned control system in order to facilitate the increase
in efficiency and output. As an incentive for increased
produotion considcrstion would be given to the officiency
of individual factories in determing their profits in-
stead of the profits being fixed on a standard profit
scale. On April 12 thcre camo into forco an ordinanco
providing for tighter restrictions on foreign oxchange.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Ambassador's conversations with the
Foreign Minister.
On May 14 the Ambassador was received by the
Minister for Foreign Affairs for the first time since
the latter's return from Europe. After completing
the routine business Mr. Grew asked Mr. Matsuoka
whether he had any particular thoughts with regard
to the present general situation. The subsequent
remarks which the Minister then made and which he said
ho was addressing to Mr. Grew as an Amorican friend
and not as the American Ambassedor were bellicose
both in tone and in substanco. The main points of
tho Ministor's discourse were an expression of deep
concern lest the United States proceed to convoy
ships to Great Britain as such action would almost
certainly lead to war with Germany; an accusation
that to declare a neutrality zone in the Atlantic or
any other ocean was a breach of international law; a
statement that Hitler had shown "great patience and
generosity" in not declaring war on the United States
in the face of America's supplying Great Britain with
war-materials; and a declaration that should Germany
sink American ships in the Atlantic and should the
United States then attack German submarines he would
regard this as an act of American aggression which
would call for deliberation as to the applicability
of Article 3 of the Tripartite Alliance, and he thought
there was no doubt that such deliberation would lead
to war between Japan and the United States. He said
that this issue therefore lies exclusively in the hands

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of President Roosevelt. He added that the "nanly,
decent and reasonable" thing for the United States to
do, in view of its present policy and actions, was to
declare war openly on Germany instead of engaging in
acts of war under cover of neutrality.
The Ambassador rebutted each of Mr. Matsuoka's
remarks in turn and he said that whether the Minister
was speaking to him as the Ambassador or not, he, as
an American citizen, emphatically resented Mr.
Matsuoka's charge that the United States was guilty
of unmanly, indecent and unreasonable conduct. The
Minister thereupon withdrew his remark. The Ambassador
pointed out the fallacy of Mr. Matsuoka's reasoning
with regard to the American alleged breach of interna-
tional law, spoke of the United States' consistent
policy concerning the freedom of the seas and argued
the full justification of such measures as the United
States might put into force in the face of Hitler's
continual breaches of international law. To Mr. Grew's
reference to Japan's action in attacking China and
ruthless bombing of undefended cities and populations
without a declaration of war Mr. Matsuoka merely
remarked that the situations were entirely different
and changed the subject.
The conversation then turned to Japan's policy
in connection with the Southward Advance.
The Amtassador observed that in spite of the Minister's
constant assurances of peaceful intentions other views
had been advanced here. Mr. Matsuoka stated that he
deplored the utterances from some Japanese sections
openly advocating the use of force in the prosecution

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of tho advance and ho declared that Prince Konoye and
he himself were determined to see that the advance
should be carried out only by peaceful measures, but
he added significently, "unless circumstances render
this impossible." In the conversation which ensued
concerning this point the Ambassador stressed the grave
concern of the United States in the potentialities of
the situation particularly as they related to the
integrity of the British lifeline in the Far East.
The Ambassador in the course of further conversa-
tion spoke of his regret that Japanese-American re-
lations were, for the moment, negative and non-con-
structive; that he had steadily worked to build up
something permanently constructive in thoserelations;
that he had been very hopeful of attaining concrete
results during the period of the Yonai Cabinet (Spring
of 1940); and that he was profoundly disappointed that
Japan's complete change of policy had made those efforts
abortive. The Minister replied in response to Mr.
Grew's remark that he still looked forward to the day
when Japan and the United States could begin to re-
construct their relations on a firm basis. that al-
though cherishing the same hope he considered that the
whole future of the world and civilization now lies
in the hands of one single man, President Roosevelt.*
Later on the same day of this conversation the
Ambassador received a letter from the Foreign Minister
in which Mr. Matsuoka stated that he desired to correet

seemed probable that solicitations of the Axis Powers
for a firm stand by the Foreign Minister against the
United States were acceptable to him in giving impetus

It was understood from good authority that a

and that counsels were divided as to the pursuit of
the policy to any dénouement involving a crisis with
the United States. It was further understood that
despite the fact that the Government did not con-
template any break from present Japanese policy the
Government was exercising care to prevent Japan from
taking hasty action and that in any event any such
grave decision as that of implementing any obligation
under Japan's alliance with Germany would not rest with
the Foreign Minister alone.**

(c) Japan's Abrogation of the Fur Seal
Convention.
In reply to the American Government's note trans-
mitted by the Embassy to the Foreign Office on March
18, the Embassy received a formal communication from
the Foreign Minister dated May 9, stating that the
Japanese Government was unable to withdraw its notice
of abrogation of the Fur Seal Convention but confirming
that it was prepared to conclude a new convention on
the basis of the principles it had proposed at the
time the notice was given last October. The note
asserted that contrary to the contention of the American
Government, seals from Pribiloff Island did frequent
waters adjacent to Japan; it denied that fish and
other marine matter consumed by the seals was of little
commercial importance to Japan;and it advocated the
return to pelagic sealing in order to reduce the
herds to the figure of 850,000 originally proposed
by the Japanese Government.***

It will be recalled that in the latter part o

respect appeared in the vernacular newspapers at tho

Mr. Matsuoka statod that although Jepan would pursue
its policy of peace towerds the United States and
discussions would continue to this end "there is
no necessity for me to visit America now since I know
the United States very well". He suggested instead
that "it would be preferable for President Roosevelt
or Secretary Hull to come here since they do not pos-

Thore wore no further developmonts on this subject
during the month under reviow.*
On May 30 the Fo reign Minister released a state-
ment reasserting that the Tripartite Alliance was the
basis of Japan's foreign policy. He referred to re-
ported speculation in some American newspapers that
Japan was becoming cooler towards the Alliance and
declared that such reports were distorted and repre-
sented a "great misunderstanding" of the truth. He
said that there had been not the slightest deflection
from the course of Japan's policy and that Japan would
not fail to carry out faithfully her obligations under

In a radio address delivered on May 27 Captain
Hideo Hiraide, spokesman of the Japanese Navy, stated,
inter alia, that should wer break out between Japan
and the United States the American fleet could not
attack Japan directly but would engage in guerilla
operations. Such operations, he said, wore possible
but would be difficult due to the distances of American
bases from Japan and would be less threatening than
similar operations in the Atlantic Ocean. The spokesman
declared that the Japanese Navy was strong and ready
for any contingencies; that two hundred naval vessels
were at present engaged in operations along the China
Coast and that Japan had an additional three hundred
warships available for defense of the Western Pacific;
and that the Japanese Navy air-arm had four thousand
airplanes. The spokesman also stated that it was impos-
sible to guarantee that Japan would not enter the war.**

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1) Military Operations.**
There was a marked increase during May in Japa-
nese military activity in China both in the air and on
the ground. Four offensives were conducted; one in the
southern part of Shansi Province and the others north
Embassy's telegram No. 729, May 24, 3 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 751, May 29, 5 p.m.
**
Prepared with the assistance of the office of the
Military Attaché.

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.98)
-13-
of Hankow in Huneh Prcvince, at Chuki in Chekieng Pro-
vince and near Weiahow in Kwangtung Province. There
were, besides, riner clearing operations elsewhere in
occupied territory.
The most importent cf these operations was the
campaign in Shensi Province. On May 7 Japanese forces,
estimated at abcut six divisions in strength, began a
large-scale offensive directed against some 300,000
Chinese troops concentrated in the south-westorn sec-
tion of the province along the Yellow River. The
Japanese scheme of meneuver was to encircle and cut
through the Chinese in the zone between the river and
the mountains to the north. By the end of the month
Japenese reports claimed that the Chinese forces had
been decimated and that the escaped remnants had fled
to the mountains for refuge. The press accounts of
this campaign were reminiscent of the reports of numerous
previous operations undertaken by the Japanese military
forces to clear Chinese troops out of Shansi Province.
(2) General Relations.
On May 14 the Japanese Ambassador tc the Nanking
Government, Mr. Kumaturo Honda, returned to Tokyo for
the purpose of holding important discussions with the
Central Government authorities regarding Japanese
policy concerning China.
Prior to his arrival here Ambassador Honda, in
interviews given at Shanghai on the eve of his departure
from China and at Kyoto, propounded his views which he
said also were concurred in by the Japanese Army, Navy
end oreign Officc officials in China, regarding the
present situation in China and regarding the Japenese

=== Page 163 === (confidence 1.00)
-16-
policy for the settlement of the China Incident. He
declared that the only course for the disposal of
the Incident lay in the strengthening and solidifica-
tion of the Nanking Government. He said that it was
foolish to consider negotiations with the Chungking
Government since Chiang Kai-shek would negotiate with
Japan only on the condition of the wholesale withdrawl
of the Japanese troops from China and that the Generalis-
simo, in view of the present internationel developments,
had no desire to discuss peace. He said that friction
between the Kuomintang and the Communists had been over-
estimated and that the recent American loans to the
Chinese National Government had reduced the adverse
effect of the conclusion of the Japan-Soviet Neutrality
Pact. He drew the conclusion that opportunity for
direct negotiations with the Chungking Government had
long past, especially since Japan had recognized the
Nanking National Government; that the Chinese National
Government would attempt to prolong the war with the
hope of being able to exploit the world situation to
its advantage; that no peace was possible by mediation
through a third nation; that the strengthening of the
Nanking Government was the only policy which could
offoct the settloment of the China Incident; and that
Japan should carry out this policy by stabilizing the
Nanking Government and by bettering the livelihood of
the Chinese masses beyond that prevailing under the
jurisdiction of the Chungking Government to induce
them to give political support to the Nanking Government.
He said that as the Nanking Government became more
powerful the matter of its absorbing the Chungking

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
Government might develop at a later time.
On the seme date that Ambassador Honia gave his
interview at Shanghai, General Shiuroku Hata, Commander
in Chief of the Japanese forces in China, in an inter-
view given at Nanking, warned Japan against over-op-
timism and over-dependence upon the temporarily improved
diplomatic situation and stated thet military operations
would continue in China in an effort to deal the enemy
a crushing blow". He said that "resistance of the
Chiang Kai-shek forces and the Chinese communist army
must be stamped out." *
Colonel Hayao Mabuchi spokesman for the Ministry of
War, in a statement made at Tokyo on May 24 regarding
Japanese military operations in China, declared that the
secret of solving the China Affair was the use of mili-
tary force in order to completely destroy Chinese
resistence.**
Although voicing some doubts as to the possibility
of affecting an early consolidation of the Nanking
Regime authority in China and as to the advisability of
giving economic aid to it in view of the opportunity for
Japanese materials finding their way to Chungking, the
press generally endorsed Ambassador Honda's plan for
strengthening the Nanking Regime. In the editorial
comments there were included, amongs others, the follow-
ing pointed observations: one, thet since the settlement

discussions concerning Japan's China policy.*
Concurrently with the reports in the press of the
official deliberations upon Japan's China policy all
vernacular newspepers carried daily lead articles
giving accounts of intensified and reportedly highly
successful military operations in Chinc, The Embassy
was informed that the press had been instructed to
play-up this latter news.
It was, indeed, evident that the Government
had purposely brought the problem of the China Incident
to the fore and had instigated the publicity campaign in
the matter to meet a si tuation that had arisen and which
apparently demanded some immediate action. Since the
return to Japan of the Foreign Minister from his trip
to Europe a definite attitude of expectancy had prevailed
in the country that the time was at hend: now that Japan
had made an agreement wicl: Soviet Russia and settled
the French Indochina - Tnailand issue, to take effective
action to resolve the China Incident. The press had
elmost daily repeated the theme that a settlement in
China must be the focal pcint of Japen's diplomacy.
There were at the same time murnurs of r wing dis-
satisfaction at the continued stalemate in China and at
the failure of the Government to take positive measures.
In view of the quickened tempo of European developments
an increasing desire was also manifest for a stabiliza-
tion of the China situation so as to enable Japan to have
freor hands to deal with other important international
* Embassy's telegram No. 712, May 20, 7 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No..689, May 16, 6 p.m.

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
matters facing the country. The formulae which the
Government had thus far advanced and the actions it had
taken to implement them had not oorn much tangible
fruit. Its latest effort to negotiate with Chungking,
had given little prospect of resulting in a settlement
acceptable to Japan. At home, moreover, the increasingly
onerous restrictions on the daily life of the people and
the strain on the national economy had told upon the
enthusiasm of the people particularly as no indications
were being received by them that their sacrifices were
resulting in any possibility of achieving the objectives
in China.
Some new step in the formation of a policy towards
China seemed to be logical and some form of solution
called for, illusory though its realization might be,
which could be considered by the Japanese public as
having body and substance and which together with en-
couraging news of the military operations in China
could stimulate enthusiasm for tha continued prosecution
of Japan's activities in China end further effort ro-
quired to intensify those activitios.
To this end the Honda statement which included the
affirmation of the existence of a "concrete plen" ap-
parently had been devised and publicized. It set
forth the seemingly acceptable thesis that Japan's
ends could be obtained through the Nanking Government
and offered as plausible reasoning to the sufficiency
of the"plan", proposals for an increase of that regime's
economic powers, for a betterment of the conditions of
the masses in the occupied territories and for the con-
solidation of authority through limited but conoentrated

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)

military activities.*
(b) French Indochina.
(1) Conclusion of econoric agreement
between Japan and Fr nch Indochina.
On May 6 there were signed the Convention of the
Establishment of Navigation and tho Agreement Con-
cerning Customs, Tariff,. Trade and Mothods of Payments,
marking the conclusion of the economic negotiations
between Japan and French Indochina. The Convention,
entered intfoá period of five years, provided for
reciprocal most-favored nation treatment of nationals,
vessels and commercial and industrial enterprises. The
Agreement, to be effective until December 31, 1943,
provided for the most-favored nation treatment of
imports into each country from the other with special
reductions or exemptions from inport duties of a large
number of articles and settlement of payments to be mado
directly between the two countries except for any
balance over five million yen which would be settled in
a foroign curroncy. A credit for a ycar was accorded to
Japan for payments for rice purchasea from Indochina.
Six related documents were also signed which
provided for the establishment of Japanese schools in
Indochina, the opening up to Japanese of certain pro-
fessions formerly prohibited to foreigners, the pri-
vilege for Japanese nationals and interests to perticipate
in banking, conmercial, agricultural, mining and water

(2) Treatyof peace between France
and Thailand.
The treaty of peace between France nd Thailand,
for which an agreement had been concluded on March 1I
(see political roport for March) was formerly signed
on May 9 together with the Franco-Japan and the Thai-
Japan protocols. The only difference of any importance
between the final treaty and the previous agreement
related to the demilitarization of the ceded areas end
the establishment of a commission under Japanese control
for the execution of the terms of the treaty. In this
connection no provision was made for any demilitariza-
tion of a zone on the Indo-Chinese side of the caw border
and instead of three commissions only one temporary
mixed commission for the delimitation of the frontier
was provided for.*

was given to the economic negotiations with the Nether-

relations second only to the question of the settlement
of the China Incident. The long drawn out affair was,
with the successful conclusion of agreements with Russia
and French Indochina, taken up as the salient issue on
the Japanese diplomatic agenda. Several statements were

(e) Germany.
The first meeting in Japan of the General Commission
established under the Tripartite Alliance was held on
May 9 under the chairmanship of the Foreign Minister and
attended by General Eugene Ott, German Ambassador and Mr.
Mario Indelli, the Italian Ambassador. The press reported
that questions including economic subjects, relating to
the cooperation between the three Axis Povers were
discussed.
The press during the month expressed, inter alia, the
view that Germany should concentrate upon the subjugation
of the British Isles in order to forestall effective
American assistance to Great Britain and, at the same time,
asserted that Germany should limit its sphere of activity
to the European continent and surrounding areas. In this

(r) Iran.

(g) General International Relations.
There has been mentioned in the foregoing section
of this report, Japanese Relations With the United States,
the remarks of the Minister for Foreign Affairs to the
Ambassador and Mr. Matsuoke's statement released on May
30 rogarding Jepanese policy towards the South Seas.
In an article written by Admiral Sankichi Takahashi,
retired, and published during the month under review,
some searching observations were presented concerning
Japan's southward advance, particularly with respect to
the Netherlands East Indies. Referring to the demand
voiced by some sections in Japan for an immediate occupa-
tion of the Netherlands East Indies he pointed out that
* Embassy's telegram No. 627, May 1, 7 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 635, May 15, 10 p:m.

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.98)
-28
if Japan started military operations they would likely
in
result-not just a local conflict but a gigantic interna-
tional war. He questioned whether it would be wise to
venture into another battle while the Sino-Japanese
hostilities were still continuing. He said that if such
a military campaign could be brought to a successful con-
clusion within a short time there was no doubt that a
settlement of the Sino-Japanese hostilities would be
greatly expedited thereby but that if Japan should be
caught in an endurance contest the conflict with China
could also become hopelessly entangled. He mentioned
that an attack upon the Netherlands East Indies might
very well defeat its very purpose as instead of resulting
in Japan's securing the materials it wanted from that
country Japan might find that the materials had been
destroyed before-hand to prevent their falling into Japa-
nese hands. He urged that Japan should first follow a
policy of attaining economic gains in order to meet its
much-needed requirements for raw materials. He asserted
the oft-repeated contention that Japan harbored no
territorial designs against the Netherlands East Indies
but was merely asking them to open their door to Japa-
nese trade and industries. He concluded, however, with
the remark that Japan should certainly take up the
challenge "if the Netherlands East Indies authorities
attempted to demonstrate hostility towards Japan by
deliberately misinterpreting Japan's true intentions."
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
e
(a) Political Parties.

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
During the latter part of Ayril there appeared in
Japan a new political party named the "Tohokai", headcd
by Mr. Seigo Nakano. On May 1 a meeting of the percy
was held in Tokyo and attended by a large gathering, The
Tohokai was the first political party to be organized
since the dissolution of political parties in the surmer
of 1940. The Government apparently had not endeavored to
obstruct its organization and, although it was still too
early for a clear indication to bo seen of future po-
litical trends in Japan, it eppeared possible that after
the reorganization of the Imperial Rule Assistance Associa-
tion in April, whereby that organization was shorn of most
of its political power, the emergence of a separate politi-
cal party might be indicative of a return to the same po-
litical organization of the country under various parties
which prevailed before the "new structure" movement was
introduced by Prince Konoye last summer. From the member-
ship and manifesto issued by the Tohokai it was apparently
to be strongly nationalistic in character.*

VI. GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
Business activities generally during May continued
to be ciroumscribed by adverse conditions. The uncertain
* Embassy's telegram No. 630, May 2, 5 p.m.

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.98)
-30
international situation had a disccuraging affect and
much concern was felt over the drastic fcod situation,
over the difficulties in export trade anc in forr.
exchange transactions., over
the continuod rise during
the month of commodity prices and living costs and ovor
continued shortage of shipping facilities. At the sane
time the Government's program for control and regula-
tion of national economy was implemented by further
measures making normal business operations more dif-
ficult and giving further priority to industries engaged
in munitions production. The stock market was general-
ly depressed durin g tho month due to approhonsion over
President Roosevelt's adaress on May 27, the difficulty
of gauging American action regarding the European War,
gloomy outlook concerning Japan's foreign trade and
increased taxation as a measure to absorb the advancing
purchasing unds of the people.
Of the more important ordinances promulgated during
the month were the Machinery Management Law, the Foreign
Trade Control Ordinance and the Revised Foreign Exchange
Control Regulations. The Foreign Trade Control Ordi-
nance, which was to become effective on June 13, gave the
Government authority to order the importation and exporta-
tion of commodities or to restrict or prohibit such importa-
tion or exportation and to determine the disposal of the
articles imported. The Revised Foreign Exchange Control
Rogulations, to become effective June 1, facilitated
remittances to the yen bloc but made still more difficult
remittancos to foroign countries.
Heavy frost in the mountaineous areas of Central Japan
during the early part of the month reportedly did a great

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
-31-
deal of damage to the first silk crop. It was announced
that the estimated sugar crop for the coming year would
represent a reduction of twenty-two per cent from the
average of that of the last four years and a drastic
reduction in consumption of sugar in Japan was fore-
cast. Estimates for the wheat crop this year also
showed a large reduction from that of last year and a
slight reduction from the average of the past few
yoars. At the boginning of the month it was announced
that the Government would purchase all wheat, barley
and rice produced in the country and undertake official
distribution of those grains.
The press carried reports throughout the month
regarding discussions being undertaken by the Govern-
ment concerning prices in Japan and on May 31 it was
announced that the price-stop system, based on the prices
prevailing on September 18, 1939, would be abolished as
of Octoborla According to pross accounts prices under
the new policy apparently would bo set arbitrarily.
In determining the quotations, equipmont and production
officioncy of tho producors and transportation faci-
litios would bo included amongst the factors to bo
takon into account, but tho most important consido-
ration was to bo that of fixing tho pricos at lovols
which would oncourago oxpansion of production.
It was announced on May 1 that arrangamonts had
finally bcin concludod for tho amalgamation of tho ports

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
of Tokyo and Yokohama and that for the first time the
port of Tokyo was to be opened to foreign commerce. It
was understood that Tokyo would take care of shipping
from "Manchoukuo", China and also coastal trade, while
Yokohama would handle shipping from foreign countries.
On May 3 the press announced the formulation of plans
for the establishment of the "Newspaper Federation". The
federation was to be organized as a public juridical
person and vould undertake to make recommendations to
the Government concerning public opinion, to investigate
means for improving the editing and management of news-
papers and to fix and adjust allotments of newsprint and
other materials.
