I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Ambassador's conversations with the
Foredgn Minister.
On May 14 the Ambassador was received by the
Minister for Foreign Affairs for the first time since
the latter's return from Europe. After completing
the routine business Mr. Grew asked Mr. Matsuoka
whether he had any particular thoughts with regard
to the present general situation. The subsequent
remarks which the Minister then made and which he said
he was addressing to Mr. Grew as an Amorican friend
and not as the American Ambassador* were bellicose
both in tone and in substance. The main points of
tho Ministor's discourse were an expression of deep
concern lest the United States proceed to convoy
ships to Great Britain as such action would almost
certainly lead to war with Germany; an accusation
that to declare a neutrality zone in the Atlantic or
any other ocean was a breach of international law; a
statement that Hitler had shown "great patience and
generosity" in not declaring war on the United States
in the face of America's supplying Great Britain with
war-materials; and a declaration that should Germany
sink American ships in the Atlantic and should the
United States then attack German submarines he would
regard this as an act of American aggression which
would call for deliberation as to the applicability
of Article 3 of the Tripartite Alliance, and he thought
there was no doubt thet such deliberation would lead
to war between Japan and the United States. He said
that this issue therefore lies exclusively in the hands

=== Page 196 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
of President Roosevelt. He added that the "manly,
decent and reasonable" thing for the United States to
do, in view of its present policy and actions, was to
declare war openly on Germany instead of engaging in
acts of war under cover of neutrality.
The Ambassador rebutted each of Mr. Matsuoka's
remarks in turn and he said that whether the Minister
was speaking to him as the Ambassador or not, he, as
an American citizen, emphatically resented Mr.
Matsuoka's charge that the United States was guilty
of unmanly, indecent and unreasonable conduct. The
Minister thereupon withdrew his remark. The Ambassador
pointed out the fallacy of Mr. Matsuoka's reasoning
with regard to the American alleged breach of interna-
tional law, spoke of the United States' consistent
policy concerning the freedom of the seas and argued
the full justification of such measures as the United
States might put into force in the face of Hitler's
continual breaches of international law. To Mr. Grew's
reference to Japan's action in attacking China and
ruthless bombing of undefended cities and populations
without a declaration of war Mr. Matsuoka merely
remarked that the situations were entirely different
and changed the subject.
The conversation then turned to Japan's policy
in connection with the Southward Advance.
The Ambassador observed that in spite of the Minister's
constant assurances of peaceful intentions other views
had been advanced here. Mr. Matsuoka stated that he
deplored the utterances from some Japanese sections
openly advocating the use of force in the prosecution

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of tho advance and ho declared that Prince Konoye and
he himself were determined to see that the advance
should be carried out only by peaceful measures, but
he added significantly, "unless circumstances render
this impossible." In the conversation which ensued
concerning this point the Ambassador stressed the grave
concern of the United States in the potentialities of
the situation particularly as they related to the
integrity of the British lifeline in the Far East.
The Ambassador in the course of further conversa-
tion spoke of his regret that Japanese-American re-
lations were, for the moment, negative and non-con-
structive; that he had steadily worked to build up
something permanently constructive in thoserelations;
that he had been very hopeful of attaining concrete
results during the period of the Yonai Cabinet (Spring
of 1940); and that he was profoundly disappointed that
Japan's complete change of policy had made those efforts
abortive. The Minister replied in response to Mr.
Grew's remark that he still looked forward to the day
when Japan and the United States could begin to re-
construct their relations on a firm basis. that al-
though cherishing the same hope he considered that the
whole future of the world and civilization now lies
in the hands of one single man, President Roosevelt.*
Later on the same day of this conversation the
Ambassador received a letter from the Foreign Minister
in which Mr. Matsuoka stated that he desired to correet
* Embassy's telegram No. 673, May 14, 5 p.m.

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
his misusing of a word and that he had not meant to
say "indecently" but rather "indiscretion". He added
that the thought he wished to inpart tc the Ambassador
was that the United States Government should be more
cautious, reasonable and careful.*
On the following day the Ambassador in replying
to Mr. Matsuoka and thanking him for his letter wrote:
"It is my bclief that the American
Government is exercising careful discretion
and that the course which it is following
is eminently reasonable both in appearance
and in fact, but in the face of great pro-
vocation our people, as history shows, have
not been, and are not now, inclined to
allow an over-cautious attitude to interfere
with vigorous action in defense of their
rights and legitimate interests or in follow-
ing what they conceive to be the dictates of
humanity.
I naturally regret the grave and far-.
reaching implication of Your Excellency's
The Ambassador received a further written com-
munication from the Foreign Minister***and on May 19
held another conversation with him. The conversa-
tion was held in an atmosphere of informality. The
Foreign Minister sought to minimize his use during
the former interview of intimidating and bellicose
language, stating that he only wished frankly to
presant his thoughts and he said that when he had
talked about Japan's future policy in the contingency
of war breaking out between the United States and
Germany he was dealing with a hypothetical case and
*Embassy's telegram No. 674, May 14, 6 p.m.
**Embassy's telegram No. 679, May 17, 3 p.m.
***Embassy's telegram No. 696, May 17, 1 a.m.

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.98)
-5~
merely presenting his on personal views but he
nevertheless confirmed his vicvs, as exprossed on Nay
14, with regard to a war ensuing between the United
States and Japan in the event of hostilities occur-
ring between the United States and Germany under the
premise he had mentioned. Upon the Minister's stating
that in the deliberation with Japan:s allies as to
the applicability of Article 3 should the United States
go to war with Germany Japan would only have one out
of three votes the Ambassador expressed estonishment
that Japan could thus be led to surrender its freedom
of action in an issue of such vitel importance to
Japan's own future. The Ambassador explained at some
length America's position as regards the world move-
ment of conquest and he expressed the view that if
Japan really desired peace with the United States the
American measures of self-defense could not possibly
be interpreted as acts of aggression. With a view to
making perfectly clear to the Minister the attitude of
the American Government towards the whole issue the
Ambassador read to him the entire text of Mr. Hull's
address before the American Society of Tuternational
Law on April 24 on the subject of The United States
And The World Situation.*
In conversations with the British and French
Ambassadors also held on May 14 the Foreign Minister
also made the statement to Sir Robert Craigie and
M. Arsène-Henry that in his opinion Japan would be
* Embassy's telegram No. 707, May 19, 10 a.m.

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
obliged to enter the war on the sids of Germany should
that country attack American convoys and should hosti-
lities ensue between the United States and Germany. He
further reiterated a comment, made in his second above-
mentioned written communication to the Ambassador, that
he feared that if the United States entered the war
it would be Armageddon and the complete destruction
of civilization. In Mr. Matsuoka's conversation with
Sir Robert Craigie the question was also discussed
of American assistance to Great Britain. In this
connootion Sir Robert Craigie referred to a previous
statement reportedly made by the Minister which was
to the effect that in Mr. Matsuoka's opinion American
aid could not be of decisive help to England before
the end of this year by which time Britain would be
beaten and the United States would have withdrawn from
the struggle to devote its attention solely to its
own security.*
There was reason to consider that the inspiration
for the Minister's statements to the Ambassador was
not devoid of German influence and was as well reflec-
tive of the Foreign Minister's concern for the defense
of the pro-Axis policy, for which he was largely re-
sponsible and which was aimed in part, if not wholly,
at intimidating the United States into isolation. It
seemed probable that solicitations of the Axis Powers
for a firm stand by the Foreign Minister against the
United States were acceptable to him in giving impetus
* Embassy's telegram No. 680, May 15, 5 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 688, May 16, 5 p.m.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
-7
for the continuance of the course of intimidation as
against a recognition of the failure of the policy
adopted.*
It was understood from good authority that a
part of the Japanese Government did not entirely con-
cur with or support the policy of the Foreign Minister
and that counsels were divided as to the pursuit of
the policy to any dénouement involving a crisis with
the United States. It was further understood that
despite the fact that the Government did not con-
template any break from present Japanese policy the
x Government was exercising care to prevent Japan from
taking hasty action and that in any event any such
grave decision as that of implementing any obligation
under Japan's alliance with Germany would not rest with
the Foreign Minister alone.**

the American Consulate at that city. On the follow-
ing day the Ambassador made strong oral representations
to the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs calling at-
tention to the fact that five notes in seven months had
been sent to the Foreign Office concerning the bombing

(c) Japan's Abrogation of the Fur Seal
Convention.
In reply to the American Government's note trans-
mitted by the Embassy to the Foreign Office on March
18, the Embassy received a formal communication from
the Foreign Minister dated May 9, stating that the
Japanese Government was unable to withdraw its notice
of abrogation of the Fur Seal Convention but confirming
that it was prepared to conclude a new convention on
the basis of the principles it had proposed at the
time the notice was given last October. The note
asserted that contrary to the contention of the American
Government, seals from Pribiloff Island did frequent
waters adjacent to Japan; it denied that fish and
other marine matter consumed by the seals was of little
commercial importance to Japan;and it advocated the
return to pelagic sealing in order to reduce the
herds to the figure of 850,000 originally proposed
by the Japanese Government.***

It will be recalled that in the latter part o
the month of April suggestions had appeared in tho
press that the Foreign Minister, following the suc-
cessful conclusion of his visit to Moscow, should make
a trip to Washington. Further writings in this
respect appeared in the vernacular newspapers at the
beginning of tho month. On Mey 4, in an interviow
given to the Japanese pross corrospondents at Kyoto,
Mr. Matsuoka stated that although Jepan would pursue
its policy of peace towerds the United States and
discussions would continue to this end "there is
no necessity for me to visit America now since I know
the United States very well". He suggested instead
that "it would be preferable for President Roosevelt
or Secretary Hull to come here since they do not pos-
sess knowledge of Japan".
Thore wore no further developmonts on this subject
during the month under reviow.*
On May 30 the Fo reign Minister released a state-
ment reasserting that the Tripartite Alliance was the
basis of Japan's foreign policy. He referred to re-
ported speculation in some American newspapers that
Japan was becoming cooler towards the Alliance and

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1) Military Operations.**
There was a marked increase during May in Japa-
nese military activity in China both in the air and on
the ground. Four offensives were conducted; one in the
southern part of Shansi Province and the others north
Embassy's telegram No. 729, May 24, 3 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 751, May 29, 5 p.m.
**
Prepared with the assistance of the office of the
Military Attaché.

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
of Hankow in Hupeh Prcvince, at Chuki in Chekiang Pro-
vince and near Waiahow in Kwangtung Province. There
were, besides, minor claaring operations elsewhere in
occupied erritory.
The most importent of these operations was the
campaign in Shensi Province. On May 7 Japanese forces,
estimated at about six divisions in strength, began a
large-scale offensive directed against some 300,000
Chinese troops concentrated in the south-western sec-
tion of the province along the Yellow River. The
Japanese scheme of maneuver was to encircle and cut
through the Chinese in the zone between the river and
the mountains to the north. By the end of the month
Japanese reports claimed that the Chinese forces had
been decimated and that the escaped remnants had fled
to the mountains for refuge. The press accounts of
this campaign were reminiscent of the reports of numerous
previous operations undertaken by the Japanese military
forces to clear Chinese troops out of Shansi Province.
(2) General Relations.
On May 14 the Japanese Ambassador to the Nanking
Government, Mr. Kumaturo Honda, returned to Tokyo for
the purpose of holding important disoussions with the
Central Government authorities regarding Japanese
policy concerning China.
Prior to his arrival here Ambassador Honda, in
interviews given at Shanghai on the eve of his departure
from China and at Kyoto, propounded his views which he
said also were concurred in by the Japanese Army, Navy
end oreign Officc officlals in China, regarding the
present situatiou in China and regarding the Japanese

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
policy for the settlement of the China Incident. He
declared that the only course for the disposal of
the Incident lay in the strengthening and solidifica-
tion of the Nanking Government. He said that it was
foolish to consider negotiations with the Chungking
Government since Chiang Kai-shek would negotiate with
Japan only on the condition of the wholesale withdrawl
of the Japanese troops from China and that the Generalis-
simo, in view of the present international developments,
had no desire to discuss peace. He said that friction
between the Kuomintang and the Communists had been over-
estimated and that the recent American loans to the
Chinese National Government had reduced the adverse
effect of the conclusion of the Japan-Soviet Neutrality
Pact. He drew the conclusion that opportunity for
direct negotiations with the Chungking Government had
long past, especially since Japan had recognized the
Nanking National Government; that the Chinese National
Government would attempt to prolong the war with the
hope of being able to exploit the world situation to
its advantage; that no peace was possible by mediation
through a third nation; that the strengthening of the
Nanking Government was the only policy which could
effect the settlement of the China Incident; and that
Japan should carry out this policy by stabilizing the
Nanking Government and by bettering the livelihood of
the Chinese masses beyond that prevailing under the
jurisdiction of the Chungking Government to induce
them to give political support to the Nanking Government.
He said that as the Nanking Government became more
powerful the matter of its absorbing the Chungking

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
Government might develop at a later time.
On the same date that Ambassador Honda gave his
interview at Shanghai, General Shiuroku hata, Commander
in Chief of the Japanese forces in China, in an inter-
view given at Nanking, warned Japan against over-op-
timism and over-dependence upon the temporarily improved
diplomatic situation and stated thet military operations
would continue in China in an effort to deal the enemy
a "crushing blow". He said that "resistance of the
Chiang Kai-shek forces and the Chinese communist army
must be stamped out." *
Colonel Hayao Mabuchi spokesman for the Ministry of
War, in a statement made at Tokyo on May 24 regarding
Japanese military operations in China, declared that the
secret of solving the China Affair was the use of mili-
tary force in order to completely destroy Chinese
resistance.**
Although voicing some doubts as to the possibility

(b) French Indochina.
(1) Conclusion of economic agreement
between Japan and French Indochina.
On May 6 there were signed the Convention of the
Establishment of Navigation and the Agreoment Con-
cerning Customs, Tariff,. Trade and Mothods of Payments,
marking the conclusion of the economic negotiations
between Japan and French Indochina. The Convention,
entered intfof period of five years, provided for
reciprocal most-favored nation treatment of nationals,
vessels and commercial and industrial enterprises. The
Agreement, to be effective until December 31, 1943,
provided for the most-favored nation treatment of
imports into each country from the other with special
reductions or exemptions from import duties of a large
number of articles and settlement of payments to be mado
directly between the two countries except for any
balance over five million yen which would be settled in
a foroign curroncy. A credit for a ycar was accorded to
Japan for payments for rice purchased from Indochina.
Six related documents were also signed which
provided for the establishment of Japanese schools in
Indochina, the opening up to Japanese of certain pro-
fessions formerly prohibited to foreigners, the pri-
vilege for Japanese nationals and interests to participato
in banking, conmercial, agricultural, mining and water

(2) Treatyof peace between France
and Thailand.
The treaty of peace between France and Thailand,
for which an agreement had been concluded on March 1I
(see political report for March) was formerly signed
on May 9 together with the Franco-Japan and the Thai-
Japan protocols. The only difference of any importance
between the final treaty and the previous agreement
related to the demilitarization of the ceued areas and
the establishment of a commission under Japanese control
for the execution of the terms of the treaty. In this
connection no provisicn was made for any demilitariza-
tion of a zone on the Indo-Chinese side of the new border
and instead of threo commissions only one temporary
mixed commission for the delimitation of the frontier
was provided for.*

Japan's southward advance, particularly with respect to
the Netherlands East Indies. Referring to the demand

* Embassy's telegram No. 627, May 1, 7 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 635, May 15, 10 p.m.

if Japan started militery operations they would likely
in

the remark that Japan should certainly take up the
challenge "if the Netherlands East Indies authorities
attempted to demonstrate hostility towards Japan by
deliberately misinterpreting Japan's true intentions."
III. INTERNAL POLITTCAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Political Parties.

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
During the latter part of April there appeared in
Japan a new political party named the "Tohokai", keadcd
by Mr. Seigo Nakano. On May 1 a meeting of the percy
was held in Tokyo and attended by a large gathering, The
Tohokai was the first political party to be organized
since the dissolution of political parties in the surmer
of 1940. The Government apparently had not endeavored to
obstruct its organization and, although it was still to
early for a clear indication to bo seen of future po-
litical trends in Japan, it appeared possible that after
the reorganization of the Imperial Rule Assistance Associa-
tion in April, whereby that organization was shorn of most
of its political power, the emergence of a separate politi-
cal party might be indicative of a return to the same po-
litical organization of the country under various parties
which prevailed before the "new structure" movement was
introduced by Prince Konoye last summer. From the member-
ship and manifesto issued by the Tohokai it was apparently
to be strongly nationalistic in character.*

President Roosevelt's adaress on May 27, the difficulty
of gauging American action regarding the European War,
gloomy outlook concerning Japan's foreign trade and
increased taxation as a measure to absorb the advancing
purchasing funds of the people.
Of the more important ordinances promulgated during
the month were the Machinery Management Law, the Foreign
Trade Control Ordinance and the Revised Foreign Exchange
Control Regulations. The Foreign Trade Control Ordi-
nance, which was to become effective on June 13, gave the
Government authority to order the importation and exporta-
tion of commodities or to restrict or prohibit such importa-
tion or exportation and to determine the disposal of the
articles imported. The Revised Foreign Exchange Control
Rogulations, to become effective June l, facilitated
remittances to the yen bloc but made still more difficult
remittancos to foroign countries.
Heavy frost in the mountaineous areas of Central Japan
during the early part of the month reportedly did a great

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
-31-
deal of damage to the first silk crop. It was announced
that the estimated sugar crop for the coming year would
represent a reduction of twenty-two per cent from the
average of that of the last four years and a drastio
reduction in consumption of sugar in Japan was fore-
cast. Estimates for the wheat crop this yeer also
showed a large reduction from that of last year and a
slight reduction from the average of the past few
years. At the beginning of the month it was announced
that the Government would purchase all wheat, barley
and rice produced in the country and undertake official
distribution of those grains.
The press carried reports throughout the month
regarding discussions being undertaken by the Govern-
ment concerning prices in Japan and on May 3l it was
announced that the price-stop system, based on the prices
prevailing on September 18, 1939, would be abolished as
of Octoberla According to pross accounts prices under
the now policy apparently would be set arbitrarily.
In determining the quotations, equipmont and production
officioncy of tho producors and transportation faci-
litios would be included amongst the factors to bo
takon into account, but tho most important consido-
ration was to bo that of fixing tho pricos at lovels
which would oncourago oxpansion of production.
It was announced on May 1 that arrangomonts had
finally bccn concludod for tho amalgamation of tho ports

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
of Tokyo and Yokohama and that for the first time the
port of Tokyo was to be opened to foreign commerce. It
was understood that Tokyo would take care of shipping
from "Manchoukuo", China and also coastal trade, while
Yokohama would handle shipping from foreign countries.
On May 3 the press announced the formulation of plans
for the establishment of the "Newspaper Federation". The
federation was to be organized as a public juridical
person and would undertake to make recommendations to
the Government concerning public opinion, to investigate
means for improving the editing and management of news-
papers and to fix and adjust allotments of newsprint and
other materials.

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Ambassador's conversations with the
Foredgn Minister.
On May 14 the Ambassador was received by the
Minister for Foreign Affairs for the first time since
the latter's return from Europe. After completing
the routine business Mr. Grew asked Mr. Matsuoka
whether he had any particular thoughts with regard
to the present general situation. The subsequent
remarks which the Minister then made and which he said
ho was addressing to Mr. Grew as an Amorican friend
and not as the American Ambassador* were bellicose
both in tone and in substanco. The main points of
tho Ministor's discourse were an expression of deep
concern lest the United States proceed to convoy
ships to Great Britain as such action would almost
certainly lead to war with Germany; an accusation
that to declare a neutrality zone in the Atlantic or
any other ocean was a breach of international law; a
statement that Hitler had shown "great patience and
generosity" in not declaring wer on the United States
in the face of America's supplying Great Britain with
war-materials; and a declaration that should Germany
sink American ships in the Atlantic and should the
United States then attack German submarines he would
regard this as an act of American aggression which
would call for deliberation as to the applicability
of Article 3 of the Tripartite Alliance, and he thought
there was no doubt that such deliberation would lead
to war between Japan and the United States. He said
that this issue therefore lies exclusively in the hands

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
of President Roosevelt. He added that the "manly,
decent and reasonable" thing for the United States to
do, in view of its present policy and actions, was to
declare war openly on Germany instead of engaging in
acts of war under cover of neutrality.
The Ambassador rebutted each of Mr. Matsuoka's
remarks in turn and he said that whether the Minister
was speaking to him as the Ambassador or not, he, as
an American citizen, emphatically resented Mr.
Matsuoka's charge that the United States was guilty
of unmanly, indecent and unreasonable conduct. The
Minister thereupon withdrew his remark. The Ambassador
pointed out the fallacy of Mr. Matsuoka's reasoning
with regard to the American alleged breach of interna-
tional law, spoke of the United States' consistent
policy concerning the freedom of the seas and argued
the full justification of such measures as the United
States might put into force in the face of Hitler's
continual treaches of international law. To Mr. Grew's
reference to Japan's action in attacking China and
ruthless bombing of undefended cities and populations
without a declaration of war Mr. Matsuoka merely
remarked that the situations were entirely different
and changed the subject.
The conversation then turned to Japan's policy
in connection with the Southward Advance.
The Ambassador observed that in spite of the Minister's
constant assurances of peaceful intentions other views
had been advanced here. Mr. Matsuoka stated that he
deplored the utterances from some Japanese sections
openly advocating the use of force in the prosecution

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of tho advance and ho declared that Prince Konoye and
he himself were determined to see that the advance
should be carried out only by peaceful measures, but
he added significantly, "unless circumstances render
this impossible." In the conversation which ensued
concerning this point the Ambassador stressed the grave
concern of the United States in the potentialities of
the situation particularly as they related to the

The Ambassador in the course of further conversa-

construct their relations on a firm basis. that al-
though cherishing the same hope he considered that the
whole future of the world and civilization now lies
in the hands of one single man, President Roosevelt.*

(c) Japan's Abrogation of the Fur Seal
Convention.
In reply to the American Government's note trans-
mitted by the Embassy to the Foreign Office on March
18, the Embassy received a formal communication from
the Foreign Minister dated May 9, stating that the
Japanese Government was unable to withdraw its notice
of abrogation of the Fur Seal Convention but confirming
that it was prepared to conclude a new convention on
the basis of the principles it had proposed at the
time the notice was given last October. The note
asserted that contrary to the contention of the American
Government, seals from Pribiloff Island did frequent
waters adfacent to Japan; it denied that fish and
other marine matter consumed by the seals was of little
commercial importance to Japan; and it advocated the
return to pelagic sealing in order to reduce the
herds to the figure of 850,000 originally proposed
by the Japanese Government.***

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1) Military Operations.**
There was a marked increase during May in Japa-
nese military activity in China both in the air and on
the ground. Four offensives were conducted; one in the
southern part of Shansi Province and the others north
Embassy's telegram No. 729, May 24, 3 p.m.
Embassy's telogram No. 751, May 29, 5 p.m.
**
Prepared with the assistance of the office of the
Military Attaché.

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.99)
-15~
of Hankow in Hupeh Province, at Chuki in Chekiang Pro-
vince and near Walahow in Kwangtung Province. There
were, besides, minor clearing operations elsewhere in
occupied territory.
The most importent cf these operations was the
campaign in Shensi Province. On May 7 Japanese forces,
estimated at about six divisions in strength, began a
large-scale offensive directed against some 300,000
Chinese troops concentrated in the south-western sec-
tion of the province along the Yellow River. The
Japanese scheme of maneuver was to encircle and cut
through the Chinese in the zone between the river and
the mountains to the north. By the end of the month
Japanese reports claimed that the Chinese forces had
been decimated and that the escaped remnants had flod
to the mountains for refuge. The press accounts of
this campaign were reminiscent of the reports of numerous
previous operations undertaken by the Japanese military
forces to clear Chinese troops out of Shansi Province.
(2) General Relations.
On May 14 the Japanese Ambassador to the Nanking
Government, Mr. Kumaturo Honda, returned to Tokyo for
the purpose of holding important discussions with the
Central Government authorities regarding Japanese
policy concerning China.
Prior to his arrival here Ambassador Honda, in
interviews given at Shanghai on the eve of his departure
from China and at Kyoto, propounded his views which he
said also were concurred in by the Japanese Army, Navy
end reign Officc officials in China, regarding the
present situatiou in China and regarding the Japanese

=== Page 257 === (confidence 1.00)
-16-
policy for the settlement of the China Incident. He
declared that the only course for the disposal of
the Incident lay in the strengthening and solidifica-
tion of the Nanking Government. He said that it was
foolish to consider negotiations with the Chungking
Government since Chiang Kai-shek would negotiate with
Japan only on the condition of the wholesale withdrawl
of the Japanese troops from China and that the Generalis-
simo, in view of the present international developments,
had no desire to discuss peace. He said that friction
between the Kuomintang and the Communists had been over-
estimated and that the recent American loans to the
Chinese National Government had reduced the adverse
effect of the conclusion of the Japan-Soviet Neutrality
Pact. He drew the conclusion that opportunity for
direct negotiations with the Chungking Government had
long past, especially since Japan had recognized the
Nanking National Government; that the Chinese National
Government would attempt to prolong the war with the
hope of being able to exploit the world situation to
its advantage; that no peace was possible by mediation
through a third nation; that the strengthening of the
Nanking Government was the only policy which could
offect the settloment of the China Incident; and that
Japan should carry out this policy by stabilizing the
Nanking Government and by bettering the livelihood of
the Chinese masses beyond that prevailing under the
jurisdiction of the Chungking Government to induce
them to give political support to the Nanking Gowernment.
He said that as the Nanking Government became more
powerful the matter of its absorbing the Chungking

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
-17-
Government might develop at a later time.
On the same date that Ambassador Honda gave his
interview at Shanghai, General Shiuroku Hata, Commander
in Chief of the Japanese forces in China, in an inter-
view given at Nanking, warned Japan against over-op-
timism and over-dependence upon the temporarily improved
diplomatic situation and stated thet military operations
would continue in China in an effort to deal the enemy
a "crushing blow". He said that "resistance of the
Chiang Kai-shek forces and the Chinese communist army
must be stamped out." *
Colonel Hayao Mabuchi spokesman for the Ministry of

secret of solving the China Affair was the use of mili-

resistance.**

(b) French Indochina.
(1) Conclusion of econonic agreement
between Japan and Fren:h Indochina.
On May 6 there were signed the Convention of the
Establishment of Navigation and the Agreoment Con-
corning Customs, Tariff,. Trade and Mothods of Payments,
marking the conclusion of the economic negotiations
between Japan and French Indochina. The Convention,
entered intfof period of five years, provided for
reciprocal most-favored nation treatment of nationals,
vessels and commercial and industrial enterprises. The
Agreement, to be effective until December 31, l943,
provided for the most-favored nation treatment of
imports into each country from the other with special
reductions or exemptions from import duties of a large
number of articles and settlement of payments to be mado
directly between the two countries except for any
balance over five million yon which would be settled in
a foroign curroncy. A credit for a ycar was accorded to
Japan for payments for rice purchasea from Indochina.
Six related documents were also signed which
provided for the establishment of Japanese schools in
Indochina, the opening up to Japanese of certain pro-
fessions formerly prohibited to foreigners, the pri-
vilege for Japanese nationals and interests to participato
in banking, conmercial, agricultural, mining and water

quantities of imports into Japan from Indochina for
the year l94l were specified for a number of the latter
country's products .*
The press greeted the announcement of the conclu-
sion of tho economic negotiations with much onthusiasm.
It asserted that the treaty had broken down the trade
barriers which had formerly givon France complete
oconomic monopoly of tho colony cnd that a further step
forward had been made in the execution of Japan's
program for e New Order and the establishment of the
Economic Co-Prosperity Sphere in Greater East Asia.
Predictions were freely made that trade in the future
between the two countries would increase by leaps and
bounds and that Japan would be able to secure from French
Indochina many urgently-required materials and products.
Later in the month the press reported that plans were
boing formulated for economic penetration on a large-
s cale into French Indochina. Several investigation
missions were to be sent to Indochina for the purpose of
surveying the possibility of mining, agricultural and
water-power projects. It was also announced that in
order to control Japan's trade, and particularly to
regulate comp tition amongst Japanese exporters engaged
in trade with tho colony, a state policy concern would
* Embassy's telegrams No. 645, May 6, 10 p.m.; No. 650,
May 7, 9 p.m. Dospatch No. 5583, May 8, 1941.

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.98)
-83-
be established under the directi on of the South Seas
Trade Association. Plans were also being discussed
for the establishment of a state policy conern similar
to the existing North and Central China Development
Company for the economic development of Indochina.
(2) Treatyof peace between France
and Thailand.
The treaty of peace between France and Thailand,
for which an agreement had been concluded on March 1I
(see political report for March) was formerly signed
on May 9 together with the Franco-Japan and the Thai-
Japan protocols. The only difference of any importance
between the final treaty and the previous agreement
related to the demilitarization of the ceded areas and
the establishment of a commission under Japanese control
for the execution of the terms of the treaty. In this
connection no provision was made for any demilitariza-
tion of a zone on the Indo-Chinese side of the new border
and instead of three co:missions only one temporary
mixed commission for the delimitation of the frontier
was provided for.*
(c) Netherlends East Indies.
On May 14 the Jepanese delegution at Batavia
presented to the Netherlands East Indies Government
a memorandum submitting Japan's "new proposals" for
an economic agreement between the twc countries. The
new demands, in sumnary, calle for the right of entry
into the Istherlands East Indias of et lecst 1, 600 Japa-
nese employees i: annum, the esteiishnent of Japanese
* Embassy's telegram No. 659, May 9. 9 p.m.
Embassy's despatch No. 6513, May 23, 1941.

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
commercial enterprises and business; exploration and
exploitation by Japanese companies of mineral resources;
increased fishery privileges and navigation in coast
waters, including the opening up of closed ports, future
establishment of direct air service between the two
countries and immediate connection between the Japanese
and Netherlands East Indies airlines at Bangkok or
Saigon; and increased trade between the two countries
including larger quotas for Japanese imports into the
Netherlands East Indies and greater supplies to Japan
of that country's products. It was understood that the
Japanese were particularly insistent on their demands
for rubber and tin. The Netherlands Indies authori-
ties, however, were opposed to permitting large quantities
of these products being exported to Japan for fear that
a part of them might be intended for re-export to Germany,
especially as Japan was known to be obtaining large
quantities of these products from French Indochina,
Thailand and other sources.*
During the month of May increasing Japanese attention
was given to the economic negotiations with the Nether-
lands East Indies and by the end of the period the nego-
tiations were accoraed a prominence in Japsnese foreign
relations second only to the question of the settlement
of the China Incident. The long drawn out affair was,
with the successful conclusion of agreements with Russia
and French Indochina, taken up as the salient issue on
the Japanese diplomatic agenda. Several statements were
made by the Deputy Spokesman of the Information Board
Embassy's despatch No. 5630, May 27, 1941.
Embassy's telegram No. 727, May 24, 1 p.m.

stressing the importance of the nogotiations. In

tentative promises mede last year to sell Japan certain
quantities of raw materials (of rubber and tin).
In the press comment the Netherlands East Indies
were warned against resisting the construction of the
New Order in East Asia and advised to give up their hope
for the victory of the Allies and reconsider their policy
of reliance upon Great Britain and the United States.
It was suggested in some of the editorials that a con-
tinued refusal of that country to associate itself with
the New Ordor "might stretoh Japan's patience too far"
and it was asserted that "a broak-down in the economic
negotiations would hinder Japan's endeavor to establish
the Co-Procperity Sphere by peaceful means and might
involve regrettable consequences."
The Embassy was informed that the strong tone of the
Japanese press was representative of intensified pressure
brought to bear by the Japanese Government in its nego-
tiations with the Netherlands Legation at Tokyo.*

(e) Germany.
The first meeting in Japan of the General Commission
established under the Tripartite Alliance was held on
May 9 under the chairmanship of the Foreign Minister and
attended by General Eugene Ott, German Ambassador and Mr.
Mario Indelli, the Italian Ambassador. The press reported
that questions including economic subjects, relating to
the cooperation between the three Axis Powers were
discussed.
The press during the month expressed, inter alia, the
view that Germany should concentrate upon the subjugation
of the British Isles in order to forestall effective
American assistance to Great Britain and, at the same time,
asserted that Germany should limit its sphere of activity
to the European continent and surrounding areas. In this
* Embassy's telegram No. 64l, May 5, 3 p.m.

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)

latter connection the position was taken by some of the
newspapers that althcugh up to the present German power
had not yet reached beyond Suez new deveicpments might
bring overwhelming German influence into the Far East and
Japan was warned that it should be on guard against such
an eventuality and should insist that it have the do-
minant role in the Orient.*
(r) Iran.
It was announced that on May 27 ratifications of the
treaty of amity and friendship negotiated last year were
formally exchanged at Tehran and that the treaty would come
into effect on June ll.
(g) General International Relations.
There has been mentioned in the foregoing section
of this report, Japanese Relations With the United States,
the remarks of the Minister for Foreign Affairs to the
Ambassador and Mr. Matsuoka's statement released on May
30 rogarding Jepenese policy towards the South Seas.
In an article written by Admiral Sankichi Takahashi,
retired, and published during the month under review,
some searching observations were presented concerning
Japan's southward advance, particularly with respect to
the Netherlands East Indies. Referring to the demand
voiced by some sections in Japan for an immediate occupa-
tion of the Netherlands East Indies he pointed out that
* Embassy's telegram No. 627, May ], 7 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 635, May 15, 10 p.m.

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
-28
if Japan started military operations they would likely
in
result-not just a local conflict but a gigantio interna-
tional war. He questioned whether it would be wise to
venture into another battle while the Sino-Japanese
hostilities were still continuing. He said that if such
a military campaign could be brought to a successful con-
clusion within a short time there was no doubt that a
settlement of the Sino-Japanese hostilities would be
greatly expedited thereby but that if Japan should be
caught in an endurance contest the conflict with China
could also become hopelessly entangled. He mentioned
that an attack upon the Netherlands East Indies might
very well defeat its very purpose as instead of resulting
in Japan's securing the materials it wanted from that
country Japan might find that the materials had been
destroyed before-hand to prevent their falling into Japa-
nese hands. He urged that Japan should first follow a
policy of attaining economic gains in order to meet its
much-needed requirements for raw materials. He asserted
the oft-repeated contention that Japan harbored no
territorial designs against the Netherlands East Indies
but was merely asking them to open their door to Japa-
nese trade and industries. He concluded, however, with
the remark that Japan should certainly take up the
challenge "if the Netherlands East Indies authorities
attempted to demonstrate hostility towards Japan by
deliberately misinterpreting Japan's true intentions."
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Political Parties.

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
During the latter part of April there appeared in
Jepan a new political party named the "Tohokai", headcd
by Mr. Seigo Nakano. On May 1 a meeting of the percy
was held in Tokyo and attended by a large gathering, The
Tohokai was the first political party to be organized
since the dissolution of political parties in the summer
of 1940. The Government apparently had not endeavored to
obstruct its organization and, although it was still to
early for a clear indication to bo seen of future po-
litical trends in Japan, it appeared possible that after
the reorganization of the Imperial Rule Assistance Associa-
tion in April, whereby that organization was shorn of most
of its political power, the emergence of a separate politi-
cal party might be indicative of a return to the same po-
litical organization of the country under various parties
which prevailed before the "new structure" movement was
introduced by Prince Konoye last summer. From the member-
ship and manifesto issued by the Tohokai it was apparently
to be strongly nationalistic in character.*

Nothing to report.

VI. GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
Business activities generally during May continued
to be ciroumscribed by adverse conditions. The uncertain
* Embassy's telegram No. 630, May 2, 5 p.m.

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
-30
international situation had a disccuraging affect end
much concern was felt over the drastic fcod situation,
over the difficulties in export trade anc in foreigr.
exchange transactions., over
the continued rise during
the month of commodity prices end living costs and over
continued shortage of shipping facilities. At the cane
time the Government's program for control and regula-
tion of national economy was implemented by further
measures making normal business operations more dif-
ficult and giving further priority to industries engaged
in munitions production. The stock market was goneral-
ly depressed durin g tho month due to apprchonsion over
Prosident Roosevelt's adaress on May 27, the difficulty
of gauging American action regarding the European War,
gloomy outlook concerning Japan's foreign trade and
increased taxation as a measure to absorb the advancing
purchasing funds of the people.
Of the more important ordinances promulgated during
the month were the Machinery Management Law, the Foreign
Trade Control Ordinance and the Revised Foreign Exchange
Control Regulations. The Foreign Trade Control Ordi-
nance, which was to become effective on June 13, gave the
Government authority to order the importation and exporta-
tion of commodities or to restrict or prohibit such importa-
tion or exportation and to determine the disposal of the
articles imported. The Revised Foreign Exchange Control
Rogulations, to become effective June l, facilitated
remittances to the yen bloc but made still more difficult
remittancos to foreign countries.
Heavy frost in the mountaineous areas of Central Japan
during the early part of the month reportedly did a great

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
-31-
deal of damage to the first silk crop. It was announced
that the estimated sugar crop for the coming year would
represent a reduction of twenty-two per cent from the
average of that of the last four years and a drastic
reduction in consumption of sugar in Japan was fore-
cast. Estimates for the wheat crop this year also
showed a large reduction from that of last year and a
slight reduction from the average of the past few
years. At the beginning of the month it was announced
that the Government would purchase all wheat, barley
and rice produced in the country and undertake official
distribution of those grains.
The press carried reports throughout the month
regarding discussions being undertaken by the Govern-
ment concerning prices in Japan and on May 3l it was
announced that the price-stop system, based on the prices
prevailing on September 18, 1939, would be abolished as
of Octoborla According to pross accounts prices under
the now policy apparently would bo set arbitrarily.
In determining the quotations, equipmont and production
officioncy of tho producors and transportation faci-
litios would bo included amongst the factors to bo
taken into account, but tho most important consido-
ration was to bo that of fixing tho pricos at lovols
which would oncourago oxpansion of production.
It was announced on May 1 that arrangomonts had
finally bcen concludod for tho amalgamation of tho ports

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
of Tokyo and Yokohama and that for the first time the
port of Tokyo was to be opened to foreign commerce. It
was understood that Tokyo would take care of shipping
from "Manchoukuo", China and also coastal trade, while
Yokohama would handle shipping from foreign countries.
On May 3 the press announced the formulation of plans
for the estcblishment of the "Newspaper Federation". The
federation was to be organized as a public juridical
person and would undertake to make recommendations to
the Government concerning public opinion, to investigate
means for improving the editing and management of news-
papers and to fix and adjust allotments of newsprint and
other materials.

(a) Russia.
A joint communique of the Japanese and Soviet Governments was
issued on June 12 announcing that legoti:tions had been completed
for a "commercial agreement" and a "trade and payment agreement"
between the two countries. The commercial greement was to be valid
for five years and provided for reciprocal most-favored-nation treat-
ment in regard to customs duties, restrictions on trade, et cetera.
The agreement concerning trade and payment was to be valid for one
year and provided for a total annual turnover of sixty million yen,
the Japanese exports to the value of thirty million yen to be ex-
changed for Sovict procucts of ecual value.**
The press was most cnthusiastic over the co.clusion or these
agreements. Although it was reco~nized th t the amount of trade in-
volved was small it was expected that Japan would obtain some need-
ed products such as petroleum and ranganese from the Soviet Union.
The agreements were said to be sicnificant not only as providing a
basis for an inerense of trade with Russic but also as paving the
way for
close economic collaboration with Gerlany and other
horthern and central Europeen countries while at the same time link-
in an economic relationship to the political onc clready estab-
lished by the Neutrality Pact between Japan cnd the Soviet Union.
No indicition was given in the press as to the attitude which
Japan would t ke with re ard to Russin following the outbreak of
var between Germany and Russic.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 8, 1941
Reference Tokyo's despatch no. 5812,
August 25, 1941, "Report of Conditions in
Japan During the Month of July, 1941".
Covered for the most part by telegrams.
I think you will wish to note the statement
on page 26 to the following effect:
8
... Unless the restrictions on
Japanese foreign trade to the
countries mentioned are lifted,
Japan's industrial, economic
and financial structure will be
very substantially weakened through
the inability to maintain those
foreign commercial relations upon
which her prosperity depends."

L RELATTONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Military Occupation of Southern French
Indoohina.
On the morning of July 25 the new Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, received for the first time
the diplomatic chiefs of mission. In his conversation with
the Ambassador, Admiral Toyoda statcd tnat he wculd like to
see Mr. Grew that evening to have a longer talk with him.
When the Ambassador called at 9:30 that night Admiral Toyoda
informed the Ambassador that the next day the Japanese Govern-
ment would issue an official announcement of an agreement en-
tered into on July 2l between the Japanese Government and the
French Government at Vichy concerning the "joint protection"
of French Indochina but that in view of the concern of the
United States he wished to explain to the Ambassador in
advance the reasons and intentions of such movement. The
document whioh he then handed to the Ambassador stated that
the agreement had been reached for the common interest of
both Japan and France in order to insure the safety of
Frenoh Indochina and to prevent the disturbance of peace in
that area under the existing conflicting international situa-
tion". It further stated that the Japanese Government wil!
strictly observe all agreements that exist between Japan
and France and especially those relating to the integrity of
territory and the respect for sovereignty and to refrain from
interfering in the domestic affairs of French Indochina, and
eiso that Japan has no intention at all of making the southern
part of Frenoh Indochina a base of armed advancement against
acjoining areas. It then stated that the Japanese Govern-
ent had oser. receiving numerous reports that the United
states miint iopt more stringent anti-Japanese economic
measures as rsinliatory steps against the Japanese action
concerning

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
conoerning French Indochina and it advised that if such
measures should be realized "it would be difficult to prevent
agitation of antagonism amcng the Japanese people against
aid to the Chiang Kai-shek Regime and the encirclament cam
paign against Jepan." It further explained that the acticu
taken by Japan was "unavoidable from a broad view point of
maintaining peace in the Pacific by preventing a more serious
aggravation of the feelings of the Japanese people, which
are s teaadily being excited by such facts as the unsatis-
factory result of the Netherlands East Indies-Japanese nego-
tiations and the almost daily reports from abroad about the
tightening of the encirclement campeign against Japan." It
conoluded with the expression of hope that the door would
be kept open for further efforts directed towards amicable
solution of American-Japanese relations.
The Ambassador after reading the document stated that
he could best reply to certain points contained therein by
conveying to the Minister the s tatement issued to the press
by the Acting Secretary of State on July 24 and he read
portions of that statement which he had picked up on the
radio that day. In further emphasizing and elaborating certain
/made
pertinent points/by Mr. Welles the Ambassador spoke of the
utter fallacy of the charge of "encirclement" and of the con-
ception that Japan was acting for the "safety and protection
of Indochina." Mr. Grew said that he was glad to take note
of the phrase that "Japan had no intention at all of meking
the southern part of Indochina a base of armed advancement
?gainst adjoining aroas" but that the American Government,
tnd he nimself, had met with so many bitter disappointsments
during sna Tast several years in the illusory natura of
pã omiacs

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
promises and assurances conveyed to him by former Japanese
Ministers that the United States was now unavoidably obliged
to rely on facts and aotions rather than upon words. Mr. Grew
spoke in detail of the successive steps of the Japanese south-
ward advance culminating for the present in the proposed
occupation of bases in Southern French Indochina and the
consequent endangering of the safety of other areas in the
Pacific,including the Philippine Islands, and he elaborated
the practicable and logical reasons for the American concern
in the safety of Singapore and tho Netherlands East Indies
which were definitely threatened by this advance. The
Minister did not try to refute the Ambassador's argument
except to repeat the peaceful intentions of the proposed
Japanese move into Indochina and to take issue with the use
of the term "occupation" in connection therewith since Japan
had no territorial ambitions. The Ambassador replied that
the term "occupation" connoted no question of time.*
In the afternoon papors of the following day the Japa-
nese press published the Government's official announcement
of the agreoment for "joint defense" of French Indochina. At
the same time the press reported the text of the executive
order issued by the President on July 25 freezing Japanese
assets in the United States and thereby bringing all
financial, import and export trade transactions with Japa-
nese interests under Government control. The Japanese press
also carried the text of the President's talk to the Volunteer
Participation Committee at the White Hous e on July 24 in
which the President spoke of the United States' past Pacific

On the morning of July 28 regulations were put into force
in Japan restricting transactions of American nationals here.
It was announced by the Finance Ministry that the regulations
had been resorted to as a counter measure to the invocation
of the freezing order by the United States Government.
The
announcement of the Finance Ministry added that the regula-
tions would be applied with as much elasticity as possible in
order to minimize the effects on economic relations between
Japan and the United States.
The Japanese press had on July 24 published despatches
under a Washington date-line of the day before reporting that
the United States Government had determined, "if ovents
in the Far East necessitated action", to take economic
measures against Japan which would likely be in the form of
freezing Japanese capital in the United States, embargoing of
oil exports to Japan, suspension of gold purchases from Japan
or possibly even a total economic blockade against Japan. It
was also reported that Great Britain would fully cooperate
with the United States in these measures.*
The press in other ways for somo days prior to the

which the American Governmont considerod to be not only a

the intantion of tho Govorrment of the United States to
movo it from its prerent positior. It was noted that the

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
Conourrent with and forming a part of the general agi-
tation over the so-called "ABCD encirclement" of Japan (See
section under General International Relations) the Japanese
press during the month under review engaged in a concerted
campaign egainst real or presumed British activities in no
Orient as being dosigned to bring pressure to bear on Japan
and to frustrate Japan's aims in south-eastern Asia. On
July 13, the press carried a report to the effect that Great
Britain and the Chinese National Government were believed to
be negotiating a militery allience which was eimed at Jepa-
nese southward advance and which would become operative when
the Jepanese drive started. Later in the month it wes reported
thet this military allience provided for: the despatch of
runitions to Chungking from Burma, the establishment of a
cargo transportation base at Singapore; aircraft assembly
plants
mcssy's despatch No. 5740, July 23, 1941.
2. *
Erucssy's despatch No. 5747, July 28, 1941.
Trassy's despatch No. 578, July 30, 1941.
 Fulsssy's telegram No. 1l28, July 30, 7 p.m.

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
-10
plants in India, Burma and at Singapore; mobilization of
jand
Chinese residents in the South Seas, simultaneous to a
British declaration of war, the despatch of Chinese troops
southward to fight under British command.
At the same time Britain was accused of endeavoring
to interfere in the affairs of French Indochina by encoura-
ging the French in the southern part of the colony to oppose
Japan, and, through economic pressure, of endeavoring to
gain control of the resources of French Indochina. On July
22, a report was published that an understanding had been
reached between the British Government, tho Chungking Govern-
ment and the De Gaulle Regime under which Chinese foroes
were preparing to invade French Indochina. Britain was said
to be preparing to transport native troops to southern Indo-
china.
Through both economic pressure and military threats
Britain was charged with intimidating Thailand in an effort
to alienate that country from the New Order in East Asia.
The press was particularly vociferous in denouncing alleged
British actions.. in this last respect. It was stated that
since the relaxation of the Near East situation additional
troops had been brought to Burma and Malaya and more airplanes
based in Burma, Malaya end B orneo for the purpose of threaten-
ing Thailand. Through an oil agreement and other economic
arrangements with Thailand, Great Britain was said to be
trying to force that country economically to rely solely
upon it and to interfere with Japan's legitimate trade with
Thailand. Diplomatically, Great Britain was also accused of
using every opportunity to create a rift between Thailand and
Japen.
As

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
As evidence of the military strength that Britain was
building up in its south-eastern Asiatic colonies it was
reported that military forces of 30o,000 men and a thousand
fighting planes had been assembled in Malaya, Burma and at
Singapore. A Domei despatch later reported that these
figures represented mere British boasting end that British
military forces in Malaya and Burma would only reach some
60,Oo0 troops of mixed nationality and limited fighting abi-
lity and that Britain only had a few effective military planes
available in those areas.
From the angle of its possible effect on the situation
in the Far East the Japanese press also viewed with some
concern the conclusion of the British-Soviet military agree-

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
On July 28 the Netherlands East Indies followed the action
taken by the United States and Great Britain and froze Japa-
nese assets in the Archipelago. The Director of the Economic
Affairs Bureau of the Indies Government announced that iry
the discontinuance of foreign exchange transactions between
Japan and the Notherlands East Indies and that all the Indies'
exports to Japan would be subject to special Government
license. The day before notice was given by the Indies Govern-
ment of the suspension of the Japanese-Netherlands East Indies
monetary agreement which had come into force on January 1.
The oil agreements between the Netherlands Oil Companies and
Japanese interests wore raportedly not cancelled but it was
understood that the freezing regulations made these: in effect
inoperative.
The Japanese press was much more bitter against the Nether-
lands East Indies for the action taken by it than against
Britain and the United States. Its action was considered
particularly grave on the grounds that Japan considered the
East Indies as an integral part of the Co-Prosperity Sphere
and that its measures amounted to a direct clash with Japa-
nese policy and should be regarded in a political rather
than economic light. Certain sections of the press went so
far as to state that the measures revealed regrettable hosti-
lity toward Japan and that the entry of Japanese troops into
French Indochina should serve as a warning to the Dutch to
reconsider their attitude for their own sake. Obviously
government

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
government inspired articles, however, sought to tone down
the press reaction and to suggest that trade relations be-
tween Japan and the Notherlands East Indies might be maintained
through arrangements between the two Governments.*
(e) Soviet Russia.
Japan's position with respect to the Soviet Union
continued to remain obscure during the month. On July 15
the Foreign Office Spokesman was reported to have stated
to foreign correspondents that Japan would observe the con-
ditions of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact "for the
present."* It was in fact generally indicated that Japan's
policy towards Russia "for the prosent" was one of "wait and
see". The Foreign Minister in an interviow with the British
Ambassador at the beginning of the month stated that the
Tripartite Pact imposed no obligation on Japan to enter
the war on Germany's side but that similarly Japan would not
b e prevented by the Neutrality Pact from taking any action
arising from the German-Soviet war which the preservation
of the Japanese interests might demand". He stated that
thus Japan's liberty of action in respect to the situation
created by the war remained unaffected whether by the
Tripartite Pact or the Neutrality Pact (See Section Below -
General International Relations)**.
The Japanese press carried full accounts emanating from
both sides of the German-Soviet hostilities but ongagod in
littlo commont or prediction.***

(g) General International Relations and Japanese
Foreign Policy.
Culminating the series of deliberations and conferences
in Japanose Governmental circles held for ten days from June
22, the dey of the outbreak of the Germen-Soviet hostilities,
en Imperial Conference was held on July 2. Following the
contereice the Government issued the laconic statement that
"the fundamental national policy to be taken toward the present
situation was decided". Another statement was then issued
by

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
by the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka, in which
he reiterated the Government's statemont and said that "the
German-Soviet war can not bo regardod simply as an armed clash
between the two countries"; that it was "necessary for Japan
to watch with close attention the development of conditions
directly affecting this situation and to maintain the firmest
resolution and the most careful preparation" and that he
believed that "a super-emergency period is developing in the
Far East which will directly affect Japan". What the policy
was and what decisions were made were kept a well-guarded
secret.*
As indicated by the statement of the Foreign Minister
that "the German-Soviet war can not be regarded simply as an
armed clash between the two countries" Japan's own policy was
considered to be affected by the German attack on Russia and
the Imperial Conference therefore doubtlessly concerned in
part if not in whole the new situation created by the new
devolopment in Gormany's military activities and the formu-
lation of a policy and adoption of decisions in the light
of the new situation.
It will be recalled that one of the announced purposes
for Japan's joining the Axis Alliance was the maintenance of
peace and that this same consideration, probably together
with the hope of establishing close cooperation or oven a bloo
between Germany, Italy, Russia and Japan, were motives for
the conclusion of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact. With
this previous foundation for Japan's alliance with the Axis
removed a new examination of the relationship between the
basic interests between Hitler's New Order and the Japanese

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.99)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Military Occupation of Southern Fronch
Indoohina.
On the morning of July 25 the new Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, received for the first time
the diplomatic chiefs of mission. In his conversation with
the Ambassador, Admiral Toyoda stated that he wculd like to
see Mr. Grew that evening to have a longer talk with him.
When the Ambassador called at 9:30 that night Admiral Toyoda
informed the Ambassador that the next day the Japanese Govern-
ment would issue an official announcement of an agreement en-
tered into on July 2l between the Japanese Government and the
Fronch Government at Vichy concerning the "joint protection"
of French Indochina but that in view of the concern of the
United States he wished to explain to the Ambassador in
advance the reasons and intentions of such movement. The
dooument whioh he then hended to the Ambassador stated that
the agreement had been reached for the common interest of
both Japan and France in order to insure the safety of
French Indochina and to prevent the disturbance of peace in
that are'a under the existing conflicting international situa-
tion". It further stated that the Japanese Government will
strictly observe all agreements that exist between Japan
and France and especially those relating to the integrity of
territory and the respect for sovereignty and to refrain from
i.terfering in the domestic affairs of French Indochina, and
elso that Japan has no intention at all of making the southern
part of French Indochina a base of armed advancement against
eujoining areas". It then stated that the Japanese Govern-
ient had been receiving numerous reports that the Unite
staces might sdopt more stringent anti-Japanese economic
measures as retaliatory steps against the Japanese action
ooncerning

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
concerning French Indochina and it advised that if such
measures should be realized "it would be difficult to prevent
agitation of antagonism among the Japanese people against
aid to the Chiang Kai-shek Regime and the encirclement cem-
paign against Jepan." It further explained that the acticu
taken by Japan was "unavoidable from a broad view point of
maintaining peace in the Pacific by preventing a more oricus
aggravation of the feelings of the Japanese people, which
are s tesadily being excited by such facts as the unsatis-
factory result of the Netherlands East Indies-Japanese nego-
tiations and the almost daily reports from abroad about the
tightening of the encirclement campeign against Japan." It
concluded with the expression of hope that the door would
be kept open for further efforts directed towards amicable
solution of American-Japanese relations.
The Ambassador after reading the document stated that
he could best reply to certain points contained therein by
conveying to the Minister the s tatement issued to the press
by the Acting Secretary of State on July 24 and he read
portions of that statement which he had picked up on the
radio that day. In further emphasizing and elaborating certain
/made
pertinent points/by Mr. Welles the Ambassador spoke of the
utter fallacy of the charge of "encirclement" and of the con-

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
Concurrent with and forming a part of the general agi-
tation over the so-called "ABCD encirclement" of Japan (See
section under General International Relations) the Japanese
press during the month under review engaged in a concerted
campaign against real or presumed British activities in the
Orient as being designed to bring pressure to bear on Japan
and to frustrate Japan's aims in south-eastern Asia. On
July 13, the press carried a report to the effect that Great
Britain and the Chinese National Government were believed to
be negotiating a militery allience which was aimed at Jepa-
nese southward advance and which wpuld become operative when
the Jepanese drive started. Leter in the month it wes reported
thet this military allience provided for: the despatch of
munitions to Chungking from Burma, the establishment of a
cargo transportation base at Singapore; aircraft assembly

At the same time Britain was accused of endeavoring
to interfere in the affairs of French Indochina by encoura-
ging the French in the southern part of the colony to oppose
Japan, and, through economic pressure, of endeavoring to
gain control of the resources of French Indochina. On July
22, a report was published that an understanding had been
reached between the British Government, tho Chungking Govern-
ment and the De Gaulle Regime under which Chinese forces
were preparing to invade French Indochina. Britain was said
to be preparing to transport native troops to southern Indo-
china.
Through both economic pressure and military threats
Britain was charged with intimidating Thailand in an effort
to alienate that country from the New Order in East Asia.
The press was particularly vociferous in denouncing alleged
British actions.. in this last respect. It was stated that
since the relaxation of the Near East situation additional
troops had been brought to Burma and Malaya and more airplanes
based in Burma, Malaya end B orneo for the purpose of threaten-
ing Thailand. Through an oil agreement and other economic
arrangements with Thailand, Great Britain was said to be
trying to force that country economically to rely solely
upon it and to interfere with Japan's legitimate trade with
Thailand. Diplomatically, Great Britain was also accused of
using every opportunity to create a rift botween Thailand and
Japen.

concern the conclusion of the British-Soviet military agree-
ment announced on July 13. The fear was expressed that Great
Britain might also attempt to draw Russia into the movement
for encirclement of Japan.*

Indochina and Thailand. At the time he emphasized the serious-
ness of the situation which would arise in that case.

(b) China.
On July 1 Germany, Italy, Hungary, Bulgaria, Spain,
Rumania, Slovakia and Croatia extended formal recognition to
the Wang Ching-wei Regime at Nanking.
On the occasion of the celebration of the fourth anni-
versary on July 7 of the beginning of the China Hostilities
the Japanese press devotod much spaco to articles extolling
the successes of the Japanese military forces in China and the
progress made in reconstructing the occupied areas in China.
In a tabulation of the results of the military campaigns
during the four years Chinese losses were computed at 3,800,000
killed, wounded or taken prisoner, with 2,Ol5,000 killed.
Japanese losses were announced as l09,250 killed. The articles
emphasized the present weakness of the Chungking Government
and the developing strength of the Nanking Regime. While
military operations progressed it was claimed that Japan had
beon steadily accumulating fighting and defense resources
behind the front and had greatly enlarged its productive
capacity with the result that Japan is much stronger and

(c) French Indochina.
On July 5 ratifications were exchanged at Tokyo between
France and Thailand, between Japan and France, and between
Japan and Thailand of the Treaty of Peace between France and
Thailand and the Protocols concerning the guarantee of the
political understanding between Japan and France and Japan
and Thailand that were signed on May 9. On the same date
ratifications were exchanged between Japan and France of the
commercial treaties between those two countries signed on
May 6.*
Several days before these treaties were finally ratified
a report was bruited in foreign circles in Japan that the
Government was about to make demands on French Indochina to
permit Japanese military penetration into southern Indochina.
The report persisted and was givon increasing currency.**
A build-up for the pending aggressive move appeared in
the Japanese press in the form of a campaign designed to show
on the one hand that French Indochina was being enciroled and
threatened by third powers, particularly Great Britain and
China, and, on the other, that the designs of third powers on
Indochina menaced Japan's national security. There were re-

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
On July 28 the Netherlnnds East Indies followed the action
taken by the United States and Great Britain and froze Japa-
nese assets in the Archipelago. The Director of the Economic
Affairs Bureau of the Indies Government announced that day
the discontinuance of foreign exchange transactions between
Japan and the Netherlands East Indies and that all the Indies'
exports to Japan would be subject to special Government
license. The day before notice was given by the Indies Govern-
ment of the suspension of the Japanese-Netherlands East Indies
monetary agreement which had come into force on January 1.
The oil agreements between the Netherlands Oil Companies and
Japanese interests wore reportedly not cancelled but it was
understood that the freezing regulations made these: in effect
inoperative.
The Japanese press was much more bitter against the Nether-
lands East Indies for the action taken by it than against
Britain and the United States. Its action was considered
particularly grave on the grounds that Japan considered the
East Indies as an integral part of the Co-Prosperity Sphere
and that its measures amounted to a direct clash with Japa-
nese policy and should be regarded in a political rather
than economic light. Certain sections of the press went so
far as to state that the measures revealed regrettable hosti-
lity toward Japan and that the entry of Japanese troops into
French Indochina should serve as a warning to the Dutch to
reconsider their attitude for their own sake. Obviously
government

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
government inspired articles, however, sought to tone down
the press reaction and to suggest that trade relations be-
tween Japan and the Netherlands East Indies might be maintained
through arrangements between the two Governments.*
(e) Soviet Russia.
Japan's position with respect to the Soviet Union
continued to remain obscure during the month. On July 15
the Foreign Office Spokesman was reported to have stated
to foreign correspondents that Japan would observe the con-
ditions of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact "for the
present."* It was in fact generally indicated that Japan's
policy towards Russia "for the prosent" was one of "wait and
see". The Foreign Minister in an interviow with the British
Ambassador at the beginning of the month stated that the
Tripartite Pact imposed no obligation on Japan to enter
the war on Germany's side but that similarly Japan would not
b e prevented by the Neutrality Pact from taking any action
arising from the German-Soviet war which the preservation
of the Japanese interests might demand". He stated that
thus Japan's liberty of action in respect to the situation
created by the war remained unaffected whether by the
Tripartite Pact or the Neutrality Pact (See Section Below -
General International Relations)**.
The Japanese press carried full accounts emanating from
both sides of the German-Soviet hostilities but ongaged in
little comment or prediction.***
