(g) General International Relations and Japanese
Foreign Policy.
Culminating the series of deliberations and conferences
in Jepanese Governmental circles held for ten deys from June
22, the day of the outbreak of the German-Soviet hostilities,.
en Imperial Conference was held on July 2. Following the
conterense the Government issted the laconic statement that
"the fundamental national policy to be taken toward the present
situatiol. was decided". Another statement was then issued
by

=== Page 407 === (confidence 1.00)
-20-
by the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka, in which
he reiterated the Government's statement and said that "the
German-Soviet war can not bo regardod simply as an armed clash
between the two countries"; that it was "necessary for Japan
to watch with close attention the development of conditions
directly affecting this situation and to maintain the firmest
resolution and the most careful preparalion" and that he
believed that "a super-emergency period is developing in the
Far East which will directly affect Japan". What the policy
was and what decisions were made were kept a well-guarded
secret.*
As indicated by the statement of the Foreign Minister
that "the German-Soviet war can not be regarded simply as an
armed clash between the two countries Japan's own policy was
considered to be affected by the German attack on Russia and
the Imperial Conference therefore doubtlessly concerned in
part if not in whole the new situation created by the new
development in Gormany's military activities and the formu-
lation of a policy and adoption of decisions in the light
of the new situation.
It will be recalled that one of the announced purposes
for Japan's Joining the Axis Alliance was the maintenance of
peace and that this same consideration, probably together
with the hope of establishing close cooperation or oven a bloc
between Germany, Italy, Russia and Japan, were motives for
the conclusion of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact. With
this previous foundation for Japan's alliance with the Axis
removed a new examination of the relationship between the
basic interests between Hitler's New Order and the Japanese
New
* Embassy's telegram No. 920, July 2, 3 p.m.

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
New Order was demanded. Before the Imperial Conference was
held the press pointed out the doubtfulness of Japan's present
treaty commitments, the fact that no full recognition of the
Co-Prosperity Sphere as one solid system dominated by Japan
had been recognized by Germany, the possibility that German
influence would now be extended beyond the Urals and into
Asiatic Russia, Outer Mongolia and possibly even North and
West China and the danger even that the European New Order
would embraoo tho Asiatic colonies of certain European
countries. Besides this possible conflict of basic interests
of Japan and Germany there was at the same time the undoubted
realization, despite the fact that all hope may not have been
given up for an iwprovement of relations with the United States,
that Japan's present policy in the Pacific could hardly permit
of a reconciliation between Japan and America and the other
democracies, assuming that Japan's policy is "immutable".
On July 1 Princc Konoye, in a broadoast to the local
councils of the Imperial Rule Assistanco Association, made
the statement to the effect that Japan must depend solely
upon her own strength. This declaration was given much
importance in the editorials commenting upon the Imperial
Conference. The editorials stressed the following further
points: Japan must follow a policy based on its own national
interests, the HOCHI stating in this respect that Japan could
no longer count on benefits from its Axis allies and had
no way to overcome its present crisis except through de-
pendence upon the Empire itself; Japan's principal policies
are the settlement of the China Incident and the construc-
tion of a Co-Prosperity Sphere, with respect to which point
the MrYAkO expressed the opinion that certain powers threatened
Asiatio

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.99)
-22-
Asiatic peace and blocked Jepan's southward advance and
that their influences must be eradicated even through force
if necessary; and Japan's internal structure must be soli-
dified, in which respect the KOKUMIN stated that there never
has been a time when complete unity of the nation and un-
wavering determination were more urgently demanded.*
In the light of the Promier's statoment and the con-
siderations affecting Japanese foreign relations mentioned
by the press it secmed reasonable to presume that the policy
decided at the Imperial Conference had a good deal to do with
the determination of how Japan independently could further
her own best interosts. The Japanese military occupation of
Frenoh Indochina was soen to be one decision taken undor this
policy.**
Shortly after the beginning of the month mobilization
of troops and military proparations wore begun in Japan on a
vast scale. Resorvists and conscripts estimated betwoen
one and two million men were called up throughout the country.
Military supplies of all kinds, including large numbers of
horses brought in from the country, were assembled. Sailings
of ships to the American continent and to other distant points
were cancelled and the ships placed on near sea runs. Restric-
tions were enforced on travel in various parts of Japan. Strict
censorship of mails and communication was carried out and
conditions approaching those applicable in a national emergency
were imposed in the entire Japanese Empire. By the middle of

The agitation regarding the so-called ABCD encirclement
of Japan, first brought to the fore in the latter part of
June, emerged into a vigorous press campaign throughout the
month under review. A three-fold objective was seen in this
encirclement mo vement: to bring economic pressure to bear
upon Japan, to create bases and lines of military defense
around Japan and around areas under Japanese influence, in-
cluding French Indochina and Thailand, and to obstruct Japan's
execution of its immutable policy of the Southward Advance.**

(a) Cabinet.
On July 16 the Konoye Cabinet resigned. The reason
given for this action, as announced in a statement by tho
Board of Information, was that there was need of perfecting

Only two days later Prince Fumimaro Konoye was summoned
by the Emperor to form a new Cabinet and on July 19 formal
investiture of the Cabinet wes held. The new Cabinet wes
constituted as follows: Premier, Prince Fumimaro Konoye;
Foreign Minister, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda; Home Ministor,
Harumichi Tanabe; Finance Minister, Masatune Ogura; War
Minister, Lioutonant Goneral Hidoki Tojo; N avy Ministor,
Admiral Koshiro Oikawa; Education Minister, Dr. Kunihiko
Hashida; Agriculturo and Forostry Minister, Hiroya Ino;
Commerco and Industry Ministor, Vice Admiral Masazo Sakonji;
Combined Communications and Railways Minister, Shozo Murata;
Overseas Minister, Adniral Teijiro Toyoda; Welfare Minister,
Surgeon Lieutenant General Dr. Chikohiko Koizumi; and two
Ministers without portfolio, Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma and
Lieutenant General Hesuke Yanagawa. On July 25 Mr. Michiyo
Iwamura was appointed Justice Minister.
Five changes were made but with the exception of Mr.
Yosuke Matsuoka the new Cabinet, as regards the important
figures in it was concerned, was practically identicel to that
of the previous Ministry. The press in fact stated that the
Cabinet change was to be considered as a "reconstruction"
rather than a "formation" of a new Cabinet and it was openly
stated that the Cabinet change had been undertaken for the
purpose of replacing Mr. Matsuoka. The reasons for the elimi-
nation of Mr. Matsuoka from the Government were not publicly
exprossod b ut undoubtedly they related to the policy for
whioh

which he was largoly responsible with rospect to Japan's

intornational situation brought about by the German-Soviet
war which altored the basis for Japen's own foreign policy
and requirod the selecting of new porsonnel to carry out a
newly formulated policy.*
In the press comments regarding the composition of
the Third Konoye Cabinet specific mention was made of the
fact that there was a predominance of military servicemen
through whom it was expected that the cooperation of the
military services with the Government would be further
strengthened.
It was expected that there would be complete harmony
between the three central figures of the Cabinet, the Prime
Minister, Admiral Toyoda, Baron Hiranuma, the last of whom
was described as being actually the Vice Premier.** At the
same time the press stressed the fact as announced by the
Prime Minister and tho new Foreign Minister that Japan's
basic policy would remain unchanged under the now Government.

in the United States; Great Britain and the Notherlands East
Indies was not brought out by the end of July but there was
no reason to doubt that it came as a very heavy blow to
Japanese industry and prosonted Japaneso business intorests
with the most discouraging outlook they had over faced.
Unless the restrictions on Japanese foreign trade to the
countries mentioned are lifted, Japan's industrial, economic
and financial structure will be very substantially weakened
through the inability to maintain those foreign commercial
relations upon which her prosperity depends. Besides the
disruption of the trade with the United States, Great Britain
and the Netherlands East Indies the action of the United
States and the withdrawal of most of the Japanese ships
from trade routes to South America brought Japanese trade
with Latin Amorica practically to a standstill. Those four
areas had accounted for almost all of Japanese foreign trade
outsido of the Yen Bloc as because of the Europcan hostilities
trade with that continent had almost ontirely coased. What
compensation Japen could obtain in developing her trade
with China, French Indochina and Thailand could only in
small measure make up for her trade losses with the other
regions. As evidence of the feelings of commercial and
financial interests in the matter, the stock market upon
the receipt of the news of the American and British orders
broke very sharply and would possibly have dropped much further
had it not been for the Government intervening to support the
market.
Roports of tho agricultural situation in Japan indicated
a rathu. poor preliminary outlook for this year's harvest
Juu primarily to excessive rains in Japan during the Spring and
thrcughout July. Crops roportedly were extonsively damaged
orer wide areas and the damago undoubtedly would rosult in tho
curtailment

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.97)

curtailment of the production of rice, the Autumn silk crop,
vegetables and potatoes. The Agriculture Minister, in a
statoment made on July 2l, admitted that the rice crop was
likely to be less than that of last year and of the yeer before
that. Those losses he advised would be made up by increased
output of other foods and importation of rice from abroad.
One encouraging foature of tho situation was, however, the
prospect of large rice crops in Korea and Formosa, both of
which were expected to show appreciable increases over those
of the previous two years. Estimates for the barley, rye
and wheat crops for the present year showed a decline from
those of last year but sizeable increases over the average
production of the past five years. The Formosa sugar crop
for the year 1940-l94l was reported to have decreased by 20%
from the previous yoar with the result that in order to make
up the necessary supplies of sugar for the country contracts
wore concluded for the importation of a iarge amount of this
commodity from Java during July, August and September.
On July l2 it was announced that plans had been formu-
lated for a new fin.mil and monetary policy. This policy
was based on the principle of the mobilization and distribu-
tion of national funds in a systematic manner. It aimed at
providing funds for national production of essential materials
cy ffecting a fundamental reorganization of finance and
oor:ing, and by projecting some new measures and consolida-

Bank would exert almost complete control over business and

mobilization of funds. On July 7 there came into effect the
new regulations regarding Japan's foreign trade which placed
a large number of commodities to foreign countries outside
of the Yen Bloc under a license system. Such goods as
cotton yarn, cotton goods, rayon and other important articles
were included in these categories. The purpose of the
regulations was to equalize exports with imports from non-Yen
Bloc countries. Pending tho preparations of the schedules
exports of all commodities wero suspended with the result
that little export trade was conducted by Japan during the
month under review.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Military Occupation of Southern French
Indochina.
On the morning of July 25 the new Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, received for the first time
the diplomatic chiefs of mission. In his conversation with
the Ambassador, Admiral Toyoda stated that he wculd like to
see Mr. Grew that evening to have a longer talk with him.
When the Ambassador called at 9:30 that night Admiral Toyoda
informed the Ambassador that the next day the Japanese Govern-
ment would issue an official announcement of an agreement en-
tered into on July 2l between the Japanese Government and the
French Government at Vichy concerning the "joint protection"
of French Indochina but that in view of the concern of the
United States he wished to explain to the Ambassador in
advance the reasons and intentions of such movement. The
document which he then handed to the Ambassador stated that
the agreement had been reached for the common interest of
both Japan and France in order to insure the safety of
French Indochina and to prevent the disturbance of peace in
that area under the existing conflicting international situa-
tion". It further stated that the Japanese Government will
strictly observe all agreements that exist between Japan
and France and especially those relating to the integrity of
territory and the respect for sovereignty and to refrain from
interfering in the domestic affairs of French Indochina, and
also that Japan has no intention at all of making the southern
part of French Indochina a base of armed advancement against
aujoining areas". It then stated that the Japanese Govern-
nent had been receiving numerous reports that the United
tates might iopt more stringent anti-Japanese economic
measures as reinliatory steps against the Japanese action
ooncerning

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
concerning French Indochina and it advised that if such
measures should be realized "it would be difficult to prevent
agitation of antagonism among the Japanese peonle againsr
aid to the Chiang Kai-shek Regime and the encirclanent cam-
paign against Jepan." It further explained that the acticn
taken by Japan was "unavoidable from a broad view point of
maintaining peace in the Pacific by preventing a more serious
aggravation of the feelings of the Japanese people, which
are s tenadily being excited by such facts as the unsatis-
factory result of the Netherlands East Indies-Japanese nego-
tiations and the almost daily reports from abroad about the
tightening of the encirclement campeign against Japan." It
concluded with the expression of hope that the door would
be kept open for further efforts directed towards amicable
solution of American-Japanese relations.
The Ambassador after reading the document stated that
he could best reply to certain points contained therein by
conveying to the Minister the s tatement issued to the press
by the Acting Secretary of State on July 24 and he read
portions of that statement which he had picked up on the
redio that day. In further emphasizing and elaborating certain
/made
pertinent points/by Mr. Welles the Ambassador spoke of the
utter fallacy of the charge of "encirclement" and of the con-
ception that Japan was acting for the "safety and protection
of Indochina." Mr. Grew said that he was glad to take note
of the phrase that "Japan had no intention at all of meking
the southern part of Indochina a base of armed advancement
zainst adjoining aroas" but that the American Government,
tnd he nimself, had met with so many bitter disappointsments
durire n rast several years in the illusory natura of
pr omisce

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
promises and assurances conveyed to him by former Japanese
Ministers that the United States was now unavoidably obliged
to rely on facts and actions rather than upon words. Mr. Grew
spoke in detail of the successive steps of the Japanese south-
ward advance culminating for the present in the proposed
occupation of bases in Southern French Indochina and the
consequent endangering of the safety of other areas in the
Pacific,including the Philippine Islands, and he elaborated
the practicable and logical reasons for the American concern
in the safety of Singapore and tho Nethorlands East Indies
which were definitely threatened by this advance. The
Minister did not try to refute the Ambassador's argument
except to repeat the peaceful intentions of the proposed
Japanese move into Indochina and to take issue with the use
of the term "occupation" in connection therewith since Japan
had no territorial embitions. The Ambassador replied that
the term "occupation" connoted no question of time.*
In the afternoon papors of the following day the Japa-
nese press published the Government's official announcement
of the agrooment for "joint defense" of French Indochina. At
the same time the press reported the text of the executive
order issued by the President on July 25 freezing Japanese
assets in the United States and thereby bringing all
financial, import and export trade transactions with Japa-
nese interests under Government control. The Japanese press
also carried the text of the President's talk to the Volunteer
Participation Committee at the White Hous e on July 24 in
which the President spoke of the United States' past Pacifio

On the morning of July 28 regulations were put into force
in Japan restricting transactions of American nationals here.
It was announced by the Finance Ministry that the regulations
had been resorted to as a counter measure to the invocation
of the freezing order by the United States Government.
The
announcement of the Finance Ministry added that the regula-
tions would be applied with as much elasticity as possible in
order to minimize the effects on economic relations between
Japan and the United States.
The Japanese press had on July 24 published despatches
under a Washington date-line of the day before reporting that
the United States Government had determined, "if ovents
in the Fa East necessitated action", to take economic
measures against Japan which would likely be in the form of
freezing Japanese capital in the United States, embargoing of
oil exports to Japan, suspension of gold purchases fronm Japan
or possibly even a total economic blockade against Japan. It
was also reported that Great Britain would fully cooperate
with the United States in these measures.*

powers was openly, although guardedly, mentioned.*

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
Conourrent with and forming a part of the general agi-
tation over the so-called "ABCD encirclement" of Japan (See
section under General International Relations) the Japanese
press during the month under review engaged in a concerted
campaign against real or presumed British activities in the
Orient as being designed to bring pressure to bear on Japan
and to frustrate Japan's aims in south-eastern Asia. On
July 13, the press carried a report to the effect that Great
Britain and the Chinese National Government were believed to
be negotiating a military allience which was eimed at Jepa-
nese southward advance and which would become operative when
the Jepanese drive sterted. Leter in the month it wes reported
thet this military alliance provided for: the despatch of
munitions to Chungking from Burma, the establishment of a
cargo transportation base at Singapore; aircraft assembly
plants
mocssy's despatch No. 5740, July 23, 1941.
Emucssy's despatch No. 5747, July 28, 1941.

plants in India, Burma and at Singapore; mobilization of
jand,
Chinese residents in the South Seas,' simultaneous to a
British declaration of war, the despatch of Chinese troops
southward to fight under British command.
At the same time Britain was accused of endeavoring
to interfere in the affairs of French Indochina by encoura-
ging the French in the southern part of the colony to oppose
Japan, and, through economic pressure, of endeavoring to
gain control of the resources of French Indochina. On July
22, a report was published that an understanding had been
reached between the British Government, tho Chungking Govern-
ment and the De Gaulle Regime under which Chinese forces
were preparing to invade French Indochina. Britain was said
to be preparing to transport native troops to southern Indo-
china.
Through both economic pressure and military threats
Britain was charged with intimidating Thailand in an effort
to alienate that country from the New Order in East Asia.
The press was particularly vociferous in denouncing alleged
British actions.. in this last respect. It was stated that
since the relaxation of the Near East situation additional
troops had been brought to Burma and Malaya and more airplanes
based in Burma, Malaya en: B orneo for the purpose of threaten-
ing Thailand. Through an oil agreement and other economic
arrangements with Thailand, Great Britain was said to be
trying to force that country economically to rely solely
upon it and to interfere with Japan's legitimate trade with
Thailand. Diplomatically, Great Britain was also accused of
using every opportunity to create a rift between Thailand and
Japen.
As

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
As evidence of the military strength that Britain was
building up in its south-eastern Asiatic colonies it was
reported that military forces of 300,000 men and a thousand
fighting planes had been assembled in Malaya, Burma and at
Singapore. A Domei despatch later reported that these
figures represented mere British boasting and that British
military forces in Malaya and Burma would only reach some
60,0o0 troops of mixed nationality and limited fighting abi-
lity and that Britain only had a few effective military planes
available in those areas.
From the angle of its possible effect on the situation
in the Far East the Japanese press also viewed with some
concern the conclusion of the British-Soviet military agree-
ment announced on July 13. The fear was expressed that Great
Britain might also attempt to draw Russia into the movement
for encirclement of Japan.*
In an approach to the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
made in the first part of the month the British Ambassador
inquired as to the accuracy of the current report that Japan
contemplated the acquisition of naval and air bases in south
Indochina and Thailand. At the time he emphasized the serious-
ness of the situation which would arise in that case.
On the evening of July 25 the Foreign Minister presented
to the British Ambassador a document, similar in contents to
that presented to Mr. Grew on the same evening, giving advance
notice of the Japanese-French agreement for "joint defense" of
French Indochina. The following day the British Ambassador
handed

(b) China.
On July 1 Germany, Italy, Hungary, Bulgaria, Spain,
Rumania, Slovakia and Croatia extended formal recognition to
the Wang Ching-wei Regime at Nanking.
On the occasion of the celebration of the fourth anni-
versary on July 7 of the beginning of the China Hostilities
the Japanese press dovoted much space to articles extolling
the successes of the Japanese military forces in China and the
progress made in reconstructing the occupied areas in China.
In a tabulation of the results of the military campaigns
during the four years Chinese losses were computed at 3,800,000
killed, wounded or taken prisoner, with 2,Ol5,000 killed.
Japanese losses were announced as l09,250 killed. The articles
emphasized the present weakness of the Chungking Government
and the developing strength of the Nanking Regime. While
military operations progressed it was claimed that Japan had
beon steadily accumulating fighting and defonse resources
behind the front and had greatly enlarged its productive
capacity with the result that Japan is much stronger and

of coal three to four times as much as they were before the

increases of supplies to Japan than to the actual increases
now.*

(c) French Indochina.
On July 5 ratifications were exchanged at Tokyo between
France and Thailand, between Japan and France, and between
Japan and Thailand of the Treaty of Peace between France and
Thailand and the Protocols concerning the guarantee of the
political understanding between Japan and France and Japan
and Thailand that were signed on May 9. On the same date
ratificstions were exchanged between Japan and France of the
commercial treaties between those two countries signed on
May 6.*
Several days before these treaties were finally ratified
a report was bruited in foreign circles in Japan that the
Government was about to make demands on French Indochina to
permit Japanese military ponetration into southern Indochina.
The report persisted and was givon increasing currency.**

on the one hand that French Indochina was being enciroled and
threatened by third powers, particularly Great Britain and
China, and, on the other, that the designs of third powers on
Indochina menaced Japan's netional security. There were re-

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
On July 28 the Netherlands East Indies followed the action
taken by the United States and Great Britain and froze Japa-
nese assets in the Archipelago. The Director of the Economic
Affairs Bureau of the Indies Government announced that day
the discontinuance of foreign exchange transactions between
Japan and the Netherlands East Indies and that all the Indies'
exports to Japan would be subject to special Government
license. The day before notice was given by the Indies Govern-
ment of the suspension of the Japanese-Netherlands East Indies
monetary agreement which had come into force on January l.
The oil agreements between the Netherlands Oil Companies and
Japanese interests wore reportedly not cancelled but it was
understood that the freezing regulations made these: in effect
inoperative.
The Japanese press was much more bitter against the Nether-
lands East Indies for the action taken by it than against
Britain and the United States. Its action was considered
particularly grave on the grounds that Japan considered the
East Indies as an integral part of the Co-Prosperity Sphere
and that its measures amounted to a direct clash with Japa-
nese policy and should be rogarded in a political rather
than economic light. Certain sections of the press went so
far as to state that the measures revealed regrettable hosti-
lity toward Japan and that the entry of Japanese troops into
French Indochina should serve as a warning to the Dutch to
reconsider their attitude for their own sake. Obviously
government

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
government inspired articles, however, sought to tone down
the press reaction and to suggest that trade relations be-
tween Japan and the Netherlands East Indies might be maintained
through arrangements between the two Governments.*
(e) Soviet Russia.
Japan's position with respect to the Soviet Union
continued to remain obscure during the month. On July 15
the Foreign Office Spokesman was reported to have stated
to foreign correspondents that Japan would observe the con-
ditions of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact "for the
present."* It was in fact generally indicated that Japan's
policy towards Russia "for the prosent" was ono of "wait and
see". The Foreign Minister in an interviow with the British
Ambassador at the beginning of the month stated that the
Tripartite Pact imposed no obligation on Japan to enter
the war on Germany's side but that similarly Japan would not
b e prevented by the Neutrality Pact from taking any action
arising from the German-Soviet war which the preservation
of the Japanese interests might demand". He stated that
thus Japan's liberty of action in respect to the situation
created by the war remained unaffected whether by the
Tripartite Pact or the Neutrality Pact (See Section Below -
General International Relations)**.
The Japanese press carried full accounts emanating from
both sides of the German-Soviet hostilities but ongaged in
littlo commont or prediction.***

the new embargo was undoubtedly designed against this country.
It was reported that the Japaneso Government had lodged a

(g) General International Relations and Japanese
Foreign Policy.
Culminating the series of deliberations and conferences
in Japanese Governmental circles held for ten days from June
22, the day of the outbreak of the German-Soviet hostilities,
en Imperiel Jonference was held on July 2. Following the
conterece the Government issted the laconic statement thet
"the fundamental national policy to be taken toward the present
situation was decided". Another statement was then issued
by

=== Page 452 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
by the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka, in which
he reiterated the Government's statement and said that "the
German-Soviet war can not bo regarded simply as an armed clash
between the two countries"; that it was "necessary for Japan
to watch with close attention the development of conditions
directly affecting this situation and to maintain the firmest
resolution and the most careful preparation" and that he
believed that "a super-emergency period is developing in the
Far East which will directly affect Japan". What the policy
was and what decisions were made were kept a well-guarded
secret.*
As indicated by the statement of the Foreign Minister
that "the German-Soviet war can not be regarded simply as an
armed clash between the two countries" Japan's own poli y wos
considered to be affected by the German attack on Russia and
the Imperial Conference therefore doubtlessly concerned in
part if not in whole the new situation created by the new
development in Gormany's military activities and the formu-
lation of a policy and adoption of decisions in the light
of the new situation.
It will be recalled that one of the announced purposes
for Japan's joining the Axis Alliance was the maintenance of
peace and that this same consideration, probably together
with the hope of establishing close cooperation or oven a bloc
between Germany, Italy, Russia and Japan, were motives for
the conclusion of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact. With
this previous foundation for Japan's alliance with the Axis
removed a new examination of the relationship between the
basic interests between Hitler's New Order and the Japanese
New
* Embassy's telegram No. 920, July 2, 3 p.m.

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
New Order was demanded. Before the Imperial Conference was
held the press pointed out the doubtfulness of Japan's present
treaty commitments, the fact that no full recognition of the
Co-Prosperity Sphere as one solid system dominated by Japan
had been recognized by Germany, the possibility that German
influence would now be extended beyond the Urals and into
Asiatic Russia, Outer Mongolia and possibly even North and
West China and the danger even that the European New Oruur
would embrace tho Asiatic colonies of certain European
countries. Besides this possible conflict of basic interests
of Japan and Germany there was at the same time the undoubted
realization, despite the fact that all hope may not have been
given up for an iwprovement of relations with the United States,
that Japan's present policy in the Pacific could hardly permit
of a reconciliation between Japan and America and the other
democracies, assuming that Japan's policy is "immutable".
On July 1 Prince Konoye, in a broadcast to the local
councils of the Imperial Rule Assistance Association, made
the statement to the effect that Japan must depend solely
upon her own strength. This declaration was given much
importance in the editorials commenting upon the Imperial
Conference. The editorials stressed the following further
points: Japan must follow a policy based on its own national
interests, the HoCHI stating in this respect that Japan could
no longar count on benefits from its Axis allies and had
no way to overcome its present crisis except through do-
pendence upon the Empire itself; Japan's principal policies
are the settlement of the China Incident and the construo-
tion of a Co-Prosperity Sphere, with respect to which point
the MIYAko expressed the opinion that certain powers threatened
Asiatic

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.99)
-22-
Asiatic peace and blocked Jepan's southward advance and
that their influences must be eradicated even through force
if necessary; and Japan's internal structure must be soli-
dified, in which respect the KOkUMIN stated that there never
has been a time when complete unity of the nation and un-
wavering determination were more urgently demanded.*
In the light of the Promier's statoment and the con-
siderations affecting Japanese foreign relations mentioned
by the press it seomed reasonable to presume that the policy
decided at the Imperial Conference had a good deal to do with
the determination of how Japan independently could further
her own best interosts. The Japanese military occupation of
Frenoh Indochina was seen to be one decision taken under this
policy.**
Shortly after the beginning of the month mobilization
of troops and military preparations wore begun in Japan on 
vast scale. Resorvists and conscripts estimated betwoen
one and two million men were called up throughout the country.
Military supplies of all kinds, including large numbers of
horses brought in from the country, were assembled. Sailings
of ships to the American continent and to other distant points
were cancelled and the ships placed on near sea runs. Restric-
tions were enforced on travel in various partsof Japan. Strict
censorship of mails and communication was carried out and
conditions approaching those applicable in a national emergenoy
were imposed in the entire Japanese Empirs. By the middle of
the

The agitation regarding the so-called ABCD encirclement
of Japan, first brought to the fore in the latter part of
June, emerged into a vigorous press campaign throughout the
month under review. A three-fold objective was seen in this
encirclement mo vement: to bring economic pressure to bear
upon Japan, to create bases and lines of military defense
around Japan and around areas under Japanese influence, in-
cluding French Indochina and Thailand, and to obstruct Japan's
execution of its immutable policy of the Southward Advance.**

Five changes were made but with the exception of Mr.
Yosuke Matsuoka the new Cabinet, as regards the important
figures in it was concerned, was practically identical to that
of the previous Ministry. The press in fact stated thet the
Cabinet change was to be considered as a "reconstruction"

In the press comments regarding the composition of

Prime Minister and tho new Foreign Minister that Japan's
basic policy would remain unchanged under the now Government.

Unless the restrictions on Japanese foreign trade to the
countries mentioned are lifted, Japan's industrial, economic
and financial structure will be very substantially weakened
through the inability to maintain those foreign commercial
relations upon which her prosperity depends. Besides the
disruption of the trade with the United St&tes, Great Britain
and the Netherlands East Indies the action of the United
States and the withdrawal of most of the Japanese ships
from trade routes to South America brought Japanese trade
with Latin Amorica practically to a standstill. Those four
areas had accounted for almost all of Japanese foreign trade
outsido of the Yen Bloc as because of the European hostilitios
trade with that continent had almost ontirely ceased. What
componsation Japen could obtain in devoloping her trade
with China, French Indochina and Thailand could only in
small measure make up for her trade losses with the other
regions. As evidence of the feelings of commercial and
financial interests in the matter, the stock market upon
the receipt of the news of the American and British orders
broke very sharply and would possibly have dropped much further
hed it not been for the Government intervening to support the
market.

curtailment of the production of rice, the Autumn silk crop,

likely to be less than that of last year and of the yeer before
that. Those losses he advised would be made up by increased
output of other foods and importation of rice from abroad.
One encoureging foature of tho situation was, however, the
prospect of large rice crops in Korea and Formosa, both of
which were expected to show appreciable increases over those
of the previous two years. Estimates for the barley, rye
and wheat crops for the present year showed a decline from
those of last year but sizeable increases over the average
production of the past five years. The Formosa sugar crop
for the year 1940-194l was reported to have decreased by 20%
from the previous year with the result that in order to make
up the necessary supplies of sugar for the country contracts
wore concluded for the importation of a large amount of this
commodity from Java during July, August and September.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Military Occupation of Southern French
Indoohina.
On the morning of July 25 the new Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, received for the first time
the diplomatic chiefs of mission. In his conversation with
the Ambassador, Admiral Toyoda stated that he wculd like to
see Mr. Grew that evening to have a longer talk with him.
When the Ambassador called at 9:30 that night Admiral Toyoda
informed the Ambassador that the next day the Japanese Govern-
ment would issue an official announcement of an agreement en-
tered into on July 21 between the Japanese Government and the
French Government at Vichy concerning tho "joint protection"
of French Indochina but that in view of the concern of the
United States he wished to explain to the Ambassador in
advance the reasons and intentions of such movement. The
document which he then handed to the Ambassador stated that
the agreement had been reached for the common interest of
both Japan and France in order to insure the safety of
French Indochina and to prevent the disturbance of peace in
that area under the existing conflicting international situa-
tion". It further stated that the Japanese Government will
strictly observe all agreements that exist between Japan
and France and especially those relating to the integrity of
territory and the respect for sovereignty and to refrain from
interfering in the domestic affairs of French Indochina, and
also that Japan has no intention at all of making the southern
part of French Indochina a base of armed advancement against
aujoining areas. It then stated that the Japanese Govorn-
ient had been receiving numerous reports that the United
tates might aiopt more stringent anti-Japanese economic
meesures as retnliatory steps against the Japanese action
ooncerning

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
concerning French Indochina and it advised that if such
measures should be realized "it would be difficult to prevent
agitation of antagonism amcng the Japanese pecple againsn
aid to the Chieng Kai-shek Regime and the encirclenent cam-
paign against Jepan." It further explained that the acticu
taken by Japan was "unavoidable from a broad view point of
maintaining peace in the Pacific by preventing a more serious
aggravation of the feelings of the Japanese people, which
are s tesadily being excited by such facts as the unsatis-
factory result of the Netherlands East Indies-Japanese nego-
tiations and the almost dcily reports from abrocd about the
tightening of the encirclement campeign against Japan." It
concluded with the expression of hope that the door would
be kept open for further offorts directed towards amicable
solution of American-Japanese relations.
The Ambassador after reading the document stated that
he could best reply to certain points contained therein by
conveying to the Minister the s tatement issued to the press
by the Acting Secretary of State on July 24 end he read
portions of that statement which he had picked up on the
radio that day. In further emphasizing and elaborating certain
/made
pertinent points/by Mr. Welles the Ambassador spoke of the
utter fallacy of the charge of "encirclement" and of the con-
ception that Japan was acting for the "safety and protection
of Indochina." Mr. Grew said that he was glad to take note
of the phrase that "Japan had no intention at all of meking
tha southern part of Indochina a base of armed advancement
egainst adjoining aroas" but that the American Government,
End he nimself, had met with so many bitter disappointsments
during tn? past several years in the illusory natura c
promisce

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
promises and assurances conveyed to him by former Japanese
Ministers that the United States was now unavoidably obliged
to rely on facts and actions rather than upon words. Mr. Grew
spoke in detail of the successive steps of the Japanese south-
ward advance culminating for the present in the proposed
occupation of bases in Southern French Indochina and the
consequent endangering of the safety of other areas in the
Paciric,including the Philippine Islands, and he elaborated
the practicable and logical reasons for the American concern
in the safety of Singapore and tho Netherlands East Indies
which were definitely threatened by this advance. The
Minister did not try to refute the Ambassador's argument
except to repeat the peaceful intentions of the proposed
Japanese move into Indochina and to take issue with the use
of the term "occupation" in connection therewith since Japan
had no territorial ambitions. The Ambassador replied that
the term "occupation" connoted no question of time.*
In the afternoon papors of the following day the Japa-
nese press published the Government's official announcement
of the agreoment for "joint defonse" of French Indochina. At
the same time the press reported the text of the executive
order issued by the President on July 25 freezing Japanese
assets in the United States and thereby bringing all
financial, import and export trade transactions with Japa-
nese interests under Government control. The Japanese press
also carried the text of the President's talk to the Volunteer
Participation Committee at the White Hous e on July 24 in
which the President spoke of the United States' past Pacific
policy
Embassy's telegram No. 1082, July 25, 8 p.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 1088, July 28, 2 a.m.
Embassy's telegram No. 1089, Jul 28, 3 a.m.

=== Page 481 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
policy regarding the supplying of American oil to Japan. T
press, however, only referred to the Acting Secretary of State's
statement but did not publish the statement itself.
On the morning of July 28 regulations were put into force
in Japan restricting transactions of American nationals here.
It was announced by the Finance Ministry that the regulations
had been resorted to as a counter measure to the invocation
of the freezing order by the United States Government.
The
announcement of the Finance Ministry added that the regula-
tions would be applied with as much elasticity as possible in
order to minimize the effects on economic relations between
Japan and the United States.
The Japanese press had on July 24 published despatches
under a Washington date-line of the day before reporting that
the United States Government had determined, "if events
in the Far East necessitated action", to take economic
measures against Japan which would likely be in the form of
freezing Japanese capital in the United States, embargoing of
oil exports to Japan, suspension of gold purchases from Japan
or possibly even a total economic blockade against Japan. It
was also reported that Great Britain would fully cooperate
with the United States in these measures.*
The press in other ways for some days prior to the
announcement of "joint defense" agreement indicated anticipa-
tion of the fact that an approaching development in Japanese
foreien relations wculd likely have important repercussions
cn nese-Amejco relations and played up the hope that
thation nct be too seriously impaired therevy.

tho situation that had arisen in the relations between tho
two countries still not fully clarified by the end of the

the editorials generally ventured the hope that the respective
ordinanoes in the United States and Japan would be so applied
as to alleviate harmful effect on Japanese-American economic
/te
relations. It was said that the scope of/American freezing

velopments and the nation wes urged to be neither optimistic
nor pessimistic. The press then protested thet Japan wo ld
not itself take the offensive but thgt the United States
should realize thnt any attempts to coerce Jopan by economic
pressure would merely harden Jcpen's resolutions to rursue
its policy and also thst Japen was fully prepered to neet
every American action quid pro quo.

had been anticipated Japanese holdings in the United States
had been previously greatly reduced and that furthermore, since
American-Japanese trade had already been extensively cur-
tailed by increased American export control, the effect
upon the Japanese economy would be comparatively slight and
adequate preparations were made in advance to cope with the
development.
Despite this general attitude of calm and quiet reserve
there were not absent some notes of marked apprehension and
even desperation over the prospects of Japan's future foreign
diplomatic end economic relations. The possibility of the
grave effect on Japanese trade with practically all countries
outside of the yen bloc and the severe strain on Japanese
diplomatic relations with the United Stetes and the Allied
powers was openly, although guardedly, mentioned.*

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
Concurrent with and forming a part of the general agi-
tation over the so-called "ABCD encirclement" of Japan (See
section under General International Relations) the Japanese
press during the month under review engaged in a concerted
campaign against real or presumed British activities in the
Orient as being designed to bring pressure to bear on Japan
and to frustrate Japan's aims in south-eastern Asia. On
July 13, the press carried a report to the effect that Great
Britain and the Chinese National Government were believed to
be negotiating a militery allience which was eimed at Jepa-
nese southward advance and which would become operative when
the Jepanese drive stcrted. Leter in the month it wes reported
thet this military allience provided for: the despatch of
runitions to Chungking from Burma, the establishment of a
cargo transportation base at Singapore; aircraft assembly

Through both economic pressure and military threats
Britain was charged with intimidating Thailand in an effort

The press was particularly vociferous in denouncing alleged

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
On July 28 the Netherlands East Indies followed the action
taken by the United States and Great Britain and froze Japa-
nese assets in the Archipelago. The Director of the Economic
Affairs Bureau of the Indies Government announced that day
the discontinuance of foreign exchange transactions between
Japan and the Netherlands East Indies and that all the Indies'
exports to Japan would be subject to special Government
license. The day before notice was given by the Indies Govern-
ment of the suspension of the Japanese-Netherlands East Indies
monetary agreement which had come into force on January 1.
The oil agreements between the Netherlands Oil Companies and
Japanese interests wore reportedly not cancelled but it was
understood that the freezing regulations made these: in effect
inoperative.
The Japanese press was much more bitter against the Nether-
lands East Indies for the action taken by it than against
Britain and the United States. Its action was considered
particularly grave on the grounds that Japan considered the
East Indies as an integral part of the Co-Prosperity Sphere
and that its measures amounted to a direct clash with Japa-
nese policy and should be regarded in a political rather
than economic light. Certain sections of the press went so
far as to state that the measures revealed regrettable hosti-
lity toward Japan and that the entry of Japanese troops into
French Indochina should serve as a warning to the Dutch to
reconsider their attitude for their own sake. Obviously
government

=== Page 495 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
government inspired articles, however, sought to tone down
the press reaction and to suggest that trade relations be-
tween Japan and the Notherlands East Indies might be maintained
through arrangements between the two Governments.*
(e) Soviet Russia.
Japan's position with respect to the Soviet Union
continued to remain obscure during the month. On July 15
the Foreign Office Spokesman was reported to have stated
to foreign correspondents that Japan would observe the con-
ditions of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact "for the
present."* It was in fact generally indicated that Japan's
policy towards Russia "for the prosent" was ono of "wait and
see". The Foreign Minister in an interviow with the British
Ambassador at the beginning of tho month stated that the
Tripartite Pact imposed no obligation on Japan to enter
the war on Germany's side but that similarly Japan would not
b e prevented by the Neutrality Pact from taking any action
arising from the German-Soviet war which the preservation
of the Japanese interests might demand". He stated that
thus Japan's liberty of action in respect to the situation
created by the war remained unaffected whether by the
Tripartite Pact or the Neutrality Pact (See Section Below -
General International Relations)**.
The Japanese press carried full accounts emanating from
both sides of the German-Soviet hostilities but ongaged in
littlo commont or prediction.***

(g) General International Relations and Japanese
Foreign Policy.
Culminating the series of deliberations and conferences
in Japanese Governmental circles held for ten days from June
22, the day of the outbreak of the German-Soviet hostilities,
en Imperiel Conference was held on July 2. Following the
contcrence the Goverrment issted the laconic statement that
"the fundamental national policy to be taken toward the present
situation was decided". Another statement was then issued
by

=== Page 497 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
by the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka, in which
he reiterated the Government's statement and said that "the
German-Soviet war can not bo regardod simply as an armed clash
between the two countries"; that it was "necessary for Japan
to watch with close attention the development of conditions
directly affecting this situation and to maintain the firmest
resolution and the most careful preparation" and that he
believed that "a super-emergency period is developing in the
Far East which will directly affect Japan". What the policy
was and what decisions were made were kept a well-guarded
secret.*
As indicated by the statement of the Foreign Minister
that "the German-Soviet war can not be regarded simply as an
armed clash between the two countries" Japan's own policy vas
considered to be affected by the German attack on Russia and
the Imperial Conference therefore doubtlessly concerned in
part if not in whole the new situation created by the new
development in Gormany's military activities and the formu-
lation of a policy and adoption of decisions in the light
of the new situation.
It will be recalled that one of the announced purposes
for Japan's joining the Axis Alliance was the maintenance of
peace and that this same consideration, probably together
with the hope of establishing close cooperation or oven a bloc
between Germany, Italy, Russia and Japan, were motives for
the conclusion of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact. With
this previous foundation for Japan's alliance with the Axis
removed a new examination of the relationship between the
basic interests between Hitler's New Order and the Japanese
New
* Embassy's telegram No. 920, July 2, 3 p.m.

=== Page 498 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
New Order was demanded. Before the Imperial Conference was
held the press pointed out the doubtfulness of Japan's present
treaty commitments, the fact that no full recognition of the
Co-Prosperity Sphere as one solid system dominated by Japan
had been recognized by Germany, the possibility that German
influence would now be extended beyond the Urals and into
Asiatic Russia, Outer Mongolia and possibly even North and
West China and the danger even that the European New Order
would embrace the Asiatic colonies of certain Europoan
countries. Besides this possible conflict of basic interests
of Japan and Germany there was at the same time the undoubted
realization, despite the fact that all hope may not have been
given up for an iwprovement of relations with the United States,
that Japan's present policy in the Pacific could hardly permit
of a reconciliation between Japan and America and the other
democracies, assuming that Japan's policy is "immutable".
On July 1 Princo Konoye, in a broadcast to the local
councils of the Imperial Rule Assistanco Association, made
the statement to the effect that Japan must depend solely
upon her own strength. This declaration was given much
importance in the editorials commenting upon the Imperial
Conference. The editorials stressed the following further
points: Japan must follow a policy based on its own national
interests, the HOcHI stating in this respect that Japan could
no longar count on benefita from its Axis allies and had

pendence upon the Empire itself; Japan's principal policies
are the settlement of the China Incident and the construo-

tho MIYAko expressed the opinion that certain powers threatened
Asiatio

Asiatic peace and blocked Jepan's southward advance and

if necessary; and Japan's internal structure must be soli-
dified, in which respect the KOKUMIN stated that there never

wavering determination were more urgently demanded.*
In the light of the Promier's statoment and the con-
siderations affecting Japanese foreign relations mentioned
by the press it secmed reasonable to presume that the policy
decided at the Imperial Conference had a good deal to do with
the determination of how Japan independently could further
her own best interosts. The Japanese military occupation of
French Indochina was seen to be one decision taken undor this
policy.**
Shortly aftor the beginning of the month mobilization
of troops and military preparations were begun in Japan on a
vast scale. Resorvists and conscripts estimated betwoen
one and two million men were called up throughout the country.
Military supplies of all kinds, including large numbers of
horses brought in from the country, were assembled. Sailings
of ships to the American continent and to other distant points
were cancelled and the ships placed on near sea runs. Restric-
tions were enforced on travel in various parts of Japan. Strict
censorship of mails and communication was carried out and
conditions approaching those applicable in a national emergency
were imposed in the entire Japanese Empire. By the middle of

cluding French Indochina and Thailand, and to obstruct Japan's
execution of its immutable policy of the Southward Advance.**
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Cabinet.
On July 16 the Konoye Cabinet resigned. The reason
given for this action, as announced in a statement by tho
Board of Information, was that there was need of perfecting

Only two days later Prince Fumimaro Konoye was summoned
by the Emperor to form a new Cabinet and on July 19 formal
investiture of the Cabinet wes held. The new Cabinet was
constituted as follows: Premier, Prince Fumimaro Konoye;
Foreign Minister, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda; Home Ministor,
Harumichi Tanabe; Finance Minister, Masatune Ogura; War
Minister, Lioutenant General Hidoki Tojo; N avy Ministor,
Admiral Koshiro Oikawa; Education Minister, Dr. Kunihiko
Hashida; Agriculturo and Forestry Minister, Hiroya Ino;
Commerco and Industry Minister, Vice Admiral Masazo Sakonji;
Combinod Communications and Railways Minister, Shozo Murata;
Overseas Minister, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda; Welfare Minister,
Surgeon Lieutenant General Dr. Chikohiko Koizumi; and two
Ministers without portfolio, Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma and
Lieutenant General Hesuke Yanagawa. On July 25 Mr. Michiyo
Iwamura was appointed Justice Minister.
Five changes were made but with the exception of Mr.
Yosuke Matsuoka the new Cabinet, as regards the important
figures in it was concerned, was practically identical to that
of the previous Ministry. The press in fact stated that the
Cabinet change was to be considered as a "reconstruction"
rather than a "formation" of a new Cabinet and it was openly
stated that the Cabinet change had been undertaken for the
purpose of replacing Mr. Matsuoka. The reasons for the elimi-
nation of Mr. Matsuoka from the Government were not publicly
exprossed b ut undoubtedly they rolated to the policy for
whioh
* Embassy's telogram No. 1016, July 17, 1 p.m.

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.99)
-25-
which he was largoly responsible with rospect to Japan's
association with Germany and Russia and the changes in the
intornational situation brought about by tho German-Soviet
war which altored the besis for Japan's own foreign policy
and required the selecting of new porsonnel to carry out a
newly formulated policy.*
In the press comments regarding the composition of
the Third Konoye Cabinet specific mention was made of the
fact that there was a predominance of military servicemen
through whom it was expected that the cooperation of the
military services with the Government would be further
strengthened.
It was expected that there would be complete harmony
between the three central figures of the Cabinet, the Prime
Minister, Admiral Toyoda, Baron Hiranuma, the last of whom
was described as being actually the Vice Premier.** At the
same time the press stressed the fact as announced by the
Prime Minister and tho new Foreign Minister that Japan's
basic policy would remain unchanged under the now Government.

in the United States, Great Britain and the Notherlands East
Indies was not brought out by the end of July but there was
no reason to doubt that it came as a very heavy blow to
Japanese industry end prosonted Japeneso business intorests
with the most discouraging outlook they had over faced.
Unless the restrictions on Japanese foreign trede to the
countries mentioned are lifted, Japan's industrial, economic
and financial structure will be very substantially weakened
through the inability to maintain those foreign commercial
relations upon which her prosperity depends. Besides the
disruption of the trade with the United States, Great Britain
and the Netherlands East Indies the action of the United
States and the withdrawal of most of the Japanese ships
from trade routes to South America brought Japanese trade
with Latin America practically to a standstill. Those four
areas had accounted for almost all of Japanese foreign trade
outsido of the Yen Bloc as because of the European hostilities
trade with that continent had almost ontirely ceased. Vhat
compensation Japen could obtain in developing her trade
with China, French Indochina and Thailand could only in
small measure make up for her trade losses with the other
regions. As evidence of the feelings of commercial and
financial interests in the matter, the stock market upon
the receipt of the news of the American and British orders
broke very sharply and would possibly have dropped much further
had it not been for the Governmont intervening to support the
market.
Reports of tho agricultural situation in Japan indicated
a "ather poor preliminary outlook for this vear's harvost
Juo primerily to excessive rains in Japan during the Spring and
thrcughout July. Crops roportedly were cxtonsively damaged
over wide areas and the damago undoubtedly would rosult in tho
curtailment

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.98)
-27-
curtailment of the production of rice, the Autumn silk crop,
vegetables and potatoes. The Agriculture Minister, in a
statoment made on July 2l, admitted that the rice crop vas
likely to be less than that of last year and of the year before
that. Those losses ho advised would be made up by increased
output of other foods and importation of rice from abroad.
One encouraging foature of tho situation was, however, the
prospect of large rice crops in Korea and Formosa, both of
which were expected to show appreciable increases over those
of the previous two years. Estimates for the barley, rye
and wheat crops for the present year showed a decline from
those of last year but sizeable increases over the average
production of the past five years. The Formosa sugar crop
for the year 1940-194l was reported to have decreased by 20%
from the previous yoar with the result that in order to make
up the necossary supplies of sugar for the country contracts
wore concluded for the importation of a large amount of this
commodity from Java during July, August and September.
On July 12 it was announced that plans had been formu-
lated for a new fin mil and monetary policy. This policy
was based on the principle of the mobilization and distribu-
tion of national funds in a systematic manner. It aimed at
providing funds for national production of essential materials
by ffecting a fundamental reorganization of finance and
De ing, and by projecting some new measures and consolida-
ti." tnese with all previously-adopted measures into one
clo~ly knit unit under strict Government control. The o.tire
rle: vns meinly set forth in ar abstract form and as an
srai rt cf tne new economic structure plan adoptel i
 mte i Lo4e. The monetary policy was to be reforme
by trcadening the powers of the Bank of Japan and making
that institution the nucleus of all monetary operaticns. The
bank

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.99)
-28-
Bank would exert almost complete control over business ald
banking transactions of all kinds thus facilitating state
mobilization of funds. On July 7 there came into effect the
new regulations regarding Japan's foreign trade which placed
a large number of commodities to foreign countries outside
of the Yen Bloc under a license system. Such goods as
cotton yarn, cotton goods, rayon and other important articles
were included in these categories. The purpose of the
regulations was to equalize exports with imports from non-Yen
Bloc countries. Pending tho preparations of the schedules
exports of all commodities wero suspended with the result
that little export trade was conducted by Japan during the
month under review.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Military Occupation of Southern French
Indochina.
On the morning of July 25 the new Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, received for the first time
the diplomatic chiefs of mission. In his conversation with
the Ambassador, Admiral Toyoda stated that he wculd like to
see Mr. Grew that evening to have a longer talk with him.
When the Ambassador called at 9:30 that night Admiral Toyoda
informed the Ambassador that the next day the Japanese Govern-
ment would issue an official announcement of an agreement en-
tered into on July 21 between the Japanese Government and the
French Government at Vichy concerning the "joint protection"
of French Indochina but that in view of the concern of the
United States he wished to explain to the Ambassador in
advance the reasons and intentions of such movement. The
dooument which he then handed to the Ambassador stated that
the agreement had been reached for the common interest of
both Japan and France in order to insure the safety of
Frenoh Indochina and to prevent the disturbance of peace in
that area under the existing conflicting international situa-
tion". It further stated that the Japanese Government will
strictly observe all agreements that exist between Japan
and France and especially those relating to the integrity of
territory and the respect for sovereignty and to refrain from
interfering in the domestic affairs of French Indochina, and
also that Japan has no intention at all of making the southern
part of French Indochina a base of armed advancement against
aujoining areas. It then stated that the Japanese Govern-
nent had been receiving numerous reports that the United
staces mizht aiopt more stringent anti-Japanese economi
measures as reinliatory steps against the Japanese action
ooncerning

=== Page 524 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
conoerning French Indochina and it advised that if such
measures should be realized "it would be difficult to pruvent
agitation of antagonism among the Japanese people against
aid to the Chiang Kai-shek Regime and the encirclamont cam-
paign against Jepan." It further explained that the acticn
taken by Japan was "unavoidable from a broad view point of
maintaining peace in the Pacific by preventing a more serious
aggravation of the feelings of the Japanese people, which
are s teaadily being excited by such facts as the unsatis-
factory result of the Netherlands East Indies-Japanese nogo-
tiations and the almost daily reports from abroad about the
tightening of the encirclement campeign against Japan." It
concluded with the expression of hope that the door would
be kept open for further efforts directed towards amicable
solution of American-Japanese relations.
The Ambassador after reading the document stated that
he could best reply to certain points contained therein by
conveying to the Minister the s tatement issued to the press
by the Acting Secretary of State on July 24 and he read
portions of that statement which he had picked up on the
radio that day. In further emphasizing and elaborating certain
/made
pertinent points/by Mr. Welles the Anbassador spoke of the
utter fallacy of the charge of "encirclement" and of the con-
ception that Japan was acting for the "safety and protection
of Indochina." Mr. Grew said that he was glad to take note
of the phrase that "Japan had no intention at all of meking
the southern part of Indochina a base of armed advancement
egainst adjoining aroas" but that the American Government,
tnd he nimself, had met with so many bitter disappointsments
durire taa Tast several years in the illusory natura of
promiscs

=== Page 525 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
promises and assurances conveyed to him by former Japanese
Ministers that the United States was now unavoidably obliged
to rely on facts and actions rather than upon words. Mr. Grew
spoke in detail of the successive steps of the Japanese south-
ward advance culminating for the present in the proposed
occupation of bases in Southern French Indochina and the
consequent endangering of the safety of other areas in the
Pacific,including the Philippine Islands, and he elaborated
the practicable and logical reasons for the American concern
in the safety of Singapore and tho Netherlands East Indies
which were definitely threatened by this advance. The
Minister did not try to refute the Ambassador's argument
except to repeat the peaceful intentions of the proposed
Japanese move into Indochina and to take issue with the use
of the term "occupation" in connection therewith since Japan
had no territorial ambitions. The Ambassador replied that
the term "occupation" connoted no question of time.*

the same time the press reported the text of the executive
order issued by the President on July 25 freezing Japanese
assets in the United States and thereby bringing all
financial, import and export trade transactions with Japa-
nese interests under Government control. The Japanese press
also carried the text of the President's talk to the Volunteer

which the President spoke of the United States' past Pacific

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
Conourrent with and forming a part of the general agi-
tation over the so-called "ABCD encirclement" of Japan (See
section under General International Relations) the Japanese
press during the month under review engaged in a concerted
campaign against real or presumed British activities in the
Orient as being designod to bring pressure to bear on Japan
and to frustrate Japan's aims in south-eastern Asia. On
July 13, the press carried a report to the effect that Great
Britain and the Chinese National Government were believed to
be negotiating a militery allience which was eimed at Jepa-
nese southward advance and which would beccme operative when
the Jepanese drive started. Leter in the month it wes reported
that this military allience provided for: the despatch of
munitions to Chungking from Burma, the establishment of a
cargo transportation base at Singapore; aircraft assembly

southward to fight under British command.
At the same time Britain was accused of endeavoring
to interfere in the affairs of French Indochina by encoura-
ging the French in the southern part of the colony to oppose
Japan, and, through economic pressure, of endeavoring to
gain control of the resources of French Indochina. On July
22, a report was published that an understanding had been
reached between the British Government, tho Chungking Govern-
ment and the Do Gaulle Regime under which Chinese forces
were preparing to invade French Indochina. Britain was said
to be preparing to transport native troops to southern Indo-
china.
Through both economic pressure and military threats
Britain was charged with intimidating Thailand in an effort
to alienate that country from the New Order in East Asia.
The press was particularly vociferous in denouncing alleged
British actions.. in this last respect. It was stated that
since the relaxation of the Near East situation additional
troops had been brought to Burma and Malaya and more airplanes
based in Burma, Malaya end B orneo for the purpose of threaten-
ing Thailand. Through an oil agreement and other economic
arrangements with Thailand, Great Britain was said to be
trying to force that country economically to rely solely
upon it and to interfere with Japan's legitimate trade with
Thailand. Diplomatically, Great Britain was also accused of
using every opportunity to create a rift between Thailaná and
Japn.

=== Page 533 === (confidence 0.99)

As evidence of the military strength that Britain as
building up in its south-eastern Asiatic colonies it was
reported that military forces of 300,000 men and a thousand
fighting planes had been assembled in Malaya, Burma and at
Singapore. A Domei despatch later reported that these
figures represented mere British boasting and that British
military forces in Malaya and Burma would only reach some
60,000 troops of mixed nationality and limited fighting abi-
lity and that Britain only had a few effective military planes
available in those areas.
From the angle of its possible effect on the situation
in the Far East the Japanese press also viewed with some
concern the conclusion of the British-Soviet military agree-
ment announced on July 13. The fear was expressed that Great
Britain might also attempt to draw Russia into the movement
for encirclement of Japan.*
In an approach to the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs
made in the first part of the month the British Ambassador
inquired as to the accuracy of the current report that Japan
contemplated the acquisition of naval and air bases in south
Indochina and Thailand. At the time he emphasized the serious-
ness of the situation which would arise in that case.
On the even g of July 25 the Foreign Minister presented
to the British Ambassador a document, similar in contents to
that presented to Mr. Grew on the same evening, giving advance
notice of the Japanese-French agreement for "joint defense" of
French Indochina. The following day the British Ambassador

(b) China.
On July 1 Germany, Italy, Hungary, Bulgaria, Spain,
Rumania, Slovakia and Croatia extended formal recognition to
the Wang Ching-wei Regime at Nanking.
On the occasion of the celebration of the fourth anni-
versary on July 7 of the beginning of the China Hostilities
the Japanese press dovotod much space to articles extolling
the successes of the Japanese military forces in China and the
progress made in reconstructing the occupied areas in China.
In a tabulation of the results of the military campaigns
during the four years Chinese losses were computed at 3,800,000
killed, wounded or taken prisoner, with 2,0l5,000 killed.
Japanese losses were announced as l09,250 killed. The articles
emphasized the present weakness of the Chungking Government
and the developing strength of the Nanking Regime. While
military operations progressed it was claimed that Japan had
beon steadily accumulating fighting and defense resources
behind the front and had groatly enlarged its productive
capacity with the result that Japan is much stronger and

(c) French Indoohina.
On July 5 ratifications were exchanged at Tokyo between

Japan and Thailand of the Treaty of Peace between France and
Thailand and the Protocols concerning the guarantee of the
political understanding between Japan and France and Japan
and Thailand that were signed on May 9. On the same date
ratifications were exchanged between Japan and France of the
commercial treaties between those two countries signed on
May 6.*
Several days before these treaties were finally ratified
a report was bruited in foreign circles in Japan that the
Government was about to make demands on French Indochina to
permit Japanese military ponetration into southern Indochina.
The report persisted and was givon increasing currency.**
A build-up for the pending aggressive move appeared in
the Japanese press in the form of a campaign designed to show
on the one hand that French Indochina was being encircled and
threatened by third powers, particularly Great Britain and
China, and, on the other, that the designs of third powers on
Indochina menaced Japan's netional security. There were re-

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
On July 28 the Netherlands East Indies followed the action
taken by the United States and Great Britain and froze Japa-
nese assets in the Archipelago. The Director of the Economic
Affairs Bureau of the Indies Government announced that day
the discontinuance of foreign exchange transactions between
Japan and the Netherlands East Indies and that all the Indies'
exports to Japan would be subject to special Government
license. The day before notice was given by the Indies Govern-
ment of the suspension of the Japanese-Netherlands East Indies
monetary agreement which had come into force on January 1.
The oil agreements between the Netherlands Oil Companies and
Japanese interests wore reportedly not cancelled but it was
understood that the freezing regulations made these: in effect
inoperative.
The Japanese press was much more bitter against the Nether-
lands East Indies for the action taken by it than again:t
Britain and the United States. Its action was considered
particularly grave on the grounds that Japan considered the
East Indies as an integral part of the Co-Prosperity Sphere
and that its measures amounted to a direct clash with Japa-
nese policy and should be regarded in a political rather
than economic light. Certain sections of the press went so
far as to state that the measures revealed regrettable hosti-
lity toward Japan and that the entry of Japanese troops into
French Indochina should serve as a warning to the Dutch to
reconsider their attitude for their own sake. Obviously
government

=== Page 540 === (confidence 0.99)
-18
government inspired articles, however, sought to tone down
the press reaction and to suggest that trade relations be-
tween Japan and the Notherlands East Indies might be maintained
through arrangements between the two Governments.*
(e) Soviet Russia.
Japan's position with respect to the Soviet Union
continued to remain obscure during the month. On July 15
the Foreign Office Spokesman was reported to have stated
to foreign correspondents that Japan would observe the con-
ditions of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact "for the
present."* It was in fact generally indicated that Japan's
policy towards Russia "for the prosent" was one of "wait and
see. The Foreign Minister in an interviow with the British
Ambassador at the beginning of the month stated that the
Tripartite Pact imposed no obligation on Japan to enter
the war on Germany's side but that similarly Japan would not
b e prevented by the Neutrality Pact from taking any action
arising from the German-Soviet war which the preservation
of the Japanese interests might demand". He stated that
thus Japan's liberty of action in respect to the situation
created by the war remained unaffected whether by the
Tripartite Pact or the Neutrality Pact (See Section Below -
General International Relations)**.
The Japanese press carried full accounts emanating from
both sides of the German-Soviet hostilities but ongaged in
little commont or prediction.***
