(g) General International Relations and Japanese
Foreign Policy.
Culminating the series of deliberations and conferences
in Japanose Governmental circles held for ten days from June
22, the day of the outbreak of the German-Soviet hostilities,
en Imperial Conference was held on July 2. Following the
confcreice the Goverrment issued the laconic statement thet
"the fundamental national policy to be taken toward the present
situation was decided". Another statement was then issued
by

=== Page 542 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
by the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka, in which
he reiterated the Government's statemont and said that "the
German-Soviet war can not bo regarded simply as an armed clash
between the two countries"; that it was "necessary for Japan
to watch with close attention the development of conditions
directly affecting this situation and to maintain the firmest
resolution and the most careful preparation" and that he
believed that "a super-emergency period is developing in the
Far East which will directly affect Japan". What the policy
was and what decisions were made were kept a well-guarded
secret.*
As indicated by the statement of the Foreign Minister
that "the German-Soviet war can not be regarded simply as an
armed clash between the two countries" Japan's own policy was
considered to be affected by the German attack on Russia and
the Imperial Conference therefore doubtlessly concerned in
part if not in whole the new situation created by the new
devolopment in Gormany's military activities and the formu-
lation of a policy and adoption of decisions in the light
of the new situation.
It will be recalled that one of the announced purposes
for Japan's joining the Axis Alliance was the maintenance of
peace and that this same consideration, probably together
with the hope of establishing close cooperation or even a bloc
between Germany, Italy, Russia and Japan, were motives for
the conclusion of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact. With
this previous foundation for Japan's alliance with the Axis
removed a new exemination of the relationship between the
basic interests between Hitler's New Order and the Japanese
New
* Embassy's telegram No. 920, July 2, 3 p.m.

=== Page 543 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
New Order was demanded. Before the Imperial Conference was
held the press pointed out the doubtfulness of Japan's present
treaty commitments, the fact that no full recognition of the
Co-Prosperity Sphere as one solid system dominated by Japan
had been recognized by Germany, the possibility that German
influence would now be extended beyond the Urals and into
Asiatic Russia, Outer Mongolia and possibly even North and
West China and the danger even that the European New Order
would embrace tho Asiatic colonies of certain European
countries. Besides this possible conflict of basic interests
of Japan and Germany there was at the same time the undoubted
realization, despite the fact that all hope may not have been
given up for an iwprovement of relations with the United States,
that Japan's present policy in the Pacific could hardly permit
of a reconciliation between Japan and America and the other
democracies, assuming that Japan's policy is "immutable".
On July 1 Prince Konoye, in a broadcast to the locel
councils of the Imperial Rule Assistanco Association, made
the statement to the effect that Japan must depend solely
upon her own strength. This declaration was given much
importance in the editorials commenting upon the Imperial
Conference. The editorials stressed the following further
points: Japan must follow a policy based on its own national
interests, the HoCHI stating in this respect that Japan could
no longar count on benefits from its Axis allies and had
no way to overcome its present crisis except through de-
pendence upon the Empire itself; Japan's principal policies
are the settlement of the China Incident and the construc-
tion of a Co-Prosperity Sphere, with respect to which point
the MIYAko expressed the opinion that certein powers threatened
Asiatio

Asiatic peace and blocked Japan's southward advance and
that their influences must be eradicated even through force
if necessary; and Japan's internal structure must be soli-
dified, in which respect the KOKUMIN stated that there never
has been a time when complete unity of the nation and un-
wavering determination were more urgently demanded.*
In the light of the Premier's statement and the con-
siderations affecting Japanese foreign relations mentioned
by the press it seomed reasonable to presume that the policy
decided at the Imperial Conforence had a good deal to do with
the determination of how Japan independently could further
her own best interosts. The Japanese military occupation of
Frenoh Indochina was seen to be one decision taken undor this
policy.**
Shortly after the beginning of the month mobilizatn
of troops and military preparations wore bogun in Japan on a
vast scale. Resorvists and conscripts estimated betwoen
one and two million men were called up throughout the country.
Military supplies of all kinds, including large numbers of
horses brought in from the country, were assembled. Sailings
of ships to the American continent and to other distant points
were cancelled and the ships placed on near sea runs. Restric-
tions were enforced on travel in various parts of Japan. Strict
censorship of mails and communication was carried out and
conditions approaching those applicable in a national emergency
were imposed in the entire Japanese Empire. By tho middle of

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese Military Occupation of Southern French
Indoohina.
On the morning of July 25 the new Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda, received for the first time
the diplomatic chiefs of mission.. In his conversation with
the Ambassador, Admiral Toyoda stated that he would like to
see Mr. Grew that evening to have a longer talk with him.
When the Ambassador called at 9:30 that night Admiral Toyoda
informed the Ambassador that the next day the Japanese Govern-
ment would issue an official announcement of an agreement en-
tered into on July 2l between the Japanese Government and the
Fronch Government at Vichy concerning tho "joint protection"
of French Indochina but that in view of the concern of the
United States he wished to explain to the Ambassador in
advance the reasons and intentions of such movement. The
document which he then handed to the Ambassador stated that
the agreement had been reached for the common interest of
both Japan and France in order to insure the safety of
French Indochina and to prevent the disturbance of peace in
that area under the existing conflicting international situa-
tion". It further stated that the Japanese Government wi!?
strictly observe all agreements that exist between Japan
and France and especially those relating to the integrity of
territory and the respect for sovereignty and to refrain from
interfering in the domestic affairs of French Indochina, and
also that Japan has no intention at all of making the southern
part of French Indochina a base of armed advancement against
adjoining areas. It then stated that the Japanese Govorn-
ment had been receiving numerous reports that the United
States might adopt more stringent anti-Japanese economic
measures as retaliatory steps against the Japanese action
ooncerning

-2-
concerning French Indochina and it adviued that if such
measures should be realized "it would be difficult to prevent
agitation of antagonism ainc ug the Jaranose pecrle against
aid to the Chiang Kai-shsk Regine and the ensli lsnont uem.
paign against Jepan." It further explained rat the acticu
taken by Japan was "unavoideole from a brced view point of

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
Conourrent with and forming a part of the general agi-
tation over the so-called "ABCD encirclement" of Japan (See
section under General International Relations) the Japanese
press during the month under review engaged in a concerted
campaign against real or presumed British activities in the
Orient as being designod to bring pressure to bear on Japan
and to frustrate Japan's aims in south-eastern Asia. On
July 13, the press carried a report to the effect that Great
Britain and the Chinese National Government were believed to
be negotiating a militery allience which was aimed at Jepa-
nese southward advance and which would become operative when
the Japanese drive started. Leter in the month it wes reported
that this military alliance provided for: the despatch of
munitions to Chungking from Burma, tho establishment of a
cargo transportation base at Singapore; airoraft assembly
a1ont

=== Page 564 === (confidence 0.98)
-10
plants in India, Burma and at Singapore; mobilization of
/and,
Chinese residents in the South Seas/ simultaneous to a
British declaration of war, the despatch of Chinese troops
southward to fight under British command.
At the same time Britain was accused of endeavoring
to interfere in the affairs of French Indochina by encoura-
ging the French in the southern part of the colony to oppose
Japan, and, through economic pressure, of endeavoring to
gain control of the resources of French Indochina. On July
22, a report was published that an understanding had been
reached between the British Government, tho Chungking Govern-
ment and the Do Gaulle Regime under which Chinese forces
were preparing to invade French Indochina. Britain was said
to be preparing to transport native troops to southern Indo-
china.
Through both economic pressure and military threats
Britain was charged with intimidating Thailand in an effort
to alienate that country from the New Order in East Asia.
The press was particularly vociferous in denouncing alleged
British actions.. in this last respect. It was stated that
since the relaxation of the Near East situation additional
troops had been brought to Burma and Malaya and more airplanes
based in Burma, Malaya and B orneo for the purpose of threaten-
ing Thailand. Through an oil agreement and other economic
arrangements with Thailand, Great Britain was said to be
trying to force that country economicaliy to rely solely
upon it and to interfere with Japan's legitimate trade with
Thailand. Diplomatically, Great Britain was also acoused of
using every opportunity to create a rift between Thailand and
Japen.

=== Page 565 === (confidence 0.99)

As evidence of the military strength that Britain was

Singapore. A Domei despatch later reported that these
figures represented mere British boasting and that British
military forces in Malaya and Burma would only reach some

From the angle of its possible effect on the situation

Britain might also attempt to draw Russia into the movement
for encirclement of Japan.*
In an approach to the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs

inquired as to the accuracy of the current report that Japan
contemplated the acquisition of naval and air bases in south
Indochina and Thailand. At the time he emphasized the serious-
ness of the situation which would arise in that case.

(b) China.
On July 1 Germany, Italy, Hungary, Bulgaria, Spain,
Rumania, Slovakia and Croatia extended formal recognition to
the Wang Ching-wei Regime at Nanking.
On the occasion of the celebration of the fourth anni-
versary on July 7 of the beginning of the China Hostilities
the Japanese press devoted much space to articles extolling
the successes of the Japanese military forces in China and the
progress made in reconstructing the occupied areas in China.
In a tabulation of the results of the military campaigns
during the four years Chinese losses were computed at 3,800,000
killed, wounded or taken prisoner, with 2,0l5,000 killed.
Japanese losses were announced as l09,250 killed. The articles
emphasized the present weakness of the Chungking Government
and the developing strength of the Nanking Regime. while
military operations progressed it was claimed that Japan had
beon steadily accumulating fighting and defense resources
behind the front and had groatly enlarged its productive
capacity with the result that Japan is much stronger and
more

increasing amounts of materials. Statistics were published
concerning the imports into Japan of certain specific commo-
dities in proof of this fact, as: imports this year into Japan
from North China of iron ore were three times as much and
of coal three to four times as much as they were before the

(c) French Indochina.
On July 5 ratifications were exchanged at Tokyo between

Several days before these treaties were finally ratified
a report was bruited in foreign circles in Japan that the
Government was about to make demands on French Indochina to
permit Japanese military penetration into southern Indochine.
The report persisted and was givon increasing currency.**
A build-up for the pending aggressive move appeared in
the Japanese press in the form of a campaign designed to show
on the one hand that French Indochina was being enciroled and
threatened by third powers, particularly Great Britain and
China, and, on the other, that the designs of third powers on
Indochina menaced Japan's netional security. There were re-

planned co-prosperity sphere in Greater East Asia.*
At noon on July 26 an official statement was issued

(a) Netherlands East Indies.
On July 28 the Netherlands East Indies followed the action
taken by the United States and Great Britain and froze Japa-
nese assets in the Arehipelago. The Director of the Economic
Affairs Bureau of the Indies Government announced that day
the discontinuance of foreign exchange transactions between
Japan and the Netherlands East Indies and that all the Indies'
exports to Japan would be subject to special Government
license. The day before notice was given by the Indies Govern-
ment of the suspension of the Japanese-Netherlands East Indies
monetary agreement which had come into force on January l.
The oil agreements between the Netherlands Oil Companies and
Japanese interests were reportedly not cancelled but it was
understood that the freezing regulations made these: in effect
inoperative.
The Japanese press was much more bitter against the Nether-
lands East Indies for the action taken by it than against
Britain and the United States. Its action was considered
particularly grave on the grounds that Japan considered the
East Indies as an integral part of the Co-Prosperity Sphere
and that its measures amounted to a direct clash with Japa-
nese policy and should be regarded in a political rather
than economic light. Certain seotions of the press went so
far as to state that the measures revealed regrettable hosti-
lity toward Japan and that the entry of Japanese troops into
French Indochina should serve as a warning to the Dutch to
reconsider their attitude for their own sake. Obviously
government

=== Page 572 === (confidence 0.98)
-18-
government inspired articles, however, sought to tone down
the press reaction and to suggest that trade relations be-
tween Japan and the Netherlands East Indies might be maintained
through arrangements between the two Governments.*
(e) Soviet Russia.
Japan's position with respect to the Soviet Union
continued to remain obscure during the month. On July 15
the Foreign Office Spokesman was reported to have stated
to foreign correspondents that Japan would observe the con-
ditions of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact "for the
present."* It was in fact generally indicated that Japan's
policy towards Russia "for the prosent" was ono of "wait and
see". The Foreign Minister in an interviow with the British
Ambassador at the beginning of the month stated that the
Tripartite Pact imposed no obligation on Japan to enter
the war on Germany's side but that similarly Japan would not
b e prevented by the Neutrality Pact from taking any action
arising from the German-Soviet war which the preservation
of the Japanese interests might demand". He stated that
thus Japan's liberty of action in respect to the situation
created by the war remained unaffected whether by the
Tripartite Pact or the Neutrality Pact (See Section Below -
General International Relations)**.
The Japanese press carried full accounts emanating from
both sides of thc German-Soviet hostilities but ongaged in
little commont or prediction.***

(g) General International Relations and Japanese
Foreign Policy.
Culminating the series of deliberations and conferences
in Japanese Governmental circlec held for ten days from June
22, the day of the outbreak of the German-Soviet hostilities,
En Imperial Conference was held on July 2. Following the
conference the Govyrnment issued the lasonic statement that
"the fundamental national policy to be taken toward the present
sitt:atior was decided". Another statement was then issued
by

=== Page 574 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
by the Minister for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Matsuoka, in which
he reiterated the Government's statement and said that "the
German-Soviet war can not bo regarded simply as an armed clash
between the two countries"; that it was "necessary for Japan
to watch with close attention the development of conditions
directly affecting this situation and to maintain the firmest
resolution and the most careful preparation" and that he
believed that "a super-emergency period is developing in the
Far East which will directly affect Japan". What the policy
was and what decisions were made were kept a well-guarded
secret.*
As indicated by the statement of the Foreign Minister
that "the German-Soviet war can not be regarded simply as an
armed clash between the two countries" Japan's own policy was
considered to be affected by the German attack on Russia and
the Imperial Conference therefore doubtlessly concerned in
part if not in whole the new situation created by the new
development in Gormany's military activities and the formu-
lation of a policy and adoption of decisions in the light
of the new situation.
It will be recalled that one of the announced purposes
for Japan's joining the Axis Alliance was the maintenance of
peace and that this same consideration, probably together
with the hope of establishing close cooperation or oven a bloo
between Germany, Italy, Russia and Japan, were motives for
the conclusion of the Japanese-Soviet Neutrality Pact. With
this previous foundation for Japan's alliance with the Axis
removed a new examination of the relationship between the
basic interests between Hitler's New Order and the Japanese
New

=== Page 575 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
New Order was demanded. Before the Imperial Conference was
held the press pointed out the doubtfulness of Japan's present
treaty commitments, the fact that no full recognition of the
Co-Prosperity Sphere as one solid system dominated by Japan
had been reoognized by Germany, the possibility that German
influence would now be extended beyond the Urals and into
Asiatic Russia, Outer Mongolia and possibly even North and
West Chine and the danger even that the European New Order
would embrace the Asiatic colonies of certain European
countries. Besides this possible conflict of basic interests
of Japan and Germany there was at the same time the undoubted
realization, despite the fact that all hope may not have been
given up for an improvement of relations with the United States,
that Japan's present policy in the Pacific could hardly permit
of a reconciliation between Japan and America and the other
democracies, assuming that Japan's policy is "immutable".
On July 1 Prince Konoye, in a broadcast to the local
councils of the Imperial Rule Assistanco Association, made
the statement to the effect that Japan must depend solely
upon her own strength. This declaration was given much
importance in the editorials commenting upon the Imperial
Conference. The editorials stressed the following further
points: Japan must follow a policy based on its own national
interests, the HoCHI stating in this respect that Japan could
no longar count on benefits from its Axis allies and had
no way to overcome its present crisis except through de-
pendence upon the Empire itself; Japan's principal policies
are the settlement of the China Incident and the construo-
tion of a Co-Prosperity Sphere, with respect to which point
the MIYAko expressed the opinion that certain powers threatened
Asiatio

=== Page 576 === (confidence 0.99)
-22-
Asiatic peace and blocked Jepan's southward advance and
that their influences must be eradicated even through force
if necessary; and Japan's internal structure must be scli-
dified, in which respect the KOKUMIN stated that there never
has been a time when complete unity of the nation and un-
wavering determination were more urgently demanded.*
In the light of the Premier's statoment and the con-
siderations affecting Japanese foreign relations mentioned
by the press it seomed reasonable to presume that the policy
decided at the Imperial Conforence had a good deal to do with
tho determination of how Japan independently could further
her own best interosts. The Japanese military occupation of
Frenoh Indochina was seen to be one decision taken under this
policy.**
Shortly after the beginning of the month mobilization
of troops and militery preparations wore bogun in Japan on a
vast scale. Resorvists and conscripts estimated betwoen
one and two million men were called up throughout the country.
Military supplies of all kinds, including large numbers of
horses brought in from the country, were assembled. Sailings
of ships to the American continent and to other distant points
were cancelled and the ships placed on near sea runs. Restric-
tions were enforced on travel in various parts of Japan. Strict
censorship of mails and communication was carried out and
conditions approaching those applicable in a national emergency
were imposed in the entire Japanese Empire. By the middle of
the

=== Page 577 === (confidence 0.99)
-23-
the month large Jipanese forces together with military supplies
and equipment were being sent to Manchuria, extensive nili-
tary preparations conducted there and military forces sent to
the north-eastern part of Manchuria facing the Maritime
Province. At the end of the month mobilization of men and
military supplies and thedespatch of troops and supplies to
Manchuria were still continuing.*
The agitation regarding the so-called ABCD encirclement
of Japan, first brought to the fore in the latter part of
June, emerged into a vigorous press campaign throughout the
month under review. A three-fold objective was seen in this
encirclement mo vement: to bring economic pressure to bear
upon Japan, to create bases and lines of military defense
around Japan and around areas under Japanese influence, in-
cluding French Indochina and Thailand, and to obstruct Japan's
execution of its immutable policy of the Southward Advance.**
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Cabinet.
On July 16 the Konoye Cabinet resigned. The reason
given for this action, as announced in a statement by tho
Board of Information, was that there was "need of perfecting
and

=== Page 578 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
and strengthening our internal structure speedily, first of
all to enable the vigorous execution of national policy in
view of an ever changing world situation".*
Only two days later Prince Fumimaro Konoye was summoned
by the Emperor to form a new Cabinet and on July 19 formal
investiture of the Cabinet wes held. The new Cabinet was
constituted as follows: Premier, Prince Fumimaro Konoye;
Foreign Minister, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda; Home Ministor,
Harumichi Tanabe; Finance Minister, Masatune Ogura; War
Minister, Lioutenant General Hidoki Tojo; N avy Ministor,
Admiral Koshiro Oikawa; Educetion Minister, Dr. Kunihiko
Hashida; Agriculturo and Forostry Minister, Hiroya Ino;
Commerce and Industry Ministor, Vico Admiral Masazo Sakonji;
Combinod Communications and Railways Minister, Shozo Murata;
Overseas Minister, Admiral Teijiro Toyoda; Welfare Minister,
Surgeon Lieutenant General Dr. Chikohiko Koizumi; and two
Ministers without portfolio, Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma and
Lieutenant General Hesuke Yanagawa. On July 25 Mr. Michiyo
Iwamura was appointed Justice Minister.
Five changes were made but with the exception of Mr.
Yosuke Matsuoka the new Cabinet, as regards the important
figures in it was concerned, was practically identicel to that
of the previous Ministry. The press in fact stated that the
Cabinet change was to be considered as a "reconstruction"
rather than a "formation" of a new Cabinet and it was openly
stated that the Cabinet change had been undertaken for the
purpose of replacing Mr. Matsuoka. The reasons for the elimi-
nation of Mr. Matsuoka from the Government were not publioly
exprossed b ut undoubtedly they rolated to the polioy for
1oh

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.99)
-25-
which he was largoly responsible with respect to Japan's
assooiation with Germany and Russia and the changes in the
intornational situation brought about by tho Gorman-Soviot
war which altored the basis for Japan's own foreign policy
and required the selecting of new porsonnel to carry out e
newly formulated policy.*
In the press comments regarding the composition of
the Third Konoye Cabinet specific mention was made of the
fact that there was a predominance of military servicemen
through whom it was expected that the cooperation of the
military services with the Government would be further
strengthened.
It was expected that there would be complete harmony
between the three central figures of the Cabinet, the Prime
Minister, Admiral Toyoda, Baron Hiranuma, the last of whom
was described as being actually the Vice Premier.** At the
same time the press stressed the fact as announced by the
Prime Minister and the new Foreign Minister that Japan's
basic policy would remain unchanged under the now Government.

VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.***
Tho full significanco of tho freezing of Japanese assets
in

=== Page 580 === (confidence 0.99)
-26-
in the United States; Great Britain and the Netherlands Last
Indies was not brought out by the end of July but there was
no reason to doubt that it came as a very heavy blow to
Japanese industry and presonted Japaneso business interests
with the most discouraging outlook they had ever faced.
Unless the restrictions on Japanese foreign trade to the
countries mentioned are lifted, Japan's industrial, economic
and financial structure will be very substantially weakened
through the inability to maintain those foreign commercial
relations upon which her prosperity depends. Besides the
disruption of the trade with the United States, Great Britain
and the Netherlands East Indies the action of the United
States and the withdrawal of most of the Japanese ships
from trade routes to
South America brought Japanese trade
with Latin America practically to a standstill. Those four
areas had accounted for almost all of Japanese foreign trade
outsido of the Yen Bloc as because of the European hostilities
trade with that continent had almost ontirely ceased. What
compensation Japen could obtain in developing her trade
with China, French Indochina and Thailand could only in
small measure make up for her trade losses with the other
regions. As evidence of the feelings of commercial and
financial interests in the matter, the stock market upon
the receipt of the news of the American and British orders
proke very sharply and would possibly have dropped much further
had it not been for the Government intervening to support the
narket.
Reports of tho agricultural situation in Japan indicated
a rather poor preliminary outlook for this vear's harvest
Juc primarily to excessive rains in Japan during the Spring and
thrcughout July. Crops reportedly were cxtensively damaged
over wide areas and the damago undoubtedly would rosult  tho
ourtailment

=== Page 581 === (confidence 0.98)
-27~
ourtailmemt of the production of rice, the Autumn silk orop,
vegetables and potatoes. The Agriculture Minister, in a
statoment made on July 2l, admitted that the rice crop was
likely to be less than that of last year and of the year before
that. These losses he advised would be made up by incroased
output of other foods and importation of rice from abroad.
one encoureging foature of the situation was, however, the
prospect of large rice crops in Korea and Formosa, both of
which were expected to show appreciable increases over those
of the previous two years. Estimates for the barley, rye
and wheat crops for the present year showed a decline from
those of last year but sizeable increases over the average
production of the past five years. The Formosa sugar crop
for the year 1940-1941 was reported to have decreased by 20%
from the previous year with the result that in order to make
up the necessary supplies of sugar for the country contracts
were concluded for the importation of a large amount of this
commodity from Java during July, August and September.
On July l2 it was announced that plans had been formu-
leted for a new finamil and monetary policy. This policy
was based on the principle of the mobilization and distribu-
tion of national funds in a systematic manner. It aimed at
providing funds for national production of essential materials
by offecting a fundamental reorganization of finance and
ianing, and by projecting some new measures and consolida-
ting these with all previously-adopted measures intc one
lo~gly knit unit under striot Government control. The entire
fla was mainly set forth in an abstract form and as an
i :tegrai : art of the new economic structure plan adortet in
Io nte cf 1940. The monetary policy was to be reformeo
by trcadening the powers of the Bank of Japan and mekink
that institution the nuoleus of all monetary operations. The
bank

=== Page 582 === (confidence 0.98)
-28-
Bank would exert almost complete control over business and
banking transaotions of all kinds thus facilitating state
mobilization of funds. On July 7 there came into effect the
new regulations regarding Japan's foreign trade which placed
a large number of commodities to foreign countries outside
of the Yen Bloc under a license system. Such goods as
cetton yarn, cotton goods, rayon and other important articles
were included in these categories. The purpose of the
regulations was to equalize exports with imports from non-Yen
Bloo countries. Pending the preparations of the sohedules
exports of all commodities were suspended with the result
that little export trade was conducted by Japan during the
month under review.

paign which reached a high point by the end of the month.
Tho burden of the attack was to the effect thet after the
United States hed denied aviation gasoline to Japan the
action in sending these supplies to the Soviet Union was
done to "mock" Jepan particularly as the tankers would have
to pass through what were "as good es Japanese territorial
waters" en route, going through the narrow straits between
the Kurile Islands and through the Strait of Soya between
Hokkaido and Saghalien in order to reach Vladivostok. Thus
under the "very nose of Japan" the United Stetes was making
this affront to Japan's prestige. The press furthermore
sought to see a deep laid plot of the United States in
seeking to gain Soviet adherence to the program of the
encirclement of Japan as a price for the material assistanoe
being given to it by the United States and Great Britain with
the possibility, moreover, that the Soviet Union was to
be brought to engege Japan's attention in the north so as
to impede Japan's advance southward. Aa a further practicable
consideration it was contended that there was a possibility
that the gasoline would not be sent to Western Russia but
would be divorted in largo part to the Soviet Far Eastern
Army and so menace the Japanese Army on the "Manchoukuo"
border. The spokesnan of the Foreign Ofrice made the
statoment on August 19 that the Japanese Government was
embarrassod by the dovelopment and thet Japan could not
"be indifferent to war supplies being sent to our neighbor".
He further stated that "in connection with this matter the
limits of Japan's territorial waters were being considered".

It was ennounced in the press during the month under
